Katalog Berita (Halaman 54/57)

#663. Hidden climate superpower: Why some ocean “snow” carries six times more carbon

Far below the ocean's surface, a constant snowfall drifts through the darkness. But unlike the frozen flakes that fall from the sky, this underwater snow consists of tiny clumps of waste, dead organisms, and other debris. Known as marine snow, these particles contain everything from mucus and animal feces to silt and the remains of microscopic plankton.

As this material sinks, it carries enormous quantities of carbon from the upper ocean toward the seafloor. Researchers estimate that marine snow removes more than 10 gigatons of carbon from surface waters each year. Yet only about two gigatons reach depths where that carbon can remain stored for hundreds or even thousands of years.

Why so much carbon fails to make it into the deep ocean has long puzzled scientists. Now, researchers from UC San Diego's Scripps Institution of Oceanography and the University of Lincoln have identified a surprisingly important factor: how sticky the marine snow is.

Their findings, published October 8 in the journal Science, show that the viscosity, or thickness and gooeyness, of marine snow influences how quickly bacteria break it apart and how far it can sink. According to the researchers, the stickiest particles can transport more than six times as much carbon to the deep ocean as particles with a thinner, less viscous consistency.

Marine snow plays an important role in Earth's carbon cycle, the natural movement of carbon among the atmosphere, oceans, land, and living organisms. By transporting carbon into deep water, sinking particles can keep some of it isolated from the atmosphere for centuries or longer.

However, not every particle survives the journey. Bacteria feed on marine snow as it descends, gradually breaking down the material and affecting how much carbon reaches the ocean's depths.

The new research provides the first detailed look at how living bacteria interact with the physical structure of marine snow and, more broadly, mucus itself. These interactions are important well beyond the ocean's carbon cycle. Similar relationships between bacteria and mucus help sustain coral reefs, kelp forests, plant root systems, and the digestive systems of humans and other animals.

Until recently, scientists lacked the technology to observe these interactions at the microscopic scale needed to understand them.

A Four-Year Quest to See Inside Marine Snow

The project began in 2021, when Bryce Inman, then a postdoctoral researcher at Scripps Oceanography, set out to explore what he calls "the muscoscape."

"Observing individual bacteria interacting with the physical structure of mucus had never been possible," said Inman.

Together with colleagues including Farooq Azam, a microbiologist and professor emeritus at Scripps, Inman spent four years trying to overcome that limitation. Multiple attempts were unsuccessful before the team finally developed a working technique in 2025.

Their solution combined 3D confocal microscopy with specially designed fluorescent molecules. Confocal microscopy uses light to capture detailed images at different depths within a sample, allowing researchers to reconstruct its structure in three dimensions. The fluorescent molecules helped make features of the material visible.

For the first time, the scientists could examine the "physical terrain" of individual marine snow particles while watching how living bacteria moved through and interacted with them.

The images revealed a surprisingly intricate microscopic environment, complete with structural differences that affect where bacteria can settle and how easily they can reach the material inside.

Much like snowflakes in the atmosphere, marine snow particles vary considerably. Each contains its own mixture of organic and inorganic materials, creating differences in composition, structure, and viscosity.

The researchers' new imaging approach, described in a companion study published in Nature Communications, revealed that bacteria preferentially colonize areas near a particle's surface where the material is less viscous.

The denser, stickier regions deeper inside are much harder for bacteria to penetrate.

"Bacteria can latch on to less viscous portions of each particle, but cannot reach more viscous regions until they break it down," said Inman. "Think of it like a worm in an apple: the flesh is easy to penetrate, but the core is tough to chew through"

This difference has important consequences for how long marine snow remains intact.

In laboratory experiments, the scientists observed that particles with greater viscosity resisted bacterial degradation and fragmentation for longer periods. Computer simulations further suggested that these stickier particles can descend to greater depths before breaking apart.

Based on these results, the team estimates that highly viscous marine snow can deliver more than six times as much carbon to the deep ocean as less viscous particles.

The findings help explain why some sinking material transports carbon much farther into the ocean than other particles, even though both begin their journeys near the surface.

"How far marine snow can sink is a surprisingly complicated problem, and we have added another - very gooey - piece to the puzzle," said Inman.

A Discovery With Implications Beyond the Ocean

Although the research focuses on marine snow, its implications extend to many environments where bacteria interact with mucus.

Mucus is often regarded as a relatively simple, slippery substance. But the new imaging technology reveals that it has a complex internal landscape, with microscopic structures that can influence how bacteria behave.

Understanding these physical features could help scientists investigate biological processes in settings ranging from human digestive health to the recovery of damaged coral reefs.

The method also opens opportunities to explore interactions that previously could not be observed directly, providing researchers with a new way to study the microscopic environments inhabited by bacteria.

Mucus, slime, whatever you want to call it, "has a surprising amount of structure," Inman said. "There are bulges, crevasses, all this sort of structure to it that we really had no idea about at a scale smaller than bacteria. This is the first time we're really examining this physical habitat."

The discovery adds an important missing piece to scientists' understanding of how carbon travels through the ocean. It also demonstrates that something as seemingly ordinary as the thickness of underwater slime can have far-reaching consequences for the planet's natural systems.

Additional funding for the research was provided by the Simons Foundation and the Engineering and Physical Sciences Research Council.

#662. Curiosity Blog, Sols 5050-5055: It’s Nazca, Our 49th Drill Hole

Mars Science Laboratory Mission Team Members

Written by Susanne P. Schwenzer, Professor of Planetary Mineralogy at The Open University, UK

Earth planning date: Friday, Oct. 23, 2026

As regular readers of this blog will know, we returned to the area where we found disc shaped inclusions in the rocks, which we interpreted as potential crystals. If they are indeed crystals, and we compare them to Earth, they could be very interesting indicators of the environmental conditions. Hence, the excitement in the team is high that we found a good block to drill. Good means first and foremost that it contains abundant potential crystals, but of course there is more before we drill. We passed the rover’s stability test, as all wheels have to be on solid ground before we extend the arm. Then we passed the preload test, which tests the stability of the rock and the forces the arm experiences when the drill bit presses into the rock.

With all those tests passed, we successfully drilled our 49th drill hole, called “Nazca.”

The drill campaign comes with many investigations before and after the drilling itself. Before the drill, we investigated the block with APXS, MAHLI and ChemCam – all on the target Nazca. After drilling, the sample was dropped off to CheMin, with the results still making their way from Mars to Earth. They are eagerly anticipated to reach us over the coming weekend. ChemCam obtained both passive spectral and active LIBS of the tailings and a LIBS measurement down the drill hole wall.

Other ChemCam activities this week included a LIBS investigation of “Palpala,” which is a dark pebble. We are on the lookout for those pebbles for a while and find that not all of them have the same composition. To make sure we understand their origin, we are measuring them when there are some in reach that are big enough. If a pebble is too small, it can move when it is being hit by the laser. Hence, we need to look out for some that are big enough.

Another such pebble was the target “Puna” identified in Friday’s planning. ChemCam LIBS on the target “Llama” is to get another depth profile to check whether our observations in the first such depth profile are consistently observed or whether they vary between locations.

The target “Pacaya” is another measurement of the disc shaped forms that drew us back to this region. In addition to the LIBS measurements, ChemCam has several long distance RMIs in the plan. They are all focused on the two buttes that have drawn a long-standing interest of the team for the various sedimentary structures and textures they reveal: Mishe Mokwa and Cordillera.

#658. Curiosity Blog, Sols 5056-5062: Charging Back Up!

Mars Science Laboratory Mission Team Members

By Remington Free, Operations Systems Engineer, NASA-JPL

Earth planning date: Friday, Oct. 30, 2026

Today’s plan covered 3 sols, 5060, 5061, and 5062, as we take a few recharging sols before getting back to our normal drilling activities. As you may know if you have been following the blog closely, we are in the middle of a drilling campaign on our target called “Nazca.” We’ve already drilled the hole, characterized the tailings from the drill hole with ChemCam, and have now done several sample dropoffs, both to our SAM and CheMin instruments within the belly of the rover. We still have a number of activities left to do as part of the campaign, including another dropoff to SAM for a different kind of analysis, and drilling cleanup activities. One of those activities is called “RAGE” (Rotate to Agitate for Granule Expulsion, a very fun acronym) where we essentially shake the drill to get any remaining sample out of it. We’ll also make sure to take lots of imaging to continue document the drill hole and tailings.

In any case, those activities are very power-hungry for our rover! So today’s plan purposefully did not have a lot of activities in it so we could get a nice few power positive sols before we get back to drilling. Each of our three sols had a standard morning science block, with an extra exciting brand-new activity on 5061 (more on that shortly!).

Over the three sols, we conducted remote science with ChemCam active and Remote Micro-Imager mosaics on some interesting targets (“Titicaca,” “Pampas,” and “Salta”), some Mastcam imaging of our calibration target and our drill target, several Navcam dust devil studies and Mastcam tau observations, and twilight imaging. Despite this plan being recharging/waiting, we got in lots of exciting science to continue to study the Nazca crystal formations and the nearby layered rocks and bedrock.

On 5061, we also conducted a brand-new engineering activity to test parallelism over one of our downlink communications windows. As the rover gets older and our power output slowly decreases over the length of the mission, we are continuously looking for new ways to execute activities with less power use. One of our strategies is parallelism – having two activities execute at the same time will save power due to the rover being awake for less time. However, we need to vet pairs of activities ahead of time to ensure they are safe to run together. This is a test of one of those pairs – running our CheMin “Get Data” activity at the same time as we are sending data to one of our orbiters. CheMin Get Data is a high CPU activity which transfers science data from the instrument to our flight computers. The test seems to have gone well, but we’ll have to wait to hear back from our engineering team to analyze the results!

Overall, it was an exciting plan full of great remote science as we wait to be able to wrap up our drill campaign. Stay tuned to hear more about how the rest of the Nazca campaign went!

#657. Pokémon card drops and restocks: Best deals from Amazon, Walmart, TCGplayer

Finding Pokémon card stock at market price hasn’t become any easier in 2026. Of course, you can help your odds by knowing where to look.

When shopping for the 30th Celebration collection, the new Delta Reign expansion, or the Ultra Premium Collection, finding actual stock can be tough. That said, by making the most of important go-tos like Amazon’s invitation system, Best Buy’s own store-only listings, and the occasional restocks elsewhere, you can add to your collection without paying inflated prices.

These are the stores we'd keep bookmarked to have the best chance of getting Pokémon TCG’s new releases. Keep in mind that some of the best options for products are through third-party sellers:

Amazon — One of the more convenient places to find sealed Pokémon products, especially Booster Bundles, ETBs, and Booster Display Boxes. In fact, Amazon recently brought back its $59.99 invitation-only 30th Celebration ETB offer. Select ‘Request invite’ on the Amazon-sold listing; an invitation isn't guaranteed, and the price will likely revert to much higher third-party prices once stock runs out.

TCGplayer — One of the first places to check for preorders on brand new releases, single cards, and listings close to market price. The dedicated trading card site’s "market price" estimations are helpful to gauge whether you’re overpaying for a Pokémon TCG product in the modern market.

Walmart — Regularly carries newly released collections and older expansions, with an extensive selection of products from marketplace sellers, usually at lower prices than Amazon. Not only can you find the in-demand Pokémon TCG 30th Celebration Ultra Premium Collection Night Edition for under market price, but Walmart+ members can sometimes receive early access to restocks.

Best Buy — Among the better options for avoiding reseller markup, thanks to its Pokémon listings that are actually priced at MSRP. For instance, its Prismatic Evolutions ETB is listed at $49.99, although this is another example where you need to request an invite and hope to be randomly selected. Meanwhile, other products sold at MSRP are available only in physical stores. Even if you’re not able to visit in-person Best Buy stores, it's worth registering for online stock drops at MSRP whenever that option becomes available.

Gamestop — Carries the latest expansions alongside older Booster Bundles, Elite Trainer Boxes, and single packs, with some surprising deals that can undercut other sellers. Its Delta Reign Booster Bundle is currently listed at $49.99, with Pro members eligible for a reduced $47.49 price. The retailer's store availability tools are particularly useful for finding nearby stock.

Target — Another useful destination for new collections, particularly if nearby stores have inventory available for pickup. As for online, anything at or near MSRP does unfortunately sell out instantly, and anything readily available comes with a massively jacked-up price. Target has also begun experimenting with invitation systems for certain Pokémon products, including the Destined Rivals Three-Booster Blister, priced at $14.99.

Pokémon Center — The go-to for finding market price and initial drops — plus getting exclusive Pokémon Center Elite Trainer Box editions with additional booster packs and promotional cards. Thanks to the massive imbalance between demand and supply, stock runs out within minutes.

The core issue that’s been plaguing the Pokémon TCG for years comes down to overall demand far outstripping supply; almost any product sells out at MSRP instantly. Between demand from collectors, competitive players, and most importantly, resellers (with many using bot accounts), any preorders or stock drops for new and popular expansions can disappear within minutes of being listed.

The Pokémon Company is printing more cards than ever to help combat the problem. It produced 10 billion cards worldwide during its latest financial year, but even that hasn't been enough to satisfy demand.

That said, the higher volumes during the 30th Celebration have at least affected scalpers' profit margins. More have been available, giving resellers less justification to drive prices above market levels. Moving forward, the company will continue to tackle the shortages while increasing production and promising additional reprints. At the same time, retailers like Amazon, Best Buy, Walmart, and Target have introduced invitation systems and randomized drawings to reduce the impact of scalpers.

With competition still fierce for the most desirable Pokémon Trading Card Game collections, a little preparation can make the difference between paying MSRP and settling for whatever a reseller is asking:

Sign up for invitations and drawings: Register for Amazon and Best Buy invitations whenever available, and check Target and Walmart for new product draws. These don't guarantee a purchase, but they remove some of the pressure of competing against other shoppers during a live restock.

Check local stores: Best Buy, GameStop, Target, and Walmart can all be worth visiting in person. Online stock may have disappeared, but that doesn't automatically mean every physical location has sold out.

Compare prices before buying: Use TCGplayer to establish the market price for whatever you're looking at, then compare it to the original MSRP.

Follow Pokémon restock trackers: Accounts like @PokemonDealsTCG and @pokemondealsuk on X (formerly Twitter) can help flag new drops, especially when the Pokémon Center opens its virtual queue. Plus, turning on notifications could save you from refreshing multiple store pages throughout the day.

Check who is actually selling the product: Amazon and Walmart regularly feature external sellers, so inspect the seller's details, shipping costs, and estimated delivery dates before committing to a purchase. Third-party sellers are fine to use if they’re selling at around market price and if their rating’s strong.

With Black Friday arriving on Nov. 27, there's a good chance that retailers like Amazon, Walmart, GameStop, Best Buy, and Target will have more Pokémon TCG stock available during their holiday sales.

However, there's currently no guarantee that high-demand products like the 30th Celebration Elite Trainer Box or Delta Reign collections will get additional inventory. If there is, these will very likely be very limited, invite-only drops — some even at MSRP if you’re lucky.

Instead, keep checking retailer listings throughout November, especially for older expansions like Perfect Order, Chaos Rising, and Pitch Black, which could see more competitive prices during the sales. It’s usually at these types of shopping events that third-party sellers undercut each other to clear old stock. Any discounts will sell out quickly, so be sure to keep Mashable’s Black Friday buyer’s guides bookmarked throughout the event.

Ben Williams is a freelance writer at Mashable, having joined the team in June 2025. With over 10 years experience in gaming, tech, TV, anime, and film, there’s nothing he hasn’t covered. Alongside Ben’s other work at IGN, Radio Times, Eurogamer, UNILAD Tech, and Rock Paper Shotgun, he also has bylines at sites like GamesRadar+, PCGamesN, ScreenSphere, Twinfinite, ScreenRant, GGRecon, and more.

When away from writing, he spends his downtime playing and watching games, films, and shows he loves covering — whether it’s replaying The Last of Us Part 2, catching Pokémon, rewatching Fullmetal Alchemist: Brotherhood, or bingeing Tarantino movies.

#656. Stronger flu shot linked to nearly 55% lower Alzheimer's risk

A higher dose of the annual flu vaccine may offer older adults an unexpected benefit: a significantly lower risk of developing Alzheimer's disease.

In a large study led by researchers at UTHealth Houston, adults ages 65 and older who received a high-dose influenza vaccine were less likely to develop Alzheimer's than those given the standard-dose version. The findings, published in the medical journal Neurology, suggest that the strength of a flu vaccine could matter for more than just protection against influenza.

The discovery grew out of an unexpected conversation between a UTHealth Houston physician and local public health officials. What began as a visit to discuss earlier vaccine research eventually led scientists to investigate whether different flu vaccine doses might be associated with different levels of Alzheimer's risk.

A Surprising Connection Between Flu Vaccines and Alzheimer's

Alzheimer's disease is the leading cause of dementia and an increasingly serious public health concern. In 2025, more than 7 million Americans ages 65 and older were living with the disease, representing approximately 1 in 9 people in that age group. That figure is projected to more than double by 2050.

Researchers have been exploring whether common vaccines might influence the likelihood of developing Alzheimer's. In a 2022 study, a team led by Paul Schulz, MD, found that flu vaccination was associated with a reduced risk of Alzheimer's among adults 65 and older.

Schulz is a professor of neurology at McGovern Medical School at UTHealth Houston and director of the Neurocognitive Disorders Center at UTHealth Houston Neurosciences.

Three years after that earlier investigation, Schulz and his colleagues published new research examining a more specific question: Could receiving a stronger flu vaccine be associated with an even greater reduction in Alzheimer's risk?

The idea came from public health officials who had learned about the team's previous findings and invited Schulz to discuss them.

"The public health department had seen our vaccine research and asked if I could come down to talk to them about it," said Schulz, who is the senior author of the study and the Rick McCord Professor in Neurology and Umphrey Family Professor in Neurodegenerative Diseases at McGovern Medical School. "We went through the findings, and they asked if there was a difference with different dosages; I was confused."

Why Older Adults Receive Stronger Flu Shots

As people grow older, their immune systems gradually become less effective at recognizing and fighting infections. This age-related decline can make influenza particularly dangerous for seniors.

To help address that vulnerability, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) recommends high-dose influenza vaccines as a preferred option for adults ages 65 and older. These vaccines contain four times as much influenza antigen as standard-dose shots. Antigens are the components of a vaccine that stimulate the immune system to recognize and defend against a virus.

The higher dose is designed to produce a stronger immune response in older adults, whose immune systems may respond less effectively to conventional vaccines.

Schulz had not previously realized that this stronger formulation was available.

"I was stunned that, as a physician, I didn't know a higher dose was offered," Schulz said.

Annual flu vaccination is recommended broadly, but preventing influenza is especially important for people with Alzheimer's disease. Flu infections and their complications can pose serious health risks for individuals already coping with cognitive impairment.

Before 2022, the CDC did not express a preference for one type of injectable influenza vaccine over another for people ages 65 and older. Instead, the guidance emphasized receiving a flu shot every year, regardless of the specific formulation.

Previous research from UTHealth Houston had already linked inactivated influenza vaccines, which use viruses that cannot cause influenza infection, to a 40% lower risk of developing Alzheimer's disease. However, researchers had not yet determined whether a higher vaccine dose might be associated with additional benefits.

Another practical issue made the question particularly relevant. As the population of older Americans continues to grow, demand for high-dose flu vaccines sometimes exceeds the available supply. Consequently, some seniors receive standard-dose vaccines instead.

This variation gave researchers an opportunity to compare Alzheimer's outcomes among older adults who had received different formulations.

"So we started looking, and sure enough, we were able to put together thousands and thousands of people over the age of 65 who got the high- or low-dose vaccine. They're more at risk for Alzheimer's than anybody else, of course, because it's age-related and we could look at high-dose versus low-dose and see if there was a difference," Schulz said.

Nearly 200,000 Adults Reveal a Significant Difference

To investigate the potential connection, the research team analyzed data from nearly 200,000 men and women ages 65 and older.

The results showed that people who received high-dose influenza vaccines had a significantly lower risk of developing Alzheimer's disease than those who received the standard-dose formulation.

The researchers reported that standard-dose influenza vaccination was associated with a 40% reduction in Alzheimer's risk, while high-dose vaccination was linked to a reduction of nearly 55%.

The association was particularly pronounced among women, who appeared to experience a greater reduction in Alzheimer's risk with the stronger vaccine.

These findings add to growing evidence of a possible relationship between influenza vaccination and brain health. They also raise important questions about whether the immune response triggered by vaccination might influence biological processes involved in Alzheimer's disease.

However, the findings demonstrate an association rather than establishing cause and effect. Additional research is needed to determine whether high-dose flu vaccination directly reduces the likelihood of developing Alzheimer's and to understand the mechanisms that might explain the connection.

What the Findings Could Mean for Alzheimer's Prevention

With Alzheimer's affecting millions of Americans and the number of cases expected to rise sharply in the coming decades, researchers are searching for practical ways to reduce risk.

The possibility that an existing vaccine could have benefits extending beyond influenza prevention is particularly intriguing. Unlike an entirely new treatment, flu vaccination is already widely used, and higher-dose formulations are specifically designed for older adults.

The new findings suggest that vaccine dosage could be an important consideration in future studies exploring the relationship between the immune system and Alzheimer's disease.

For now, the research provides another reason to investigate the potential benefits of influenza vaccination while emphasizing the need for further studies before drawing conclusions about Alzheimer's prevention.

Avram Samuel Bukhbinder, MD, a graduate of McGovern Medical School who is currently a clinical fellow in neurology at Massachusetts General Hospital and Harvard University, served as a corresponding author of the study.

Schulz is also a faculty member of The University of Texas MD Anderson Cancer Center UTHealth Houston Graduate School of Biomedical Sciences.

Other contributors from McGovern Medical School included Elizabeth He, visiting scholar in the Department of Neurology; Kristofer Harris, MPH, research coordinator in the Department of Neurology; Mya Rodriguez, graduate research assistant in the Department of Neurology; Kamal Phelps, MD, an alumnus of the Department of Neurology; Jenna Thomas; Gabriela Cruz, MD, resident in the Department of Neurology; and Lauren Jhin.

Researchers from the McWilliams School of Biomedical Informatics at UTHealth Houston also participated. They included Xiaoqian Jiang, PhD, associate vice president for medical AI, chair of the Department of Health Data Science and Artificial Intelligence, and Christopher Sarofim Family Professor in Biomedical Informatics and Bioengineering; Yejin Kim, PhD, assistant professor in the Department of Health Data Science and Artificial Intelligence; Luyao Chen, researcher; and Yaobin Ling, PhD, postdoctoral research fellow.

#655. Autumn Mornings Bring Valley Fog

Low clouds trace the branching valleys of the Appalachian Plateau and the Cumberland River, then burn off by afternoon.

On a cool November morning, low clouds traced the branching valleys of the Appalachian Plateau and the Cumberland River. By afternoon, they had faded, leaving clear skies over the autumn landscape.

The change is visible in this pair of images from November 8, 2026. The MODIS (Moderate Resolution Imaging Spectroradiometer) on NASA's Terra satellite acquired the left image that morning. In this natural-color view, low clouds appear white as they fill many of the valleys. Autumn vegetation appears in shades of brown and green.

The VIIRS (Visible Infrared Imaging Radiometer Suite) on NOAA-21 acquired the right image that afternoon, after the clouds had dissipated and exposed the rivers and lakes beneath them. This false-color view makes it easier to distinguish water (dark blue) from vegetation (bright green).

The clouds appear to be fog, a type of cloud that forms near the ground. Foggy mornings in the region are common this time of year. As nights lengthen, the ground has more time to radiate away the day's heat, and the air just above it cools to the dew point—the temperature at which air becomes saturated and water vapor begins condensing into the tiny droplets that make up fog.

Because cold air is denser than warm air, it flows downslope and pools in valleys, so fog tends to form there first. Notice the fog filling the branching valleys across part of the Appalachian Plateau in eastern Kentucky and southwestern West Virginia. Rivers and streams that run along many of the valley floors add to the effect by supplying extra moisture. The same pattern is visible along the Cumberland River and its reservoirs in north-central Tennessee and south-central Kentucky.

According to the National Weather Service, temperatures in many places on the morning of November 8 dipped into the 30s Fahrenheit before giving way to a mild, sunny afternoon. Forecasters reported locally dense valley fog that morning. Dense fog had also blanketed a wider part of the region earlier in the week, prompting advisories for hazardous travel conditions.

NASA Earth Observatory images by Lauren Dauphin, using MODIS data from NASA EOSDIS LANCE and GIBS/Worldview, and VIIRS data from NASA EOSDIS LANCE, GIBS/Worldview and the Joint Polar Satellite System (JPSS). Story by Kathryn Hansen.

#654. Want to live and work on the moon? NASA is hiring.

Ever looked at the moon and thought, man, I'd love to work there? Well, NASA is hiring.

Announced over the weekend, applications are now open for the next batch of astronauts who will train specifically to work and live on the lunar surface.

The U.S. space agency is looking for the next class to be trained at NASA's Johnson Space Center in Houston, those "who will join NASA as America returns to the Moon, builds the Moon Base, and lays the groundwork for the first crewed missions to Mars." Applications close 11:59 p.m. ET Dec. 3 and NASA will make its selections in late 2027.

Saturday's announcement came with a "NASA wants you" recruitment video, featuring NASA astronauts Jessica Meir, Andre Douglas, Deniz Burnham, Randy Bresnik, Kayla Barron, Jack Hathaway, and Chris Williams.

Notably, remote work isn't available for astronauts, and relocation fees to Houston may be covered by NASA. But the salary is out of this world: between $164,395 to $164,395 per year.

Of course, you can't just spacewalk into a job on the moon. To apply, you'll need to be a U.S. citizen with a master's degree in a STEM (science, technology, engineering, and mathematics) field from an accredited institution. If you don't have the latter, NASA will also accept currently enrolled or completed Test Pilot School students (who also have a STEM bachelor's degree), people with medical degrees with professional experience or completed residency training, or people who have completed two years of a STEM-based doctoral program. Candidates also need at least three post-degree years in their field.

Applying pilots also need to have considerable flight experience and pilot-in-command time whether in high-performance jet, rotary-wing, or other fix-wing aircrafts.

Along with a dazzling CV, applicants will need to upload five references (not letters of recommendation), all STEM-related academic transcripts, and if necessary, a completed pilot qualifications form. Candidates can then track their applications online.

It's been two years since NASA opened astronaut applications in 2024, from which 10 candidates made it through from over 8,000 applications. Nicknamed "The Platypi," the class of 2026 are currently training at Johnson Space Center. When their training's complete, they'll join the 32 active astronauts at NASA in missions and tasks like spacecraft development and maintenance (including those all-important spacewalks), research experiments, Mission Control ground support, and more. Who knows? A future Artemis mission could be on your CV.

"This is an extraordinary time to join NASA’s astronaut corps," said NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman in a press statement. "The people we select could walk on the Moon, live and work at the Moon Base, and help pioneer the systems and operations that will ultimately carry crews to Mars."

So, if you think you meet NASA's requirements (and I certainly don't), you can apply to be an astronaut via the U.S. government's job website.

#652. Scientists discover a surprising link between acorns and lyme disease

Tick-borne illnesses are becoming increasingly common across the United States, and Lyme disease remains the most frequently reported. Nearly 500,000 Americans are diagnosed each year. The infection is caused by the bacterium Borrelia burgdorferi, which typically reaches humans through the bite of an infected blacklegged tick. Antibiotics can treat Lyme disease, but without treatment, the infection may affect the joints, heart, and nervous system. Some patients also experience symptoms that persist over time.

Now, one of the longest-running studies of Lyme disease ecology is challenging several assumptions about what makes the disease more likely to spread. For more than 35 years, scientists at the Cary Institute of Ecosystem Studies in Millbrook, New York, have tracked the complicated relationships among ticks, mice, oak trees, predators, and weather. Their research has uncovered unexpected patterns that could help predict when people face greater exposure to infected ticks.

A new study published in Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences brings together nine of the project's most surprising discoveries. The findings show how decades of observations can reveal connections that shorter studies might miss.

"On balance, these surprises have enriched our knowledge of this system, helping us to inform management of tick-borne disease risk," said lead author Richard Ostfeld, a disease ecologist at the Cary Institute who leads the research program alongside Shannon LaDeau.

According to Ostfeld, the extensive data collected over the years have been essential for separating meaningful ecological patterns from coincidences and statistical fluctuations.

"Studies of this depth and length are incredibly rare," said LaDeau. "This research has followed an ecological community -- including oak trees, mammals, and microorganisms -- examining how interactions like predation, parasitism, and competition shape the system over time. The evolution of understanding summarized in this paper is something you simply cannot get without such a long-term and system-focused study."

Deer May Play a Smaller Role in Lyme Disease Risk Than Expected

When Lyme disease first gained recognition in the United States during the 1980s, white-tailed deer were considered a likely driver of rising tick populations. Hunters frequently encountered deer carrying numerous adult ticks, suggesting that areas with more deer might also produce more ticks.

The reasoning seemed straightforward. Although deer cannot pass the Lyme disease bacterium to ticks, they provide blood meals for adult female ticks. Scientists suspected that larger deer populations would allow more female ticks to reproduce, eventually increasing the number of young ticks capable of spreading the infection.

But decades of monitoring have produced a different picture. On the Cary Institute's 2,000-acre forested research property in Dutchess County, New York, researchers found no statistically significant connection between deer numbers and the abundance of nymphal ticks. Nymphs are immature ticks responsible for much of the Lyme disease transmission to humans.

"We had detected a weak positive relationship between deer and subsequent nymph abundance over short subsets of the data," said Ostfeld, "but the whole data set in its entirety shows no such thing."

Mice Are a Much Stronger Predictor of Tick Surges

While deer populations failed to predict the abundance of nymphal ticks, mouse populations showed a much clearer relationship.

Years with especially large mouse populations were followed by approximately 40% more nymphal ticks the next year. White-footed mice are particularly important because they provide excellent blood meals for tick larvae and are highly effective at transmitting Borrelia burgdorferi to those feeding on them.

Once infected as larvae, ticks can retain the bacteria as they develop into nymphs. When these nymphs take another blood meal, they can pass the infection to humans.

The findings suggest that tracking mouse populations could provide a valuable early warning of unusually high tick numbers.

How Acorns Can Predict Tick Populations Two Years Ahead

One of the most unexpected discoveries involves something that might seem entirely unrelated to Lyme disease: acorns.

Oak trees periodically produce exceptionally large crops of acorns in synchronized events known as acorn masting. These unusually productive years provide an abundance of food for forest rodents, allowing mouse populations to expand during the following year.

That increase in mice then supports larger numbers of young ticks. As a result, the researchers found that a particularly heavy acorn crop can reliably predict an increase in nymphal ticks two years later.

The relationship reveals a remarkable sequence of events in which the reproductive cycle of forest trees helps determine future tick abundance. It also offers researchers another potential tool for anticipating periods of greater Lyme disease exposure.

Why More Mice Don't Always Mean More Infected Ticks

Although mouse abundance helps predict how many nymphal ticks will appear, the researchers discovered that it does not consistently predict what percentage of those ticks will carry Lyme disease bacteria.

Initially, scientists expected both measures to rise together. Because mice are efficient transmitters of B. burgdorferi, an increase in mice seemed likely to produce not only more ticks but also a greater proportion of infected ticks.

Early observations appeared to support that idea. However, as additional years of data accumulated, the apparent relationship gradually disappeared.

"It kept me up at night," said Ostfeld, "because it meant that my understanding of how the system works was incomplete or wrong."

The explanation appears to involve the other animals that share the forest with mice. Skunks, squirrels, and opossums also serve as hosts for feeding ticks, but most are much less effective at passing the Lyme disease bacterium to them.

When these animals are abundant, more larval ticks may feed on hosts that are unlikely to infect them. This can reduce the percentage of nymphal ticks carrying the bacteria, even when mouse populations are high.

"So the infection prevalence in the nymphs really depends on how all the larval tick meals are distributed across all the hosts," explained Ostfeld. "It would have been convenient if one host predicted that for us, but that has not been the case."

This finding highlights an important distinction between the total number of ticks in an environment and the proportion carrying the pathogen. Predicting the risk of infection requires understanding the broader community of animals that ticks feed on, not simply counting mice.

Extreme Heat and Cold May Not Kill Ticks in the Wild

Weather has also produced some unexpected results.

Earlier laboratory experiments suggested that exposure to intense heat or severe cold could kill blacklegged ticks. Yet observations in natural habitats revealed that ticks are surprisingly capable of surviving these conditions.

Researchers believe ticks protect themselves from temperature extremes by retreating into deeper soil layers or hiding beneath fallen leaves, where conditions are less severe than at the surface.

However, the long-term data also revealed a more complicated relationship with temperature. Years that are warmer overall tend to be followed by fewer nymphal ticks than usual, despite the ability of individual ticks to withstand short periods of extreme heat.

At the same time, warmer conditions generally favor mouse populations, which can lead to more ticks. These opposing effects make it difficult to predict how changing temperatures will ultimately influence Lyme disease risk.

What 35 Years of Research Mean for Lyme Disease Prevention

Taken together, the discoveries show that Lyme disease risk depends on a network of ecological interactions that is complicated but not entirely unpredictable.

Mouse abundance, for example, provides a stronger indicator of future nymphal tick numbers than deer abundance at the study site. Similarly, unusually harsh winters may not reduce tick populations as dramatically as previously expected.

By understanding these patterns, public health officials may be better equipped to anticipate periods of increased tick exposure and develop more effective prevention strategies.

Still, important questions remain, particularly about how climate change will affect ticks, the animals they feed on, and the oak trees that influence rodent populations.

"You would expect that a warming climate, which makes life better for the mice, would eventually make life better for the ticks, but we have not seen that so far," said Ostfeld. "We need to know what to expect from the different manifestations of climate change, which are sometimes warmer and drier, and sometimes warmer and wetter. The time of year when it's very warm also matters to ticks, oaks, and acorn production."

The research site in Dutchess County has undergone significant environmental changes since monitoring began in 1991. Climate shifts, changes to the surrounding landscape, and the arrival of invasive species have all altered the conditions in which the forest's plants and animals live.

Researchers intend to continue following these changes to determine how ticks and their hosts respond over time. Their work could provide increasingly valuable insights into how future environmental changes will influence human exposure to Lyme disease and other infections carried by ticks.

Explanation: Sometimes Saturn disappears. It doesn't really go away, of course -- it just disappears from our view when Earth's Moon moves in front. Such a Saturnian eclipse, more formally called an occultation, was visible in early 2025 along a small swath of Earth -- from Greenland through central Europe and into northern Africa. The featured image is a digital fusion of three images, the first of just Saturn before it disappeared, and then two more of the Moon with different exposure lengths. The images were taken from Sardinia, Italy, after sunset. The bright dot near Saturn is not a moon of Saturn, but rather the star 85 Aquarii, which is further behind Saturn than Saturn is behind the Moon. #647. Mysterious gas leaks suggest Mars may still be volcanically alive

Mars may not be as geologically inactive as it appears. New evidence from mysterious gas emissions near an enormous Martian volcano suggests that heat and volcanic processes could still be present deep beneath the planet's surface.

Researchers have identified unusual concentrations of hydrogen chloride gas above fractures in the Alba Mons volcanic region. The findings suggest that gases from the planet's interior may be escaping through cracks in the surface, potentially revealing volcanic activity that has remained hidden until now.

An international team led by Alexander Trokhimovskiy believes the gas could originate from hot magma underground. If confirmed, this would indicate that Mars still has dormant volcanic systems capable of releasing gases to the surface.

The European Space Agency's ExoMars Trace Gas Orbiter (TGO) first detected hydrogen chloride in the Martian atmosphere in findings reported in 2021. On Earth, hydrogen chloride is frequently released through openings connected to magma, raising the possibility that similar processes could be occurring on Mars.

"Our findings suggest that Mars has retained enough internal heat to support volcanic activity until recently, and possibly still does," says Daniel Mège, Professor at the Space Research Centre of the Polish Academy of Sciences and co-lead author of the study published in Science Advances.

Mysterious Gas Emissions Defy Mars' Seasonal Pattern

The researchers noticed something unusual during early summer in the Martian northern hemisphere. Hydrogen chloride concentrations were unexpectedly elevated in a particular region, even though the gas normally becomes scarce at that time of year.

Hydrogen chloride levels on Mars generally follow a seasonal cycle. They typically peak during northern winter before declining to very low levels in summer. Scientists believe much of this behavior is associated with the planet's dust and water cycles.

However, the observations revealed a striking exception. Elevated hydrogen chloride repeatedly appeared in early summer over volcanic fractures on the slopes of Alba Mons. The unusual timing and consistent location suggested that something beyond ordinary atmospheric chemistry might be responsible.

Mars is home to the largest volcanoes in the Solar System, but scientists still do not know whether volcanic processes remain active beneath their surfaces.

Images taken from orbit indicate that some Martian volcanoes experienced eruptions within the past few million years. Instruments on the surface have also recorded marsquakes, demonstrating that the planet continues to experience seismic activity.

Together, these observations suggest that Mars may retain some of its geological energy. However, scientists have never directly observed a new volcanic eruption or fresh lava flow on the planet, leaving the question of present-day volcanism unresolved.

Fresh Cracks Reveal Clues to Hidden Volcanic Heat

To investigate the unusual gas concentrations, the research team combined atmospheric measurements with detailed observations of the Martian surface.

The Atmospheric Chemistry Suite aboard TGO uses infrared spectrometers to identify gases in the atmosphere. These instruments detected hydrogen chloride concentrated in a relatively small area shortly after the disappearance of winter frost.

Researchers then examined the same region using the orbiter's CaSSIS camera. The images revealed narrow fractures that appeared relatively fresh, with some extending hundreds of meters across the surface. Unusual changes in surface color were also visible around the cracks.

"The Trace Gas Orbiter was able to find the smoking gun. It was like looking for a needle in a haystack. It's truly amazing that it spotted a handful of molecules from a perhaps intermittent source, whilst moving continuously along its orbit to monitor the whole Martian atmosphere," explains Daniel.

One possible explanation is that gases originating from magma deep underground travel upward through these fractures. On Earth, comparable volcanic openings are known as fumaroles.

The researchers propose that extremely cold Martian winters, when temperatures can fall to -120°C, allow ice to seal the fractures and temporarily prevent gases from escaping. As temperatures rise in early summer, the ice sublimates, changing directly into vapor and potentially opening pathways for underground gases to reach the atmosphere.

The discoloration observed around the fractures could also be significant. It may result from minerals or other substances deposited by escaping gases, similar to the deposits commonly found around fumaroles (volcanic gas vents) on Earth.

Although the connection between the gas emissions and volcanic fractures appeared promising, the researchers needed to rule out other explanations.

"Looking at the spatial distribution of the seeping, it became instantly obvious to me that there was something volcanic behind it, but it had to be demonstrated," concedes Daniel.

The team carefully evaluated the chemical reactions that could produce hydrogen chloride in the Martian atmosphere, including processes unrelated to volcanism.

"A huge part of the work consisted of examining all the possible chemical processes going on in the Martian atmosphere. We needed to be rigorous, critical and creative to explain the presence of hydrogen chloride. The data were complex, but also excellent brain food," adds Daniel.

After examining the atmospheric and geological evidence together, the researchers proposed that the gas could be escaping from a dormant magma chamber beneath Alba Mons. Such a chamber would form part of an underground volcanic system that could continue releasing gases even without producing visible eruptions.

Hidden Volcanic Activity Could Help Mars Support Life

The implications may extend beyond understanding the planet's geological history. Volcanic gases could also provide clues about how Mars evolved and whether it ever supported environments suitable for life.

In the distant past, gases released from magma, including hydrogen chloride, may have helped create a denser atmosphere and warmer conditions. Those conditions could have allowed liquid water to remain across large portions of the Martian surface.

Volcanic activity might also create habitable environments underground. If rising magma or lava comes into contact with groundwater, the interaction can produce warm hydrothermal systems. Such environments could provide both chemical energy and protection from the harsh conditions on the surface.

"Looking for Martian life in the seeping fissures of the Alba Mons region would make a lot of sense. If hydrothermal systems are active, they could contain the ingredients and energy needed for microorganisms to develop, and shield microorganisms from the hostile and changing environment above the surface," ponders Daniel.

Although the findings do not establish that Mars is currently erupting or that life exists beneath its surface, they offer an intriguing new reason to investigate the volcanic fractures around Alba Mons.

"These findings may prove to be TGO's greatest success yet," highlights Colin Wilson, ESA's project scientist for the ExoMars Trace Gas Orbiter.

#646. Consumers are spending on physical gifts even as travel tops wish lists, Visa survey finds

Consumers are hoping for more travel and experiences this holiday season, but physical gifts continue to win the lion's share of their wallets, according to a new report from Visa on Thursday.

The report found that more than half of survey respondents are putting travel and live events at the top of their wish lists. Still, only 15% of those respondents are prioritizing spending on experiences for others, while 27% of them plan to favor physical gifts.

That gap between what shoppers want and what they plan to buy is largely due to higher travel costs, lower consumer sentiment and an emphasis on value, according to Michael Brown, Visa's principal U.S. economist.

"There is some interesting trade-offs there that we're seeing on the part of consumers," Brown told CNBC. "If I'm traveling more, I'm probably going to be gifting those physical goods less, so this is really the broader context."

The survey was conducted among roughly 4,660 U.S. adults ages 18 and older, designed to be representative of the broader population of consumers.

Brown said budget pressure also factors into traveling for the holidays, as Visa expects more people to celebrate the holidays "a little bit closer to home" than in the past given rising airfares and general inflation. In September, consumer prices rose 3.7% annually, driven higher by surging energy costs.

Airlines have hiked the price of plane tickets this year as their fuel costs have risen by billions of dollars due to the war in the Middle East. And even if fuel prices drop, executives have said fares are likely to stay high, with fares up more than 22% in September compared with a year earlier, according to the consumer price index.

Those trends mean consumers will likely be more intentional about where they're spending, especially as inflation drives up prices across the board.

"Consumers are aspirational. They want those experiences, they want to travel, they want to be out in the world," Brown said. "However, the economic reality is with prices rising, with economic uncertainty, they need to balance the reality of today's economic environment with those aspirations."

The report also found that only households with an income above $200,000 spent more on trips departing between Nov. 1 and early next year compared with 2025 into 2026. On the other hand, many other income groups pulled back on their travel spending.

"If you think about that segment, a lot of them own equities, and the equity market runup over the last several months has created a lot of positivity among that specific affluent demographic, and I think that's one of the factors that continues to motivate even aggregate macro spend today," Brown said.

Meanwhile, less affluent shoppers are placing an emphasis on value. Visa's consumer survey from October showed that roughly one-third of consumers with an income below $100,000 are going to switch to lower-cost brands or stores when shopping for holiday gifts this season.

Though Visa is projecting a 4.5% year-over-year increase in holiday sales growth, much of that figure is due to inflation. Without those price increases, sales are expected to rise just 1.1% this year, down from the 1.3% increase during the 2025 holiday season.

Ultimately, Brown said the survey findings may be encouraging for the broader retail scene this holiday season. With less emphasis on travel and more projected spending on physical gifts, it could boost the sector at a time when spending is under pressure.

At the same time, he said that consumers may just be expecting to spend more this holiday season because they've become accustomed to the higher prices.

"But, of course, consumers are internalizing higher prices of things, and I think that's why they're reporting higher anticipated spend this year," Brown said. "I think these numbers just need to be looked at through the lens of the inflation and particularly consumers' perception of where inflation is going to go. I think that weighed heavily on consumer psychology this season."

#644. NASA Scientists Show How Gravitational Waves Can Reveal New Worlds

The LISA (Laser Interferometer Space Antenna) observatory, now in development, will detect gravitational waves, the tiny ripples in space-time caused by orbiting masses. Now two studies led by NASA astrophysicists show in new detail how LISA could reveal a new and unique population of exoplanets — worlds beyond our solar system — throughout our galaxy.

“The most common source for LISA is expected to be double white dwarf systems, which are binaries composed of white dwarfs in tight orbits,” said Casey McGrath, a postdoctoral researcher at the University of Maryland, Baltimore County and NASA’s Goddard Space Flight Center in Greenbelt, Maryland. “If an exoplanet also orbits within one of these systems, its presence becomes imprinted on the gravitational wave signal in a way we’ll be able to uncover.”

Two papers published Tuesday in The Astrophysical Journal detail the findings. The first, authored solely by McGrath, examines the detection method and determines the planet masses and orbital periods where it would work best. The second paper, by McGrath and coauthors, investigates how such planets could be confirmed using NASA’s James Webb Space Telescope.

The possibility of detecting exoplanets around LISA double white dwarf systems was first proposed by Japanese astronomer Naoki Seto in 2008.

Led by ESA (European Space Agency), the LISA mission is slated for launch in the mid-2030s. As a collaborative partner, NASA is contributing the telescopes, other critical hardware, and engineering and scientific support as part of its mission to better understand how the universe works.

The LISA detection method is similar to one called radial velocity that uses light and is already employed to find exoplanets around nearby stars. As a planet orbits, its gravity tugs on its host star, imparting a small reflex motion, or wobble, onto it. This wobble causes the star’s spectrum to oscillate, with spectral lines shifting from redder wavelengths as the star moves away from us to bluer ones as the star moves toward us. The shift is due to a phenomenon called the Doppler effect.

“Something similar occurs with gravitational waves,” said Scott Noble, a theoretical astrophysicist at NASA Goddard and a coauthor on the confirmation paper. “By themselves, double white dwarfs will emit gravitational waves at a nearly continuous frequency. Add a sufficiently massive planet to the system, and its presence causes the binary to experience reflex motion, adding a Doppler modulation to the gravitational signal.”

For LISA, this modulation will appear as sidebands, secondary signals at both higher and lower frequencies than the primary one.

McGrath likens the situation to modern radio communication, where some of the energy of a main radio carrier frequency goes into forming sidebands at adjacent upper and lower frequencies that carry messages (like voices or music). For gravitational waves, the sidebands carry messages about the presence of a planet.

White dwarfs are the Earth-sized remnants of stars like the Sun. While exoplanets have been detected around a few isolated white dwarfs, none have yet been found around a binary white dwarf, which means the LISA detections would represent a distinct new exoplanet population.

Astronomers estimate that our Milky Way galaxy contains 10 million double white dwarf systems with orbital periods of less than an hour, which generate gravitational waves in the right range for LISA to find. They expect LISA will resolve about 10,000 individual systems, as well as around 100 mixed binaries — with white dwarfs, neutron stars, or black holes — orbiting in similar periods.

“Once LISA captures its first exoplanet detections, it will be critical for us to validate some of them,” said Mary Anne Limbach, an assistant research scientist at the University of Michigan and a co-author on the confirmation paper. “These joint detections will allow us to establish that the gravitational signals correspond to genuine planets rather than false positives.”

The team described a new method for confirming these planets by taking advantage of the Webb telescope’s infrared sensitivity. A planet in a double white dwarf system produces a larger portion of infrared light than what’s expected from the stars themselves. Webb’s instruments can easily detect this infrared excess out to a distance of at least 500 light-years.

Using this technique for a test case system at this distance, the researchers find that Webb could detect an exoplanet about five times Jupiter’s mass, while LISA could detect a sub-Jupiter exoplanet under ideal circumstances.

The LISA observatory will consist of a trio of spacecraft following Earth as it orbits the Sun. They will form a vast triangular array stretching 1.6 million miles (2.5 million kilometers) on each side. Each spacecraft will include two telescopes that will use infrared laser beams to simultaneously transmit and receive signals between adjacent spacecraft. Through these telescopes, the spacecraft will measure miniscule changes — smaller than the width of a helium atom — in their relative distances, the signals of passing gravitational waves.

Gravitational waves were predicted by Albert Einstein’s 1916 general theory of relativity and first detected by ground-based observatories in 2015. The waves form whenever massive objects accelerate, such as two stars in orbit around each other. They travel through space-time, moving at the speed of light, and are unaffected by objects they encounter along the way. These properties make them a valuable tool for probing the cosmos.

Additional NASA contributions to the mission include the laser system, devices to manage the buildup of electric charge on the proof masses, data analysis for identifying and characterizing individual gravitational wave sources, and additional scientific and engineering expertise.

To learn more about the LISA mission, visit:

#642. Ancient Siberian tomb reveals surprising secrets of cannabis use 2,800 years ago

A remarkable discovery in a nearly 2,800-year-old Siberian tomb has provided the oldest directly dated archaeological evidence that people used cannabis for its intoxicating effects. By examining ancient cannabis seeds and chemical traces preserved in human hair, researchers have uncovered new clues about the role of the plant in prehistoric burial rituals.

Cannabis is among the most widely used psychoactive plants in the modern world, but tracing its use thousands of years into the past presents a major archaeological challenge. Flowers, leaves, and resins tend to decompose over time, leaving little physical evidence behind. Ancient communities also relied on cannabis for practical purposes, including producing fiber and oil, making it difficult to distinguish these uses from medicinal or recreational consumption.

An international team led by Dr. Gino Caspari, affiliated with the Institute of Archaeological Sciences at the University of Bern and the Max Planck Institute of Geoanthropology, investigated an elaborate tomb in the Republic of Tyva in southern Siberia. The burial site belonged to prominent members of the Scythians, a culture known for its skilled horseback riders who inhabited the vast Eurasian steppe.

Ancient Siberian Tomb Contains 1,000 Cannabis Seeds

During the investigation, researchers recovered approximately 1,000 cannabis seeds from the tomb. They used radiocarbon dating to determine the age of six individual seeds. Combined with other dating evidence, the results indicate that the seeds were deposited in the burial site during the early eighth century B.C.

"The seeds show that cannabis played an important role in the rituals surrounding the burial," says Caspari, an archaeologist and the study's lead author.

The researchers also analyzed human hair preserved in the tomb's central burial chamber. Chemical evidence in several samples pointed to cannabis consumption, providing a crucial connection between the plant remains and the people buried at the site.

"This study is particularly insightful because it combines the analysis of plant remains with molecular traces in the human body," says Caspari.

The findings were published in the journal Science.

Chemical Traces in Ancient Hair Reveal Cannabis Use

Although the cannabis seeds were remarkably well preserved, their presence alone could not establish whether the plant was used for its mind-altering effects. The seeds probably came from wild cannabis plants, and researchers could not determine whether people had deliberately cultivated them for their psychoactive properties.

The strongest evidence came from the preserved human hair. Using high-resolution mass spectrometry and other analytical techniques, the team identified clear chemical signals consistent with cannabinol (CBN) in several samples.

CBN can form when tetrahydrocannabinol (THC), the primary compound responsible for cannabis intoxication, undergoes oxidation. The researchers also detected weaker signals that matched the mass of THC, further supporting the possibility that the individuals had consumed cannabis.

While these chemical findings indicate cannabis use, they cannot reveal how much was consumed or exactly how the compounds entered the body. The individuals may have inhaled cannabis smoke or consumed the plant in another way, but the available evidence cannot establish a particular method.

Other archaeological discoveries from later periods in Central Asia provide clearer evidence of cannabis inhalation. At those sites, researchers have identified cannabinoid residues on heated stones and inside vessels, pointing to the use of heat to release compounds that could be inhaled.

"The molecular traces in the hair and the cannabis seeds come from the same grave and suggest that cannabis was used for its intoxicating properties," says Caspari, who conducts research at the Institute of Archaeological Sciences at the University of Bern and the Max Planck Institute for Geoanthropology in Jena (Germany).

Cannabis, Horse Sacrifices, and Ancient Burial Rituals

The cannabis evidence was discovered within an elaborate ceremonial burial site, suggesting that the plant may have been connected to important Scythian funerary traditions.

"In this princely tomb, the burials were accompanied by extensive horse sacrifices and, most likely, human sacrifices as well," says Gino Caspari.

Earlier research at the site identified the remains of 18 horses that had been deliberately positioned on the burial mound. Continued excavations have since uncovered evidence of several dozen more sacrificed horses, along with scattered human bones.

These discoveries reveal the extraordinary scale of the burial ceremonies and provide important context for understanding why cannabis may have been included among the materials deposited in the tomb.

Oldest Directly Dated Evidence of Cannabis Intoxication

Taken together, the seeds and chemical traces in human hair represent the oldest directly dated archaeological evidence currently known for cannabis use associated with intoxication.

However, several questions remain unanswered. The researchers cannot determine how much THC the ancient cannabis contained, whether the plants were particularly potent, or whether Scythian communities had begun deliberately selecting or cultivating cannabis varieties with higher concentrations of psychoactive compounds.

To investigate these questions, the team is now studying genetic material recovered from the ancient plant remains. The unusually good preservation of the cannabis DNA could provide valuable insights into how humans domesticated the plant and how it spread across different regions over thousands of years.

"We have already begun sequencing some of the plant samples," says Gino Caspari. "DNA preservation is excellent in this case. This will help us learn more about the history of the domestication and spread of this crop soon."

Materials provided by University of Bern. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#641. NASA to Welcome Expedition 74 Astronauts Home at Space Center Houston

Johnson Space Center Office of Communications

NASA will welcome home the Expedition 74 crew during a public event at Space Center Houston following their return from a long-duration mission aboard the International Space Station.

NASA astronauts Jessica Meir, Jack Hathaway, and Chris Williams; ESA (European Space Agency) astronaut Sophie Adenot; and Roscosmos cosmonauts Sergey Kud‑Sverchkov and Sergei Mikaev will share mission highlights at 6 p.m. CST Tuesday, Nov. 17, at NASA Johnson Space Center’s visitor center. An awards ceremony recognizing key mission contributors will follow the presentation.

The astronauts will be available for media interviews before the event. Media may request an in-person interview no later than 5 p.m. Monday, Nov. 16, by emailing the JSC Newsroom at jsc-newsroom@mail.nasa.gov.

Williams, Kud‑Sverchkov, and Mikaev launched aboard the Soyuz MS-28 spacecraft from the Baikonur Cosmodrome in Kazakhstan on Nov. 27, 2025. The trio returned to Earth on July 26, making a parachute-assisted landing southeast of Dzhezkazgan, Kazakhstan. The crew was in space for 241 days, orbiting the Earth 3,856 times and traveling more than 102 million miles. The mission was the first for Williams and Mikaev and the second for Kud-Sverchkov.

Meir, Hathaway, and Adenot launched aboard a Dragon spacecraft on Feb. 13 as part of NASA’s SpaceX Crew-12 mission from Space Launch Complex 40 at Cape Canaveral Space Force Station in Florida. After more than seven months aboard the space station, Meir, Hathaway, and Adenot safely splashed down on Oct. 8 in the Pacific Ocean off the coast of Los Angeles.

During their 237-day mission, the four crew members traveled more than 100 million miles and completed more than 3,792 orbits around Earth. The mission was the second for Meir and the first for Hathaway and Adenot. Roscosmos cosmonaut Andrey Fedyaev also flew as part of Crew-12 but is unavailable for the event.

The Expedition 74 crew dedicated more than 1,400 hours to scientific research and technology demonstrations aboard the International Space Station. Its work advanced in-space manufacturing by testing metal and tissue 3D-printing technologies and supported human health studies examining the effects of long-duration spaceflight. Research included blood flow studies, assessments of piloting capabilities under changing gravitational conditions, and activation of a new compact exercise system. The crew also evaluated life-support and environmental systems critical to future exploration missions under NASA’s Artemis program.

In addition, the crew members conducted pharmaceutical research to crystalize proteins for cancer medications, advance tumor targeting treatments, and grow stem cells for therapeutics. They cared for the first set of plants studied outside of curated systems, upgraded an instrument used to advance our understanding of matter and develop new quantum technologies, managed visiting spacecraft, and performed essential maintenance to support the continued operation of the orbiting laboratory.

Beyond research and operational achievements, Expedition 74 engaged students and audiences around the world through live educational events, outreach activities, and the sharing of stunning imagery that highlights the importance of scientific discovery and international collaboration in space.

For more than 26 years, people have lived and worked continuously aboard the International Space Station, advancing scientific knowledge and making research breakthroughs not possible on Earth. The space station helps NASA understand and overcome the challenges of human spaceflight, expand commercial opportunities in low Earth orbit, and build on the foundation for long-duration missions to the Moon, as part of the Artemis program, and to Mars.

#638. Expedia CEO says AI agents aren't changing booking habits — yet

Expedia CEO Ariane Gorin said Thursday the company hasn't yet seen artificial intelligence agents affecting the way customers book, but the early signs show potential for an industry shift.

"It's a great way for people to do inspiration, to do trip planning," Gorin said in an interview with CNBC. "We haven't seen it change the way they're actually doing booking."

The comments come as personal AI agents like Meta's Muse and OpenAI's Dots enter the mainstream, promising to tackle everything from scheduling to shopping for everyday Americans. Opportunities in travel planning and booking could pose a competitive threat to traditional sites like Expedia.

Gorin said Expedia is "working with all of the agents" to make sure it surfaces in AI results, to understand how the agents get information and to make sure that the company has the necessary integrations.

She acknowledged it's possible that AI agents could develop their own travel programs and eventually claim market share, but she said complexities within the industry — customer service, for example — would be difficult to fully replace with artificial intelligence.

"People were already shopping across a number of places, so the agent can do that in a much easier way," Gorin said. "But when it comes to actually booking — being confident that the content that you're getting is right, the amenities are right, the price is right, if something goes wrong, someone's going to actually either solve it for you, answer the phone, be there for you — I don't think those agents are going to do it for you, and that's why people trust Expedia and come to us."

Shares of Expedia were down about 12% Thursday after the company delivered weaker-than-expected guidance for the fourth quarter on Wednesday. The company forecast revenue of between $3.69 billion and $3.75 billion for the final three months of the year, making the midpoint lower than analyst estimates of $3.74 billion, according to FactSet.

Gorin attributed the "moderation" in Expedia's fourth-quarter guidance to "geopolitical events in the Middle East and Europe," the midterm elections and the company moving its fall sale from the fourth quarter to the third quarter. But she noted the company reported a strong third quarter, as total gross bookings rose 11% and revenue climbed 12%.

#637. NASA Invites Media, Public to Join Voyager 1 One-Light Day Activities

Launched nearly 50 years ago in 1977, NASA’s Voyager 1 on Wednesday, Nov. 18 will have traveled one light-day from Earth, which is more than 16 billion miles (25.9 billion kilometers). Voyager 1 is the most distant human-made object, and any light from Earth would take 24 hours to reach the spacecraft. NASA will highlight this historic milestone with two events.

On Tuesday, Nov. 17, NASA invites the world to participate in “Shine Bright for Voyager’s Light-Day” by creating a symbolic wave of golden light encircling the planet after sunset. NASA has created a website that enables smart phones and tablets to flash “VGR”, Voyager’s call sign, in morse code.

For more information about how to participate in “Shine Bright for Voyager Light-Day,” visit:

Everyone is encouraged to capture photos or videos of their light-day signal and share it on social platforms, tagging @NASA.

On Thursday, Nov. 19 at 12 p.m. EST (9 a.m. PST), NASA’s Jet Propulsion Laboratory (JPL) in Southern California will host a moderated discussion with mission members and NASA leadership about the status of Voyager 1 and 2, the unique science the twin probes continue to gather in interstellar space, and the challenges of keeping the spacecraft operating for so long over such vast distances. A Q&A with media and other audience members will follow. In addition, there also will be a preview of the upcoming NASA+ Voyager documentary, planned for release in 2027 for the mission’s 50th anniversary.

The event will take place in NASA JPL’s von Kármán Auditorium, where JPL hosted Voyager briefings during the spacecraft’s grand tour of the outer planets.

To be considered for onsite credentials, foreign national media must RSVP no later than Thursday, Nov. 12. Media interested in participating virtually must RSVP no later than two hours before the event. NASA’s media accreditation policy is available online.

For almost half a century, the agency’s Voyager mission has blazed a trail through space. Voyager 1 and Voyager 2 were designed to explore the outer planets by taking advantage of a planetary alignment that occurs once every 176 years. Voyager 1 entered interstellar space in 2012. Following a different trajectory, Voyager 2 entered in 2018. Both spacecraft continue to provide unique scientific observations of the environment beyond the heliosphere, the protective bubble created by the Sun.

Managed by Caltech in Pasadena, California, NASA JPL built and operates the Voyager spacecraft. NASA’s Deep Space Network, also managed by NASA JPL, enables communication with the distant probes. The Voyager mission is funded by NASA’s Heliophysics Division at NASA Headquarters in Washington.
