#636. A nuclear blast could save Earth from an asteroid, but there's a catch

A nuclear explosion could potentially protect Earth from an incoming asteroid by releasing powerful X-rays that heat its surface and push it onto a safer trajectory. However, new research suggests that the success of such a mission could depend heavily on which side of the asteroid receives the blast.

Unlike the smooth, round objects often used in computer simulations, real asteroids have uneven surfaces and irregular shapes. Those differences can dramatically influence how an asteroid moves when exposed to intense radiation.

A new study published in Icarus found that changing the direction of an X-ray burst could produce vastly different results, even when targeting the same asteroid. In one simulation, the asteroid's speed changed by 3.9 millimeters per second when irradiated from one direction, compared with only 0.8 millimeters per second from another. That represents a nearly fivefold difference in deflection velocity, the change in the asteroid's forward speed caused by the burst.

"We expected aim to matter," said Nathan Moore, a physicist at Sandia National Laboratories who led the research. "What surprised us was how much."

The effects on an asteroid's rotation could be even more dramatic. For another simulated asteroid, the change in how quickly it spun varied by a factor of 17 depending on the direction of the X-rays.

These findings suggest that understanding an asteroid's shape and carefully selecting where to direct a nuclear blast could be essential to protecting Earth from a future impact.

The research received funding through Sandia's Laboratory Directed Research and Development program.

A New Way to Simulate Asteroid Deflection

To investigate how different asteroid shapes respond to nuclear blasts, Moore and his colleagues developed what they describe as the most accurate asteroid deflection model yet.

Earlier computer models faced a major limitation. Researchers typically had to choose between representing an asteroid's complex shape and performing highly detailed calculations of the physical forces involved. Combining both approaches demanded more computing power than was practical.

The new system overcomes that obstacle, allowing scientists to study realistic asteroid shapes while maintaining the precision needed to calculate their response to an X-ray burst. The result is the most detailed simulation of asteroid deflection produced so far.

"Our new algorithm is over 1,600 times faster, which was critical to this breakthrough," Moore said. "With the old models, computers just couldn't handle it."

Moore's interest in improving these calculations grew out of an earlier experiment using Sandia's Z machine. That experiment provided the first demonstration that X-rays could deflect a small piece of asteroid material.

The next challenge was determining how the same physical process might affect an entire asteroid.

"I started wondering how to scale up the calculation to more accurately predict deflection of a full-scale asteroid."

A major problem was the widespread practice of treating asteroids as spheres. Although this simplifies the mathematics, it overlooks the irregular shapes of real asteroids, which can have ridges, depressions, and uneven surfaces.

"You can make estimates that way, but asteroids come in all kinds of odd, lumpy shapes," he said. "That's when I latched onto some work one of my colleagues was doing related to outgassing of objects in space. I think we had an aha moment when we realized we could do this. No more spheres. We would be able to model deflection for any asteroid."

The solution involved bringing together two sophisticated simulation systems already used at Sandia for very different types of physical problems.

"The ALEGRA code is normally used to model fast, high-energy experiments, like magnetically imploding wires on the Z machine, while SPARTA, a direct simulation Monte Carlo code, is usually used to model comparatively weak, slowly evolving forces, like the drag on a satellite from the solar wind," Moore explained.

"The key innovation here was figuring out how to combine the two simulation approaches."

Predicting What Happens After a Nuclear Blast

The new computer model allows researchers to reconstruct an asteroid using detailed 3D images captured during spacecraft flybys. They can then simulate a nuclear X-ray burst while adjusting the explosion's strength, its distance from the asteroid, and the direction from which the radiation arrives.

The software calculates how much material would vaporize across different parts of the asteroid's surface and displays the results as a heat map. Using that information, it estimates the resulting push, changes in the asteroid's rotation, and the extent of surface damage.

These calculations are important because a successful planetary defense mission would ideally change an asteroid's trajectory without breaking it into multiple dangerous fragments. Redirecting one large object could be preferable to creating several pieces that might still threaten Earth.

However, the simulations cannot yet predict every possible consequence of a nuclear blast.

"Right now, our computer model only considers breakup of rocks on the surface. We can't predict breakup of the entire asteroid body accurately yet," Moore said.

Developing that capability will require additional research, investment, and more advanced computational tools. An intense X-ray burst can trigger numerous physical effects, some of which influence one another. Accounting for all those interactions makes it exceptionally difficult to predict exactly how an asteroid would behave during a real deflection attempt.

Choosing the Right Blast Could Save Earth

Despite these remaining challenges, the findings offer valuable guidance for future planetary defense efforts. The research shows that the direction of a nuclear blast is not simply a minor detail. It can substantially change how effectively an asteroid is redirected.

"Our study shows how important it is to aim carefully," Moore said. "Pick the right side and you win. Guess wrong and it could be a bad day."

The new simulation tools could eventually help mission planners identify the most effective way to deflect a threatening asteroid. In principle, researchers could enter detailed information about its shape, then calculate the ideal explosive strength, distance, and direction needed to move it away from Earth.

Turning those calculations into a real mission would still be extremely difficult.

For one thing, the software requires reasonably accurate information about the asteroid's shape from every direction. If an asteroid were discovered on a collision course with Earth, there might not be enough time to gather the necessary observations.

Another challenge would be delivering and positioning a nuclear device at precisely the right location relative to an asteroid traveling through space. Even if scientists could identify the ideal blast configuration, executing it would require extraordinary precision.

Nevertheless, the improved simulations could eliminate much of the uncertainty surrounding one possible method of asteroid defense. By better understanding how nuclear X-rays interact with the complex surfaces of real asteroids, scientists are moving closer to predicting which strategies would work best.

For Moore, that progress is an encouraging step toward ensuring humanity has a better chance of preventing a catastrophic asteroid impact.

Materials provided by Sandia National Laboratories. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#633. An ancient ocean full of giant predators reveals a surprising twist in evolution

An extraordinary collection of fossils in central Colombia is revealing a surprising chapter in Earth's evolutionary history. Researchers have uncovered evidence of a time when ancient animal groups that were approaching extinction lived alongside newer groups that would eventually flourish. The discovery suggests that major evolutionary transitions can occur without the widespread destruction associated with mass extinctions.

Scientists from McGill University and the Centro de Investigaciones Paleontológicas (CIP) identified this unusual pattern while studying fossils from Colombia's Paja Formation. The ancient marine ecosystem preserves an exceptional mixture of surviving Jurassic lineages and emerging Cretaceous groups, offering a rare glimpse into a period of profound biological change.

Drawing on these findings, McGill biology professor Hans Larsson and PhD graduate Dirley Cortés have proposed a new concept called a "Biotic Inflection." The term describes an evolutionary turning point when older groups nearing extinction coexist with newer lineages that are beginning to expand and diversify.

A Perfect Storm for Ancient Ocean Biodiversity

The researchers believe an unusual combination of environmental conditions helped make this remarkable coexistence possible. Warm greenhouse temperatures, elevated sea levels, and coastal waters rich in nutrients may have supported an unusually diverse range of marine life.

Another important factor was the emergence of new ocean connections between the eastern Pacific and the early Atlantic through the region that is now Colombia. Together, these conditions likely created opportunities for different groups of animals to thrive within the same ecosystem. Many of these environmental influences continue to shape biodiversity around the world today.

"The Paja Formation records an extraordinary process in the history of life, when a 'perfect storm' of environmental factors we know to promote ecological complexity coincided with ancient lineages that were about to disappear. At the same time, new groups were beginning to flourish," Larsson said.

A Lost World of Giant Marine Reptiles and Predators

The evidence comes from the Early Cretaceous Paja Formation near Villa de Leyva, Colombia. This fossil-rich region has preserved an astonishing variety of prehistoric marine animals, providing scientists with an unusually detailed picture of an ancient ocean ecosystem. Most of the fossils collected from the formation are housed at the CIP.

"This geological formation has produced an exceptionally rich fossil record that includes giant marine reptiles like long-necked plesiosaurs and marine crocodile relatives, as well as turtles, sharks, crustaceans and others," Cortés explained. "Together, these animals formed the Paja Biota, one of the most ecologically complex marine communities found to date."

In a previous study, the researchers reconstructed the feeding relationships among animals in the Paja Biota. Their analysis revealed an exceptionally intricate food web, with an unusually large variety of apex predators occupying the highest levels of the ecosystem.

This remarkable complexity makes the Paja Formation especially valuable for investigating how ecosystems change as older evolutionary groups decline and new ones become established.

Could This Discovery Change How Scientists Understand Evolution?

The proposed Biotic Inflection offers a potentially important new way to understand major changes in Earth's biodiversity. Rather than being driven by a mass extinction, some evolutionary transitions may involve periods of extraordinary diversity, when declining lineages and emerging groups coexist.

However, the researchers emphasize that more evidence is needed to establish how common this pattern may have been. Additional fossil discoveries, more accurate estimates of fossil ages, and comparisons with geological formations in other parts of the world will help determine whether similar transitions occurred elsewhere in Earth's history.

"I think we've discovered a new model of global biodiversity change. Now we'll need to see just how widespread this could be in Earth's history and how we can quantify its impacts," Larsson said.

The research received funding from the Natural Sciences and Engineering Research Council of Canada, the Smithsonian Tropical Research Institute, the Anders Foundation, the 1923 Fund, Gregory D. and Jennifer Walston Johnson, the Fonds de recherche Nature et technologies Québec, and the McGill Biology Department Writing-Year Award 2024-2025.

#630. Wendy's rejects $1.4 billion takeover proposal from Nelson Peltz’s Trian

Wendy's on Thursday said it is rejecting a $1.4 billion takeover offer from a group of investors led by Nelson Peltz's Trian Fund Management.

The group offered $7.35 per share in cash on Oct. 28 to take the struggling burger chain private. In addition to Trian, it included investment firms BlueFive Capital and Sixth Street Partners, plus Flynn Group, one of Wendy's largest franchisees.

In February, Wendy's board formed a committee of independent directors to assess any potential bid from Trian in response to a regulatory filing from the firm. The committee found that the proposal "substantially undervalues" Wendy's and does not reflect potential gains from the chain's ongoing turnaround strategy, the company said in a press release Thursday.

"Our confidence rests firmly in [CEO] Bob Wright's proven track record of seasoned industry leadership, including restaurant business turnarounds," Art Winkleblack, Wendy's board chair and a member of the independent committee, said in a statement.

Trian declined to comment on Wendy's decision.

To stave off a hostile takeover, Wendy's has adopted a shareholder rights plan — or poison pill — with a one-year duration, as of Wednesday, the company said. Wendy's added that the investor group led by Trian rejected an offer from Wendy's for a standstill agreement that would have paused additional share purchases, adding to Wendy's decision to use a poison pill.

The rejection is the latest in the long-running saga between Peltz and the burger chain, which dates back to an activist campaign he led more than two decades ago. A Peltz firm has even owned Wendy's before. In 2008, his company Triarc, which also owned Arby's, bought Wendy's in a $2.3 billion deal. It later sold Arby's, turning Wendy's into a stand-alone company again.

In 2024, Wendy's named Peltz as chairman emeritus after he spent 17 years on the company's board. Trian executive Peter May and Peltz's son, Bradley, still sit on Wendy's board.

Wendy's also reported its third-quarter results on Thursday. Its same-store sales declined for the seventh straight quarter. However, its quarterly earnings and revenue topped Wall Street's expectations.

Shares of Wendy's fell less than 1% in morning trading. The takeover offer had lifted the stock, which has lost about 60% of its value over the last three years.

Wendy's is not the only restaurant chain that has found itself in the crosshairs of activist investors. Applebee's owner Dine Brands recently adopted a poison pill to block a takeover bid from investment firms Arkhouse Management and Cobblers Cove Capital.

#629. I Used NYT Cooking’s New AI-Powered Recipe Modifier, and It’s Actually Pretty Good

In an aside in the New York Times Cooking video I watched recently, where Melissa Clark made her lemony white bean soup with turkey and greens, the food reporter and recipe columnist invited viewers to leave a comment telling her what they do with the kale stems. It highlighted this sweet, funny relationship between a media brand’s personalities and its audience, a warm-casual vibe.

Below the article announcing the change on October 23, commenters loudly aired their grievances, threatening to cancel subscriptions, asking who wanted this, and how to turn it off.

After four days and 172 almost exclusively angry comments, commenting for the story was turned off.

My writing for WIRED over the past decade has shown me again and again that adding tech to a kitchen product is risky at best. I've repeatedly watched companies try and fail: Bluetooth connections for all the appliances, apps that lean too heavily on crummy recipes, toasters with optical sensors or touchscreens that still couldn't toast particularly well. Just about anything that was part of the "smart kitchen" tended not to be very smart at all.

Developing dependable, delicious recipes is hard, expensive work that can require a whole staff. Yet skimping can hurt the brand. Two notable companies with a deep bench—The New York Times Cooking and America's Test Kitchen—are cooking content makers. Recipes, which is to say delicious, high-quality, tested recipes, are what they do.

Since AI is often a lousy chef, giving it a role in the presentation of NYT's gold mine of content is a big risk.

Earlier this year, the Times allowed AI to help with a recipe-scaling feature (modifying ingredient quantities so "serves 4" turns into "serves 6") and converting from imperial measurements to metric—cups, tablespoons, and Fahrenheit to grams, milliliters, and Celsius. Now, in the iOS-only beta, a "customize this recipe" field appears just above the ingredient list.

"We put a lot of effort into trying to tell you when not to do something if it's no longer in the spirit of the recipe," says Camilla Velasquez, the general manager of NYT Cooking.

For its recommendations, the LLM looks at the recipe itself, the Times’ ingredient substitution guide, user comments, and then what it calls AI "drawing on our expertise," in that order. Velasquez says the AI should be a helper, not the main act, noting, "You shouldn't be chatting with it."

She explains that when the Times built the AI functionality, recipe editors helped create the original rule set, then evaluated how it worked at the end.

When I ask what this means for the jobs of 60 editors, writers, video journalists, and other employees—the team that creates the recipes people use and curates the vibe readers relate to—Velasquez stresses that NYT Cooking has been "hiring more than ever" and that it has "zero intention of publishing fewer recipes."

So far, she says people are mainly using it for ingredient and equipment substitutions, along with adjusting recipes for dietary needs. "Some people use the chatbot to scale, or they'll scale to match an ingredient," giving the example of someone who had only one cup of oil for a recipe that needed more.

As a food writer whose career has taken some substantial turns due to AI recently, I wouldn't say I've been clamoring for this, but I find the Times’ measured approach works pretty well.

I started testing it out by making chicken and dumplings using a five-star Alison Roman recipe with more than 6,000 ratings. I asked the AI tool to swap out bone-in, skin-on chicken thighs for boneless, skinless breasts. It thought about it for a moment—"simmering" in AI Cooking patois—and then offered up "Chicken Breast and Dumplings," with the "breast" in the title rendered in the red-orange color that indicates a modification. Just below the title, it said it was "Joseph's version made with AI. Not tested or endorsed by the original recipe's creators." Along with those visible changes, I could also toggle back and forth between my version and the original.

Once I started cooking, things went smoothly. I'm a recipe follower, not an improviser, and the Times’ recipes are easy to follow. The modifications were too. I was also impressed by how the app flagged that the chicken breast simmering time would be reduced because it cooks faster than thighs, and since they don't render as much fat as skin-on thighs do, it added some butter to the roux. These changes were incorporated into the right places in the recipe.

It all made sense except for one bit. It suggested the shredded, cooked breast go back into the pot for about another 30 minutes while the sauce thickens and the dumplings cook. I saw this coming and, well, I wasn't going to do that to my dinner. I put a handful of shredded meat in as directed so I could see what happened and reserved the rest. The shredded bits that were in for that extra half hour weren’t bad per se, but their texture was notably less succulent than what I'd reserved and way less than thighs would be.

This was the largest issue I encountered in all of my testing. If I had been the creator of the Times' original recipe, I'm not sure how I'd feel about the option to use a breast instead of thighs, which is not unheard of, but not very common in classic chicken and dumpling recipes. Perhaps it's something that could be fine-tuned or simply not offered as an option.

From there, it was fairly smooth sailing. I had it turn a Francis Lam roasted mushroom and broccoli grain bowl into a roasted mushroom, cauliflower, and squash grain bowl. The crucifer crisscross was an easy one-to-one, but I also told it to add a pound of delicata squash. It had me slice the squash into half moons so everything would finish at the same time, then upped the total amount of olive oil. Lam's poetic voice was preserved in both versions of the recipe, telling you to heat the olive oil "until it slips around the pan like water" before adding beaten eggs for festive dish-topping egg ribbons. My buddy Dave had come by for cribbage night, and this made for an enjoyable, healthy, pregame dinner before he went on to skunk me twice.

I'd been curious how it would handle batters, and it capably transformed Yewande Komolafe’s Toasted Sesame and Scallion Waffles into a yeasted version, which, unlike the original, gets an overnight in the fridge, letting the active dry yeast get bubbly and add an appealing tang.

A friend suggested Colu Henry's pasta and chickpea stew. I swapped canned diced tomatoes I had in my pantry for whole, and asked it to "add more vegetables." It gave options in line with the recipe—carrots, celery, onion, zucchini, mushrooms, and extra greens—then put what I chose into the right spots throughout the procedure so everything went in at the right time. It made for a healthy, hearty dinner.

Having been to the Portland Fermentation Festival recently and wanting guidance on incorporating a few extra ingredients, particularly garlic, into my sauerkraut, I used the AI to modify Lidey Heuck's original, saying "add carrots, garlic, and kale," which it suggested grating, thin slicing, and mincing, respectively, all in the right-feeling proportions. It also added more salt, since I was making more kraut. The big jar is sitting next to me as I type, and while it's several days from being ready, a sniff test tells me it's on its way to being a good batch.

Finally, I turned Sue Li's peanut noodles with spicy tofu into peanut udon with tofu, whitefish, and cucumber. I started by asking it to make the original less spicy, add veggies, and "keep the tofu," but add some good-looking rockfish I found at the grocery store. Impressively, since the fish was an add-on, it had me cook it, then set it aside and wipe out the pan (!) before cooking the tofu. Since I swapped udon for the original bucatini, it customized the noodle-cooking guidance, too.

It seems pretty natural that the Cooking section ran into some resistance when launching this feature. The NYT employs some of the best recipe creators in the business. Using AI to help us make substitutions is helpful, and the way the app integrates those changes into the recipes is impressive, but this tinkering with the original is essentially a subtractive process. How many of us are going to make substitutions and land on something better than Melissa Clark, Yewande Komolafe, or Francis Lam set out to make by design?

Instead, it's saving us a trip to the store, or helping us use something we have on hand, or making it gluten-free, serving as a good and rare example of how you can use technology to make a product incrementally better.

It also sounds like the commenters’ concerns have been heard. Velasquez let me know right away when we spoke that people will be able to hide the AI feature in the app if they want. The Times is also keeping the whole thing in iOS-only beta for now, and not saying when it would be available on other platforms.

That bond that you can see between the Cooking team and its users is a pretty sweet thing. Deep down, I'm guessing the upset Cooking commenters felt worried they were going to lose some of that human connection. I hope it never goes away.

#627. A hidden limit of space and time could unlock electronics 100,000 times faster

Quantum physics places fundamental limits on what scientists can know about the behavior of particles. Werner Heisenberg's famous uncertainty principle states that certain properties, such as a particle's position and momentum, cannot both be measured with unlimited precision. This restriction is not caused by flaws in scientific instruments. It is built into the laws of nature.

But position and time are different. Heisenberg's uncertainty principle does not impose the same restriction on these quantities. Now, researchers have experimentally demonstrated another fundamental limitation: an electron's location and its behavior over time cannot both be determined with arbitrary precision. Known as the "space-time limit," this newly observed relationship could have major implications for the future of quantum technology and ultrafast electronics.

The discovery comes from an international collaboration involving research groups at the Regensburg Center for Ultrafast Nanoscopy (RUN), led by Profs. Jascha Repp, Rupert Huber, Franz Giessibl, and Klaus Richter, along with a team at the Max Planck Institute in Hamburg led by Angel Rubio.

Watching Electrons Move at Incredible Speeds

Understanding how matter behaves at the smallest scales is essential for developing the next generation of technology. Advances in clean energy, quantum computing, and powerful electronics for artificial intelligence all depend on knowing how chemical reactions unfold, how light interacts with materials, and how electrons travel through electronic devices.

However, even the sharpest microscopic photographs cannot reveal the full picture. Scientists also need to observe how these processes change over time, effectively creating slow-motion movies of events occurring at the atomic scale.

At RUN, researchers are developing ultrafast microscopes capable of recording the movements of electrons, atoms, and molecules with extraordinary precision in both space and time. About ten years ago, scientists in Regensburg used ultrafast scanning tunneling microscopy to resolve the movement of a single molecule across space and time for the first time.

Tracking individual electrons presents an even greater challenge. At these microscopic distances, electrons can move roughly a thousand times faster than atoms and molecules, with important changes taking place over just attoseconds.

To appreciate how extreme these scales are, consider that an atom is approximately ten million times smaller than a millimeter. An attosecond is one-billionth of a billionth of a second. The difference between an attosecond and a second is comparable to the difference between one second and the entire age of the universe.

Making these measurements is especially difficult because electrons do not behave like ordinary objects. Their motion follows the unusual laws of quantum mechanics rather than the familiar rules of classical physics.

Ultrafast Lasers Reveal Quantum Tunneling

To observe electron behavior at these extraordinary speeds, the researchers built a new laser system that provides much finer time resolution than earlier experiments. The system allows them to control and study the quantum motion of individual electrons directly.

In the experiments, carefully timed laser pulses drive electrons from an extremely sharp metal tip toward a silver surface. The distance they travel is only a few atomic diameters.

The researchers detect this movement by measuring the resulting electrical current. To determine exactly when the electrons move, they use two pulses of light separated by a precisely controlled interval.

Simon Maier, the lead author of the paper, explains: "By varying the time interval between the two laser pulses, we can directly observe how the electrons respond."

This approach revealed clear evidence of electron motion occurring on attosecond timescales. It showed that the laser pulses could transfer electrons within these incredibly brief intervals while allowing scientists to observe the process as it happened.

What makes the result particularly remarkable is how the electrons travel. Instead of moving like conventional particles, they behave as quantum waves. This allows them to pass through an energy barrier separating the metal tip from the silver surface, even though classical physics predicts they should not have enough energy to cross it.

This phenomenon, known as quantum tunneling, is somewhat like an object passing through a solid wall without breaking or damaging it.

"Our measurement can be understood as a high-speed camera for the electron wave packets, since you can see at what point in time the tunneling process takes place," explains Katharina Glöckl, a doctoral student and co-author of the publication.

To understand the measurements more deeply, researchers working with Prof. Angel Rubio performed sophisticated quantum simulations of the electrons' behavior near the "space-time limit."

Their calculations closely reproduced the experimental findings and uncovered another remarkable detail. The electrons did not respond to the light field instantaneously. Instead, their motion lagged behind it by approximately 500 attoseconds.

At such extreme scales, several fundamental features of quantum physics become important simultaneously.

For example, the researchers found that the laser pulses could not be described entirely by treating light as a wave or entirely by treating it as individual particles called photons. Their effects displayed characteristics of both descriptions. This combination was essential for exploring electron behavior so close to the "space-time limit."

The team also discovered that controlling electron motion over extremely short intervals changes how the electrons are distributed in space. Quantum mechanics describes these electrons using wave packets, which represent how their wave properties extend across different locations.

The relationship between the timing of electron motion and the spatial extent of these wave packets revealed the central limitation behind the discovery.

Raffael Spachtholz, another co-author of the study, explains: "The more precisely we want to pin down the electron's position in time, the more energy we need to provide. And as a result, the electron wave packet spreads out more spatially."

In other words, improving the precision of an electron's timing comes with a cost. The more tightly researchers define when its motion occurs, the more broadly its quantum wave packet spreads through space.

To examine this relationship experimentally, the team placed a single atom on the surface to confine the electron wave packets to an extremely small region immediately before the laser pulses arrived.

This setup allowed the scientists to directly measure the connection between the electrons' spatial distribution and the timing of their motion.

Importantly, even when the electrons received substantial energy from the laser pulses, their wave packets remained sufficiently localized for microscopy with atomic spatial resolution and attosecond time resolution.

The findings provide experimental insight into a fundamental space-time constraint that scientists had previously only suspected. They also make it possible to systematically investigate how changes in electron motion over time affect the spatial structure of quantum wave functions.

Could Quantum Electronics Become Hundreds of Thousands of Times Faster?

Beyond revealing a new aspect of quantum behavior, the discovery could open the door to technologies that operate on timescales far beyond the capabilities of today's electronics.

One especially promising possibility involves using precisely controlled electron wave packets to influence chemical reactions.

Transferring a single electron to a molecule represents the smallest possible transfer of electric charge. But when that electron is concentrated within an exceptionally small region of space and time, the resulting local peak current density can reach as much as 1 trillion amperes per square centimeter.

Such extreme conditions could give scientists new ways to control chemical bonds and observe how molecules change during reactions.

"In the future, we want to use such wave packets to specifically trigger chemical reactions and observe, on the relevant length and time scales, how chemical bonds can be broken or altered," explains Prof. Jascha Repp enthusiastically.

The implications could extend well beyond chemistry. Greater control over electron motion may eventually help engineers develop electronic devices and quantum information systems that operate at speeds approaching the fundamental limits of electron dynamics.

"In the long term, the insights gained could also contribute to operating electronics and quantum information processing at the intrinsic speed limit of electron motion itself -- hundreds of thousands of times faster than the currently dominant CMOS technology," adds Prof. Rupert Huber.

That possibility remains a long term technological goal rather than an achievement demonstrated in the current experiments. Nevertheless, the findings provide a new foundation for exploring how electrons can be manipulated at the smallest distances and shortest timescales.

For Repp and Huber, the results suggest that the range of potential applications is extraordinarily broad. They believe that future advances in controlling electrons at the space-time limit may ultimately depend more on human creativity than on the constraints imposed by nature.

#625. Xfinity introduces WiFi-pausing Holiday Mode feature, with help from John C. Reilly

Xfinity is introducing a new "Holiday Mode" feature in its app, with a little help from actor John C. Reilly, who plays the Ghost of Very-Recent Christmas Past in a new holiday advertisement.

Holiday Mode lets customers pause WiFi for all devices on their home network with a single tap on their phone screen. The idea is to keep families connected and off their phones, if only for a few precious moments during the holidays.

The company said that Holiday Mode will be available starting from Nov. 9 for eligible Xfinity Internet customers through the Xfinity app.

The company also debuted a new Christmas advertisement to promote the feature, starring the aforementioned ghost. Reilly's character rescues people who miss precious holiday moments because they can't look away from their phones, an unfortunately relatable scenario.

90-second and 60-second versions of the holiday ad are debuting on Nov. 9 on YouTube and TV. #624. 8 Best Computer Speakers (2026) After Testing 30 Pairs

Good computer speakers can make for a quick desktop upgrade. Sound is often overlooked when people buy a PC. Some folks prefer headphones, others stick with those old, battered speakers they inherited, and some rely on the awful built-in speaker in their monitor. With any of the speakers in this list, you can enhance voices in video calls and movies, tease out greater depth in your favorite music, and provide spatial awareness in games. After several months of testing, I've narrowed my selection across various budgets and preferences. Whether you want a soundbar for your computer, a pair of speakers, or a surround sound system, you can find it here.

Be sure to check out our many other audio guides, like the Best Soundbars, Best Bluetooth Speakers, Best Wireless Headphones, Best Gaming Headsets, and Best Bookshelf Speakers.

Updated November 2026: I added speakers from Creative, a mention for speakers from Pioneer, and updated prices.

After testing more than 30 computer speakers and soundbars over the last few years, I always return to the Audioengine A2+. With an elegant design and quality construction, they are compact enough for my busy desktop. But don’t be fooled by the small stature—these speakers pack a serious punch. The clarity in the higher registers is excellent, but there’s also depth and accuracy in the mids. While the lack of a subwoofer means the bass can be a little shallow, it’s not distractingly so. Music is where the Audioengine A2+ shines, but I had no trouble hearing cloaked Elites with positional sound in Halo Infinite, and voices came through crisp and clear during in-game chat, video calls, and movies.

Connectivity is well rounded, but just watch out for jumps in volume with different input devices. The volume knob, which also turns the speakers on or off, is a little awkward behind the left speaker, and there’s no remote, but I have no other complaints about these PC speakers. You can also upgrade the A2+ by adding a subwoofer and 15-degree stands. Audioengine updated the A2+ in 2025, making the best even better by adding Bluetooth 5.3, 24-bit audio, and USB-C connectivity. There’s also a built-in USB DAC (digital-to-analog converter), so USB-C is the best way to connect your PC or laptop. Ultimately, these are the best computer speakers for most people because they strike the perfect balance of style, power, features, and price.

With a properly compact form, 6.5 x 4.3 x 4.2 inches (165 x 112 x 109 mm), these cute wee computer speakers can squeeze onto the busiest desktops. There’s an elegance to the molded plastic design with rubbery feet, and the Kanto UKI speakers come in a couple of fun colors (blue or green), alongside the classic black or white. More importantly these speakers produce rich, warm, and detailed sound, with surprisingly good bass. They served me well for music, games, and video calls.

I connected the speakers and plugged the right one into my PC via USB-C (no cable supplied), but there’s also a pair of RCA inputs, a subwoofer output, a 3.5 mm headphone port around front, and support for Bluetooth 5.0. Pairing and streaming from my phone was quick and easy. I appreciated the simple volume knob on the front of the right speaker that you press the switch modes. Desktop stands ($30) are available (sold separately) to angle these speakers at your ears, and they’re probably worth snagging, though there is also a rear screw mount hole.

Boasting a big sound for such a compact set of speakers, the iLoud Micro Monitors come recommended by WIRED's resident audiophiles. These speakers impressed me right from the simple setup (plug in the 3.5-mm audio output). The sound is well-balanced, with plenty of detail in the warm mids and sparkly highs. Even the bass response is good, considering there’s no subwoofer. I love the tiny fold-out feet, enabling you to angle them up for a better sound. There is also Bluetooth support (A2DP) to stream from your phone or other devices. The iLoud Micro Monitors do a great job with music, movies, and games.

But the design might not be for everyone; I don’t like the big iLoud logo on the side. You also have to reach around the back of the left speaker for the power switch, volume dial, and Bluetooth button, though I like the straightforwardness of these controls. There are also three switches to tweak EQ settings, but these speakers are basically plug-and-play. There is no USB port or built-in DAC.

Creative has a long history of affordable but decent-sounding speakers, and the XF1 proves it has not lost its touch. These are terrifically well-rounded PC speakers for the money. Relatively compact, they come with lovely cork speaker stands for a 15-degree angle. The sound is warm, with a decent thump of bass (there’s also an optional subwoofer output port), pleasing detail, and the ability to fill a room without distorting too much when you crank up the volume. Creative’s Nexus app (Windows, Android, macOS, iOS) gives you a 10-band parametric equalizer, so you can dial in the sound you want, or select one of the presets—and there are more than 100. These worked great for my day-to-day, meetings, videos, and some gaming. Playing music was the only time I felt a slight step down from my top pick.

Connectivity is also impressive with USB-C (which will be best for most folks), 3.5 mm line-in, and Bluetooth 6.0, as well as sub out. You hold down the power button on the right speaker to turn them on and off, and press it to cycle through the modes. I found powering down a little annoying, as it switches mode if you don’t hold down long enough. But that's a minor complaint. There’s also an energy-saving mode that powers down after 15 minutes of inactivity, but you can switch it off with a physical switch on the back. These speakers narrowly unseat the Edifier M60 (see below) in this category, largely because they are a bit cheaper for a very similar overall package. The XF1 justify the full $180 retail price, but I have already seen these drop as low as $130, which is a total bargain.

You don’t have to spend big to get a decent set of computer speakers, as Edifier proves with the G2000. Angled to fire sound up at you, these speakers have LED lighting on the back and a compact form that makes them easy to fit on a crowded desk. My test speakers were white, but you can also find them in black, pink, or red. Controls on the right speaker include a switch-like volume rocker and a mode button that cycles between music, movies, and games. Long-press the mode button to cycle LED lighting effects (there are 12). There is distortion on anything bassy, and the G2000 are a bit muddy on the high end. Because the sound imaging is far from precise, it can be tough to pinpoint sounds spatially. This is particularly noticeable with movies and games.

Connectivity options are solid, with USB, 3.5-mm aux, and Bluetooth 4.2. There is also a sub-out for adding a subwoofer, and they really need one if you want a deep rumble. Unfortunately, when you power up these speakers, they play a silly revving race car sound. I also dislike the volume rocker. (Why can't it just be a knob?) The Edifier G2000 can’t compete with our top picks here, but for the money, these compact speakers are impressive, and they offer a nice upgrade over our budget pick below. The RRP on these has crept up to $130, but they are often on sale, so I recommend you don't pay more than $100 for them.

These tiny, cute, affordable computer speakers from Creative have a lovely orb-shaped design with a gold-colored cutout contrasting the black or white exterior. Angled up to fire sound at your ears, and just 4.8 inches tall, these speakers fit on the most crowded desktops. The right speaker has a volume control and a gain switch. WIRED reviewer Nena Farell raved about these speakers and was impressed by their volume and sound quality, considering the price.

Connectivity options include USB-C, 3.5-mm aux, or Bluetooth 5.0. The box includes a USB-C to USB-A adapter, but you need a port that can provide enough power to get the most out of these speakers. Bass is inevitably lacking, and some distortion can kick in at higher volumes. Creative also offers the even cheaper V2 if you don’t need Bluetooth.

The latest compact soundbar from Creative is my favorite from the company so far. This affordable 2.1-channel soundbar and subwoofer system has been hooked up to my desktop computer via USB for the last few weeks, and I’m pleasantly surprised by its performance. The near field mode is ideal for desktop use, and I enjoyed using the Stage Pro for gaming and movies. Voices come through clearly, and there’s a deep rumble of bass, courtesy of the subwoofer. (The subwoofer is large, but tall and thin, so it easily fit beneath my desk). Music sounds good in general, though lacking the warmth, stereo separation, and clarity of our top picks.

Connectivity options include HDMI ARC with CEC, Bluetooth 5.3, optical-in, 3.5-mm aux, and USB audio, so you can also use this soundbar with your TV in a home theater setup or with your smartphone. There are controls on top, and you get a slim remote that thankfully includes the option to turn the display off. The Creative Stage Pro looks smart and fits neatly beneath my superwide monitor. It can be tough to find room for speakers on my desk, as I have a vertical monitor to the left. A soundbar can be a better fit if you have a similar setup using monitor arms. The Creative Stage Pro is a big improvement over previous Creative soundbars I tested (see below), and it’s a bargain if you’re set on a soundbar. Read our Best Soundbars guide for other picks.

As a fan of SteelSeries, makers of some of the best gaming mice, and the Apex Pro keyboard I use, I was excited to try its speaker lineup. The Arena 9 is SteelSeries' top-of-the-line surround sound system. It includes a central speaker flanked by a pair of front speakers with LED zones and a control unit, all of which plug into a large subwoofer. A pair of rear speakers connects to each other and a separate power outlet, but they connect to the rest of the system wirelessly, so you don’t have to run long cables. The Arena 9 speakers are fine for music, but the real reason to get a surround system is for movies or games. They elevated the epic battle scene as House Atreides falls in Dune and helped me react in the nick of time when Banished troops flanked me in Halo Infinite. Only a good set of headphones can provide better spatial sound.

The SteelSeries Arena 9 connects to a computer via a single USB cable, but there’s also an 3.5-mm aux and optical-in and -out ports. There are no controls on the speakers because you get a wired control unit with a touchscreen that acts as a volume dial by default, but can also be used to tweak EQ, lighting, and system settings. You can also dig into this on your computer via the SteelSeries GG software. The Prism RGB lighting can sync with the rest of your SteelSeries gear, with a huge range of animated effects, colors, and even the option to match the colors from your screen. Sadly, there are only four zones of LEDs (two on each front speaker), so it's not like the entire setup lights up. You will also need quite a bit of desk space and suitable spots for those rear speakers, but if you crave surround sound in your games, the Arena 9 is worth the hassle.

There are tons of computer speakers on the market, and many of the ones I tested are perfectly fine, but don't deserve a spot above for one reason or another.

Pioneer DJ DM-40D-BT for $239: Designed as entry-level speakers for bedroom DJs, the Pioneer DJ DM-40D-BTs sport a molded black plastic front with an MDF cabinet, but they feel well-built and solid. There’s a 4-inch fiberglass woofer with a 0.75-inch soft‑dome tweeter, and a class‑D amplifier with DSP (digital signal processing). Input options are RCA, 3.5-mm stereo mini, or Bluetooth 5.0, with a 3.5-mm headphone port in front, a simple volume knob, and a Bluetooth button. On the back of the control speaker, there’s a DJ/Production switch, which dials up the boomy bass. The sound quality is decent for the money, but they’re a bit too big for my desktop. If you need a budget option for music production to pair with a laptop, they might be a good shout, though the IK Multimedia iLoud Micro Monitors above are better if you can find the extra cash.

Edifier M60 for $200: Close to matching my top picks in sound quality, but significantly cheaper, the Edifier M60 speakers produce a rich, clear sound that persists when you crank the volume. The bass is decent, considering the lack of a subwoofer, but it can get muddy and a bit rattly. You have three connection options, including USB-C, 3.5 mm, or Bluetooth 5.3. My test music playlist sounded great on these speakers, and only the rumbly bass distracted me in games and movies, though they come with angled stands that point them at your ears and reduce vibrations. The M60 speakers are also the perfect size for most desktops. On the downside, the touch controls are a little annoying (you have to hold down for three seconds to power on/off or change mode), and the Edifier ConneX app for Android (also available on iOS) was a little flaky, though it offered some handy customization options and a six-band equalizer. Overall, these speakers are a solid option if this is your budget.

Creative Pebble Nova for $266: These eye-catching orb-shaped speakers with RGB rings around the base are a statement piece for your desk. They are big, angled at 45 degrees, and you have the choice of a flat base or elevated stands. They light up, produce a pleasant sound that’s rich across the board and with real depth, and can, frankly, get far too loud for your desktop. I was also impressed by the virtual surround sound for gaming. Don’t make the mistake of thinking the Creative App is superfluous, because it’s packed with options, from an equalizer to dialogue enhancement to RGB lighting controls. While I love the stand, it made them too tall for my desk because of my dual monitors. I also found the touch controls a bit finicky, and I advise switching off the power saving mode.

Ruark MR1 MK3 for $599: Perfect for a retro vibe, the styling includes a fabric front, and black finish with a dial on top. With a fluid and expansive soundstage, music sounds great on these speakers, and even without a subwoofer, there’s a decent rumble of bass. I found Ruark’s speakers equally pleasing for watching movies and video calls, as voice clarity is excellent, though I experienced some slight distortion with deep voices during meetings. They are also quite large, maybe too large for some desks.

OXS Thunder Pro for $600: With a compact design, sci-fi styling that includes RGB lighting, and no separate subwoofer, I was unsure what to expect from this soundbar. The Thunder Pro is 24 inches long and just 3 inches tall, so slotted into place perfectly under my monitor. It comes with a wired control dial, a remote control, and extra feet in the box to angle it. I was immediately impressed when the bass of Dead Prez’s “Hip-Hop” kicked in, bouncing the passive radiators up and down. The sound is expansive, the bass is far better than it has any right to be without a large subwoofer, and the highs are surprisingly crisp. This soundbar is Dolby Atmos-certified and has three distinct gaming modes. The first-person shooter mode offered excellent spatial sound, allowing me to hear the footsteps and grunts of a cloaked Elite in Halo Infinite just in time to avoid being impaled. Connectivity is comprehensive with USB-A and USB-C, 3.5-mm aux, Bluetooth 5.0, two HDMI ports (one eARC), a mic-in port, and a headphone jack. All that’s missing is the option to hook up a subwoofer. The wired control unit lights up, acts as a volume dial and power button, and has EQ and mode buttons on the back. While the OXS Thunder Pro impressed, you will get better sound, especially for music, if you buy separate speakers and a subwoofer.

Razer Nommo V2 Pro for $438: Stick to one brand for all your gaming gear, and it’s easier to achieve harmony. Plus, you only need one app to configure everything. If Razer is your jam, the Nommo V2 Pro is a 2.1 system with tempting bells and whistles. These stylish cylindrical speakers fire light out the back and tilt up towards your ears. The chunky subwoofer is wireless, which could be handy if you prefer to keep the space under your desk free. The wireless control puck acts as a volume dial, and you can press it to mute, play, or skip. You can expect booming bass and rich mids, but there’s a little crispness lacking in the highs, particularly when you crank the volume up. The sound profile is well-suited to action movies and games, with a deep bass rumble that you feel in the pit of your stomach. Connectivity is limited to USB or Bluetooth 5.3. I had some setup issues with the wireless control puck, but after a few attempts, the new firmware was installed successfully. I still found it a bit flaky and didn’t like having to hold it down to turn the speakers on. (They switch off automatically unless you deactivate the power-saving setting.) While Razer Chroma RGB now supports more than 300 games, offers various effects, and an ambient awareness mode that reacts to your screen, there are only two lighting zones (one for each speaker). Unfortunately, the Razer Synapse software is the only way to get at some settings, and it feels bloated and sometimes hogs system resources.

Logitech G560 Lightsync for $250: Folks who prefer Logitech gear should consider the G560 Lightsync. This 2.1 system comprises a huge subwoofer and two speakers with an attractive curved loop design. The speakers project RGB lighting onto the wall behind them. Sound-wise, the bass is dominant and the soundstage lacks definition, but you can play around with the equalizer or use the presets Logitech provides. For movies and gaming, the G560 does better, with DTS:X Ultra virtual surround sound to emulate larger systems. There’s a good thump of bass for explosions, and they provide a decent positional sense in games. These speakers connect via USB, but there’s also a 3.5-mm aux port and support for Bluetooth 4.1. Sadly, for lighting fans, you only get four zones of LEDs here, but there are lots of presets and colors to choose from, and you can have them react to the onscreen action. While these speakers don’t grab me, with the bass bossing the show, they are decent value, particularly as the price has dropped significantly since they were first released.

SteelSeries Arena 7 for $380: The SteelSeries Arena 7 boasts great sound and real versatility. This 2.1 system is the sweet spot in the SteelSeries Arena lineup, as it adds a chunky subwoofer and RGB lighting over the Arena 3, but is still relatively affordable. Simply plug in the USB and attach the pear-shaped speakers to the subwoofer. The adjustable speakers look interesting, with RGB lighting around the base and back, and a mode button and volume dial on the right speaker (headphone port behind it). This system produces a soundstage with clarity and precision, but can sound too bassy and flat, though you can tweak it in the SteelSeries GG software. The spatial sound for games is immersive and accurate enough to help you pinpoint footsteps or the whomp of an approaching helicopter. With USB, Bluetooth 4.2, optical in and out, and 3.5-mm aux, your connectivity bases are covered. You can also sync the Prism RGB lighting with the rest of your setup, have it react to music, or just pick a color. On the downside, the speakers are quite tall, and the subwoofer is bulky and sometimes overpowers the sound.

Majority DX10 Desktop Speakers for £13: They lack bass and general clarity, but these USB-powered speakers are the cheapest I’ve tried. These 10-watt, 2.5-inch, full-range speakers are angled up toward your ears and have a simple power and volume dial. There’s also a 3.5 mm headphone port. Only available in the UK, they could be enough if your budget is very tight.

Edifier G2000 Pro for $212: A spruced-up version of the G2000 above, the Pro is an upgrade to 64-watt power, 3-inch full-range drivers, and virtual 7.1 surround sound. Edifier also made these speakers translucent and added garish RGB lighting effects courtesy of LED strips in each speaker. They do sound a bit better, though still lacking in bass, and some folks will love the sci-fi light show, but there isn’t enough extra to justify the significant jump in price.

Creative Pebble X Plus for $123: If our budget pick isn’t quite doing it for you, Creative’s Pebble X Plus adds a cube-shaped subwoofer under your desk. The larger speakers, with RGB lighting, are also more powerful, and the sub provides punchy bass. They sound reasonably good for the money. Connectivity is also beefed up compared to the V3 above, with headphone and microphone ports and Bluetooth 5.3, but all of this invariably comes at a higher cost.

Onkyo Creator GX-10DB for $250: Capable of producing a warm and rich sound, Onkyo’s speakers are the perfect size for your desktop and come with angled stands (though they are plastic). The connectivity options are solid, with USB-C, optical, and stereo RCA inputs, and support for Bluetooth 5.3. These speakers get a little louder than our top pick, though you’ll need to pair a subwoofer if you’re into bass (there is a subwoofer-out port). Sadly, the volume knob on the back of the right speaker is tricky to reach, the slim remote control has a weird layout, and the source LED on the front is always on. But what I struggled with most was the automatic power-off (Onkyo says this “feature” is an eco-regulation in Europe) that turns off the speakers if they aren’t used for 15 minutes. If you have more space and crave more power, I also tested the Onkyo Creator Series GX-30ARC ($350), which goes far louder and adds a decent thump of bass.

Creative Sound Blaster GS5 for $95: While it doesn’t quite have the power of the OXS Thunder Lite above, this soundbar is cheaper and matches it on connectivity, even adding cool RGB lighting to the underside. It also has a remote and decent software for making EQ and lighting adjustments. The sound lacks clarity, and the bass is limited, but this is a solid, small soundbar for the money and works well as a desktop device.

Logitech Z407 for $110: A complete 2.1 system at this price is no mean feat. These speakers connect via USB, 3.5-mm aux, or Bluetooth, and there’s a wireless remote volume dial. Construction is cheap, and the sound lacks definition, but there’s a good thump of bass from the subwoofer, and mids and highs are reasonably crisp, provided you don’t turn the volume up too high. WIRED senior reviews editor Julian Chokkattu says he used a variation of the Logitech Z-series for nearly a decade and only recently donated them to someone else.

Razer Nommo V2 X for $150: The sound quality of Razer’s entry-level speakers is solid for music and good for movies and games (there's THX Spatial support), though lacking in bass. These no-frills speakers look great but lack a subwoofer, RGB lighting, and a remote. They offer USB and Bluetooth connectivity and can be customized in Razer’s Synapse software.

These are the computer speakers that had deal-breaking flaws for me.

OXS Thunder Duo X for $550: These powerful speakers get plenty loud and come with a wireless neck speaker for surround sound, but they are very large (way too big for my desk) and, frankly, kinda garish when they light up. The sound quality is decent and I liked the surround sound for gaming, but the neck speaker is not comfortable, especially for long gaming sessions (much will depend on your office chair). It can get plenty loud, supports virtual surround sound, and comes with a small remote. Unfortunately, separation was poor, so it was tough to tell where sounds were coming from in games, and it is severely lacking in bass for a soundbar with thunder in its name. Edifier QR65 for $314: Delivering classy looks with supercool lighting effects and a seriously big, powerful sound, these speakers are excellent for music, movies, or games. They also come with two angled stands to point them at your ears. They have USB, RCA, and Bluetooth 5.3 connectivity and USB ports for charging your gadgets. Controls are decent, with a physical button on the right speaker for power and cycling through modes. The problem is that they are huge and won’t fit on most desktops, including mine.

Creative Stage V2 for $150: This affordable soundbar offers excellent connectivity with HDMI ARC, optical, USB, 3.5-mm aux, and Bluetooth 5.0. But it’s not especially powerful, can sound muddy and flat, and has a short cable (no good for sit-stand desks). The subwoofer lends some decent bass, there’s a remote control, and I like the Clear Dialog option to sharpen voices, but you can do better.

House of Marley Get Together Duo for $170: Sporting an attractive and eco-friendly blend of bamboo and fabric, the Get Together Duo from House of Marley is affordable. They connect via Bluetooth 5.0, RCA, or 3.5-mm aux. There’s no subwoofer, the midrange is dominant, and they are lacking something at the high end. The battery-powered right speaker can also double as a portable speaker, and you can use these as bookshelf speakers, but as computer speakers, they are just OK.

SteelSeries Arena 3 for $150: Sporting a nice pear-shaped design with tiltable speakers and subtle branding, the entry-level Arena 3 from SteelSeries can get loud. But they lack bass, lose detail in the highs, and sound a bit fuzzy for music. Even for SteelSeries fans, there isn’t much reason to choose these over some of our picks above. They don’t have lighting, USB, or any other extras.

Logitech Z906 5.1 Surround Sound Speaker System for $450: This 5.1 system is THX-certified and comes with a huge subwoofer, five speakers, and a central control unit. The design feels dated, maybe even a bit ugly, and you need to run cables around your room. The surround sound effect is solid, and you can even convert stereo with the 3D mode, but overall, it’s a bass-heavy system.

Budget is a deciding factor for most folks, but even cheap desktop speakers offer far better sound than your monitor. Headphones are a great alternative, but not everyone wants to wear headphones all the time. You can spend as much as you like on audio gear, but I have focused on reasonably affordable desktop systems here, starting from as low as $35 and not exceeding $700.

What Should I Look for in Computer Speakers?

It depends. There’s plenty to weigh when shopping for a set of computer speakers, so it’s vital to start by deciding what’s most important to you. For example, I work and play on the same desktop PC, so I need speakers that perform well for video calls, long gaming sessions, listening to music, and watching movies. I also have a standing desk, and my monitor setup doesn’t leave much room, so I prefer compact all-rounders with long cables.

Sound quality: The top consideration for most folks, you want the best sound quality you can afford. A good set of speakers will offer a balance of clear vocals, rich treble, and rumbling bass. If you crave bass you feel in the pit of your stomach, a subwoofer is essential, though it might not be a good idea for folks who have downstairs neighbors. For accurate positional sound, you might want to look at a surround sound system with multiple speakers.

Design: Consider the finish. Your speakers will be on show, so you’ll want to pick something that fits your decor.

Brand: Gamers with devices from an existing brand might lean toward the same brand of speakers because they can be configured in the same software. This is particularly important with speakers that have built-in lights, so you can sync up your entire setup.

Extras: Some people will consider these superfluous, but if you want a remote control for kicking back or RGB lighting to bring a splash of color to your desk, these are extra perks on higher-end speakers. Snag speakers with a decent range of connectivity, and you can always repurpose them later. Most speakers come with all the cables you need, but it’s worth checking before you buy. I have listed what you will find in the box with all the computer speakers I recommend.

Where Should I Position My Desktop Speakers?

You ideally want the speakers to be angled slightly up (around 15 degrees) and pointed at your ears. Since you know the exact spot you’ll be in when listening, you can tweak the position of your speakers for the best results. Don’t just chuck them on the desk and leave it at that. Try moving and angling them until you find that sweet spot.

Speaker stands can maximize sound quality by helping to reduce vibrations from your desktop that can muddy the sound. Angled speaker stands are also a good way to direct the sound toward your ears. Some manufacturers, such as Edifier and Onkyo, include stands; IK Multimedia and Razer include adjustable bases or fold-out feet; Audioengine sells stands separately. You will also find a wide choice of third-party stands online.

If space is limited, a compact set of speakers will work best. The trade-off is power and volume, as small speakers generally don’t produce as big or high-quality a sound as large speakers. If you opt for a subwoofer, you will want a suitable space under your desk. For surround sound systems, you must run cables and find suitable spots for speakers behind you. If you have a wide monitor on a mount, a compact soundbar beneath it could be the best fit. Check the dimensions carefully and measure the space on your desk before you buy.

What’s the Best Way to Connect My Speakers to My Computer?

Speakers can come with a multitude of ports and connectivity options. USB connections usually sound best because they output the digital signal for the digital-to-analog converter (DAC) in the speakers, which is likely to be better unless you have a good sound card. But the best way to be sure is to try both and see which sounds better. If you want to be able to connect a phone or laptop wirelessly, then Bluetooth is paramount. Most speakers have a 3.5-mm aux input, and some have an output for headphones. Just make sure your preferred speakers support the connectivity you want. You should also make sure that you have the necessary free ports on your PC or laptop, though you can always add more with a USB hub.

Every set of speakers in this guide was tested for at least a week, sometimes longer. I don’t use headphones, so they were all used for video calls throughout my work day, listening to music and podcasts, and for movies and games in the evening. While I played several games, watched many movies or shows, and listened to lots of music and podcasts, I ran through a specific list of test content with every speaker system to make it easier to compare them.

My test content included the game Halo Infinite, the movie Dune, and a music playlist including “Hip-Hop” (Dead Prez), “The National Anthem” (Radiohead), “All Along the Watchtower” (Jimi Hendrix), “Hey” (Pixies), “Sabotage” (Beastie Boys), “A Day in the Life” (the Beatles), “Nothing Compares 2 U” (Sinéad O'Connor), and “Only God Knows” (Young Fathers).

#622. DNA on the Shroud of Turin reveals centuries of hidden secrets

Scientists have uncovered a remarkable collection of DNA hidden within the Shroud of Turin, revealing a surprisingly complex biological history behind one of the world's most mysterious and controversial religious artifacts. The genetic evidence includes traces of humans, bacteria, fungi, plants, animals, and even Mediterranean red coral, offering new clues about the environments and people that have come into contact with the famous cloth over the centuries.

Published in Scientific Reports, the study used advanced DNA sequencing technology to examine samples collected from the Shroud in 1978. For the first time, researchers applied a sophisticated technique (rigorous PCR-free next-generation metagenomic sequencing) to these officially collected samples, allowing them to identify genetic material preserved within the linen fibers in unprecedented detail.

Unlike traditional DNA testing, which often focuses on genetic material from a single organism, metagenomic sequencing can detect DNA from many different species within the same sample. This makes it particularly useful for studying historical objects that have been handled, stored, and exposed to changing environments over long periods.

By examining this genetic material, the international research team assembled what they describe as the most comprehensive picture yet of the biological traces accumulated on the Shroud throughout its history.

Human DNA and a Hidden World of Microorganisms

Among the discoveries were multiple lineages of human mitochondrial DNA, suggesting that genetic material from different human populations has accumulated on the cloth. Mitochondrial DNA is a small portion of our genetic material that is inherited through the maternal line, making it especially useful for studying ancestry and population history.

The researchers also identified an unexpectedly diverse microbiome, the collection of microscopic organisms associated with a particular environment or object. These included bacteria commonly found on human skin, various fungi, and microorganisms adapted to living in salty environments.

Some of these organisms belong to a group known as archaea, which are single-celled microorganisms distinct from bacteria. Certain archaea can thrive in extremely salty conditions that would be difficult for many other forms of life to tolerate.

Together, these findings reveal that the Shroud carries a complicated biological record shaped by repeated human contact and exposure to different environmental conditions.

DNA From Plants, Animals, and Mediterranean Coral

The discoveries extended far beyond human DNA and microorganisms. Scientists also detected genetic traces from numerous plant and animal species, adding another layer to the Shroud's biological history.

Plant DNA included cultivated wheat, carrots, maize, bananas, and peanuts. Genetic material from domesticated animals was also present, including cattle, pigs, chickens, dogs, and cats.

One particularly unusual finding was DNA associated with Mediterranean red coral.

The wide variety of biological material suggests that the cloth has encountered a diverse range of living organisms and environmental influences. These genetic traces may reflect its long history of handling, storage, and movement through different geographical regions.

Importantly, the presence of DNA from a particular species does not establish when that material reached the cloth. Some traces could have accumulated relatively recently, while others may reflect much older interactions. This makes the findings valuable for reconstructing the Shroud's environmental history, but also highlights the difficulty of interpreting DNA recovered from an object that has been exposed to human activity for centuries.

Can DNA Solve the Mystery of the Shroud of Turin?

Despite the remarkable diversity of genetic material, the researchers emphasize that their findings cannot establish the Shroud's age or authenticity.

The Shroud of Turin is a linen cloth bearing the faint image of a human figure and has long been associated by some believers with the burial of Jesus. Its origins have been debated for generations, making it one of the most extensively investigated historical and religious artifacts in the world.

Because the cloth has been touched and exposed to different environments over many centuries, its DNA represents a mixture of biological material accumulated at different times. This makes it impossible to reliably separate any original DNA that might have been associated with the fabric from the many genetic traces deposited afterward.

Instead, the research demonstrates how modern forensic genomics and proteomics can reveal information that traditional methods might miss. Forensic genomics uses genetic evidence to investigate biological origins and interactions, while proteomics examines proteins, which can also preserve valuable clues about biological materials.

Together, these approaches offer powerful new ways to investigate how historical objects have been preserved, handled, and exposed to different environments.

Modern DNA Technology Reveals New Historical Clues

Professor Noemi Procopio, Professor of Forensic Science at the University of Lancashire, contributed to the international research collaboration. She emphasized that advances in forensic technology are making it possible to recover information from historical samples collected decades earlier.

Professor Noemi Procopio, Professor of Forensic Science at the University of Lancashire, said: "The Shroud of Turin continues to fascinate historians, scientists and the public, and we're thrilled to reveal new information about the biological complexity preserved within samples collected almost 50 years ago by applying new cutting-edge forensic DNA technologies.

"The Shroud represents a rich archive of genetic information that has accumulated over centuries of human interaction and environmental exposure. While the DNA evidence cannot answer all the questions about the age or authenticity of the cloth, it provides novel insights into its biological history and demonstrates how advances in forensic science can unlock new information from historical artifacts."

#621. Marc Lore's Wonder confidentially files for IPO

Food startup Wonder has confidentially filed for an initial public offering, the company said Thursday.

Created by serial entrepreneur Marc Lore in 2018, Wonder advertises itself as "the best restaurants from across the country, all in one place," available for delivery and takeout on its vertically integrated app.

Originally envisioned as a new take on food delivery through sprinter vans outfitted with mobile kitchens, Wonder has grown to roughly 170 brick-and-mortar locations throughout the U.S. Its locations serve menu items from more than two dozen "restaurants" across a variety of cuisines, some with branding from celebrity chefs.

Starting Nov. 17, the company plans to introduce more dining options through Wonder Create. Influencers, entrepreneurs and brands can pay $10 a month to start their own restaurant business based out of a Wonder location and earn a portion of the revenue from orders.

Wonder has spent at least $1 billion on acquisitions, such as delivery service Grubhub, meal kit brand Blue Apron and delivery and logistics provider Relay, along with some buzzy restaurant concepts. Wonder has been able to afford such expensive dealmaking thanks to more than $3 billion in venture funding.

Wonder's Series D fundraising round earlier this year raised its pre-money valuation to $9 billion.

With the proceeds from an IPO, Wonder will be able to make more acquisitions and work toward its goal of making food more accessible, Lore told CNBC in September.

But as a public company, Wonder will also likely face investor scrutiny. Weak consumer sentiment has weighed on restaurant stocks recently, plus market volatility has resulted in a shaky IPO market for companies across a range of industries.

Lore previously founded Diapers.com and Jet.com, which he sold to Amazon and Walmart, respectively.

#620. Elon Musk insists Cybertruck demand is strong as Tesla scraps it

Tesla Cybertruck is dead. The massive, steel-plated electric pickup has never sold particularly well, and Tesla CEO Elon Musk said last week that Cybertruck production will wind down next year.

Musk would like the world to know, however, that the company isn't scrapping Cybertruck due to unprofitability or lack of demand.

"The Cybertruck order rate far exceeds our production rate. We will try to make as many as possible before shutting down the line in about 8 months, but will have to return deposits for a lot of people," wrote Musk in a tweet on Monday. "This is not a case of Cybertruck being unprofitable, as has been misreported by legacy media, but rather that the opportunity cost of the complexity of Cybertruck is vastly outweighed by the value of incremental Cybercab production," he wrote.

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

That's an interesting logical hoop Musk would like you to jump through. Given that Tesla doesn't report precise Cybertruck sales numbers, and typically doesn't talk about profitability of individual models, it's hard for an outsider to definitively call this or that model unprofitable. We've never said it, by the way; we merely pointed to poor sales figures, based on reports and Tesla's own figures, which lump Cybertruck with "other models" in quarterly reports.

But how do we know the sales figures are bad? Well, we merely compare Musk's predictions with actual sales numbers. In 2023, Musk predicted Tesla would sell 250,000 to 500,000 Cybertruck units annually. In the third quarter of 2026, Tesla delivered 8,295 "other models," which include Cybertruck, Model X, and Model S. The total deliveries for this category in the past four quarters are 48,431; even if all those were Cybertrucks (which they definitely weren't), that's still less than a fifth of Musk's lower projected figure for Cybertruck annual sales.

So yes: Cybertruck isn't selling nearly as well as Musk predicted. The company is placing all bets on autonomous driving, and has scrapped Model S, Model X, and Cybertruck while ramping up Cybercab production. Musk is free to call the Cybertruck a great victory, but we bet the company wouldn't be winding down production so easily had the demand been five or ten times bigger than what it was.
