#619. Welcome to Weed Island

Steven Tan walks along a narrow concourse in the Northern Leaf greenhouse in St Lawrence, Jersey, reading aloud the strains of his cannabis plants: Pink Gasoline, Moon Beam Gelato, Carbon Fibre, Gorilla Kush. Rows of plants stretch off into the distance. To the untrained eye, they look largely the same, with their distinctive jagged-edged, multi-fingered leaf shape and sticky buds like tiny pinecones.

But Tan, the CEO and director of medical cannabis manufacturer Northern Leaf, knows them intimately. “These plants are all different,” he says, stopping next to a mature Carbon Fibre specimen. “Just like humans.”

Northern Leaf is one of the largest licensed medical marijuana producers in Europe. Its 100,000-square-foot growing facility is based in Jersey, the largest of the Channel Islands, located between England and France. Previously, this greenhouse was home to Jersey tomatoes, once a famed crop on the island that came close to extinction, thanks to growing fuel costs and cheaper competition from overseas. Now, it is fitted out with all of the technology required for cultivating cannabis that meets strict regulations for medical use, which include: automated irrigation, environmental monitoring, and air purification.

Despite being just 45 square miles in size, with a population of 100,000, Jersey—which lies 14 miles off the coast of the French mainland but is a British Crown Dependency—has emerged as an unlikely player in the medical cannabis industry. Although its total exports (over 9,200 kilograms in 2025) are small compared to UK growers, this represents an enormous amount per capita, and cannabis is fast becoming one of the island’s important new industries. Jersey’s chief minister, Lyndon Farnham, has previously said that he wants Jersey to be a “centre of excellence” for medical cannabis, and for cannabis production to become a mainstay of the island’s economy.

Jersey, a British Crown Dependency that sits between the UK and France, that has become an integral hub for the medical cannabis industry.

Currently, 90 percent of Jersey’s cannabis production comes from Northern Leaf. In 2025, the company exported £8.4 million ($11.3 million) worth of medical marijuana to markets including the UK and Germany (regulations mean it is only permitted to grow for export and cannot sell to customers in Jersey). Tan has much greater ambitions. He points to another famous Jersey export, Jersey Royal potatoes. “Jersey’s potato industry did £40 million in 2025,” he said. “I am already doing a quarter of the potato industry on 5 percent of the land. On 20 percent of the land, I could create an industry worth more than the potato industry. That is where it will get interesting.”

Born in Malaysia to a Chinese dad and Irish mother, Tan, 50, wanted to become a vet, but after graduating from university in 1998, ended up developing software for Citibank. He was making good money, but his heart wasn’t in finance. What really interested him was growing weed. “I loved the science of it, the genetics, the seeds, the smells,” Tan says.

The early 2000s were the perfect time for an inquisitive young business grad to get into the cannabis business. Across the world, governments were legalizing medical marijuana and moving towards decriminalization over draconian sentencing.

Tan began bouncing around jurisdictions that were relaxing laws on cannabis, working as a consultant in California, the Netherlands, and Canada. “I started to see these legal frameworks opening up. The minute they existed, I went there,” he says.

Jersey has a somewhat unique regulatory environment. As a Crown Dependency, it has close ties to Britain, but is not a part of the UK. Crucially, it is self-governing: It can make its own laws. Jersey’s main reputation today is as a tax haven, attracting some of the world’s wealthiest individuals and companies, thanks to its lack of capital gains tax and 0 percent corporation tax for most companies. Along its coastline, next to twee beachside cafes and Nazi bunkers (the Channel Islands were the only part of the British Isles to be occupied by Hitler’s forces in World War II), you can find offices of international banks and wealth management firms catering to the super rich.

Although traditionally culturally conservative, Jersey has become something of a trailblazer on cannabis. The island legalized medical cannabis in 2019. Today, five private clinics operate on the island. The latest figures suggest that 6 percent of the population is prescribed cannabis, compared to an estimated 0.4 percent in Germany and 0.1 percent in the UK. Upon legalization, the island’s chief minister, Lyndon Farnham, laid out ambitious plans for the island to become a hub of the medical cannabis trade, repurposing the horticultural facilities and know-how from its tomato-growing glory days for this new endeavor.

Tan joined Northern Leaf as CEO in 2024, when the company was in dire straits after a failed IPO. That year, it was valued at just £3.7 million, despite having spent as much as £30 million on its weed-growing facility.

Tan nevertheless saw a lot of potential, and over the last two years has seen capacity at the site increase from 25 percent to 75 percent. He believes the £8.4 million of product the company exported last year is just the beginning.

Northern Leaf's 100,000-square-foot growing facility. “On 20 percent of the land,” says CEO Steven Tan, “I could create an industry worth more than the potato industry. That is where it will get interesting.”

Sarah Clover, a barrister at Kings Chambers who works on cannabis licensing in Jersey, told WIRED the island’s advantage is hard to replicate. Growers are inspected by the UK Medicines and Healthcare products Regulatory Agency (MHRA), which is known for enforcing rigorous standards on quality. “Anybody buying from Jersey knows that the stuff coming out of it is absolutely gold standard, because the MHRA says it is,” she says. Permits, however, are handled by a small team on the island: “You can make an application for a permit on a Monday and get it on a Tuesday.”

Many within the Jersey medicinal cannabis industry now want to go beyond growing the raw ingredient and package it for export as a final product on the island. Northern Leaf already has the facilities to do so, but Jersey law dictates it can only export bulk amounts of raw cannabis for other companies to process and pack for distribution and sale. The company is waiting to see if Jersey will change its law. “At the minute we’re kind of on this seesaw—which way will the island go?” Clover says. “It could really take off here. Jersey could be a life sciences hub, not just for cannabis but for all sorts of pharmaceuticals.”

But running a cannabis business on a small island poses challenges, and not everyone is enthused about the idea of expansion. Tan has faced off against residents every time that the facility has expanded production. Some locals have complained that they can smell cannabis from their balconies. Others have linked the growing industry to concerns over misuse of cannabis on the island, despite the fact that all of its product is exported. Cannabis is a “contributory factor” in 22 percent of admissions to the island’s acute mental health ward, according to Jersey’s health minister.

One patient, Michael, told WIRED that he was prescribed medical cannabis for 18 months after having tried two other prescribed medications for insomnia and anxiety. “The initial assessment was simple. I had a meeting with the cannabis doctor who was pretty evangelical about its medicinal properties. They check your medical history with your GP, and within 10 days you’re good to go,” he says.

Michael was prescribed 30 grams a month of “unbelievably good” cannabis and told that if he ran out he could back and ask for more. “I stopped because it was seriously affecting my mental health [and] I have since seen a psychiatric professional who said I should never have been prescribed it given my documented mental health history,” he says.

Illegal resale of medical cannabis is also an issue, with a rise in so-called “user-dealers,” who sell their excess medical marijuana to others. Jersey Police told WIRED this “undermines the legal framework and creates opportunities for criminal supply.”

Overall, however, cannabis-related offenses have dropped since Jersey legalized medical cannabis in 2019—from 216 in 2016 to just 12, as of July 2026.

Tan is circumspect about the challenges of operating on Jersey. “Culturally, the people here are harder to convince about cannabis,” he says. “It’s an older society.” But after 25 years working in the industry, he’s used to the pushback.

“It’s a normal challenge,” he says. “And I think when anyone sits down and meets me, they understand. This isn’t a bunch of stoners growing a bit of weed in a plastic greenhouse.”

While Jersey’s medical cannabis industry grows, some people on the island want to go further. In 2024, a vote to decriminalize recreational cannabis was defeated by a single vote in the island’s parliament. When it was brought back for another vote by the island’s then health minister, it was withdrawn before it could be debated. It is expected to be brought back before the parliament in the coming months.

Some individuals on the island have decided not to wait. By exploiting a loophole in current laws, they have made Jersey one of the only places in Europe outside of Amsterdam where THC products can be purchased and consumed legally.

One morning in February 2024, Gary Tucker arrived at work, his weed shop in St Helier, Jersey, to find a dozen customs officers and the local media waiting outside. It was a raid—and not his first. The officers emptied shelves and boxes into bin bags. “They were literally running their arms down the shelves and putting everything into bags. They took everything,” Tucker, a California native and former analyst in the US Army, told WIRED.

Tucker operates outside of Jersey’s medical cannabis industry. His shop, Bailiwick Botanicals, sells products that are clearly recreational: hash cakes, gummies, and other snacks, all infused with tetrahydrocannabinol (THC), the psychoactive ingredient in cannabis that gets you high and is heavily controlled.

He’s able to do this owing to a loophole in Jersey law which allows the sale of products containing CBD—another critical ingredient in cannabis, but one that doesn’t get you high. Jersey’s law says that the amount of THC in CBD products cannot exceed 3 percent of the weight of the CBD. So, if you want to sell products with high levels of THC, you just have to boost the CBD. “It’s just a math equation,” Tucker says.

Tucker admits it isn’t an ideal situation: studies suggest that ingesting high levels of CBD can cause liver damage. But he sees that as an issue with the regulation, not his solution. “I’m just following consumer demand,” he says.

Tucker is somewhat evangelical about the properties of cannabis. In his time as a weapons trainer for the military, he struggled with the mental health impacts of hearing that recruits he had trained had been killed in action. “I needed to step back from it. It started to really affect my mind,” Tucker says.

Veterans he knew were turning to cannabis, and Tucker began buying it on the black market. “It was the only thing that helped me,” he says. ”Cannabis gave me my life back.”

Tucker currently manufactures most of his products in the US before shipping them to Sark—a smaller Channel Island best known for having no cars and also its own government—and then to Jersey. New regulations in the US, however, mean Tucker may need to change his approach.

Tucker sees Jersey as a potential export hub for THC products to Europe. He believes Jersey’s budding weed industry should push beyond medical cannabis and into the recreational market. “This isn’t just a bunch of guys in a cellar somewhere. Not only does cannabis help people, but getting it to the shelf creates jobs. From the grower to the retailer. It permeates across all industries. We’re making cosmetics. We’re making gummies. We’re making drinks,” he says.

After the 2024 raid, Bailiwick Botanicals shut its doors for two weeks, and its full stock wasn’t returned until six months later. As of June 2026, Tucker and his team are selling THC-infused fudge, chocolate, and other sweets. His newest product, THC-infused coffee beans, is selling well. The police have stayed away.

In a written statement to WIRED, Jersey’s government said that the “possibility of abuse of our limits to get round the law in other jurisdictions is a matter of concern to the Government of Jersey and we are looking at ways to address it. Work is underway to consider whether Jersey’s existing legal exemptions for CBD products require amendment.”

“These plants are all different. Just like humans," says Tan.

For his part, Tan is reluctant to be drawn on his views on the so-called “rec” (recreational) market. For now, Jersey still has harsh sentencing rules on recreational cannabis use, with the penalties for possession and possession with intent to supply remaining up to five years and 14 years in jail, respectively. The proposed (and withdrawn) law to decriminalize cannabis notes that, the way the law is currently written, someone who rolled three joints and gave one to a friend would be guilty of supply and receive a criminal record. “Without change, people will continue receiving serious criminal records for small amounts … punishments [that] can result in additional harms greater than the drug itself,” it said.

While Jersey makes its own laws, however, it may not be able to completely legalize cannabis. Jersey is a signatory to the UN Convention on drugs control via the UK, and the UK Home Office would undoubtedly have a view if the island were to set itself up as an “Amsterdam on the Sea.” In 2022, an effort by the government of Bermuda (a British Overseas Territory) to legalize recreational cannabis was blocked after the UK withheld Royal Assent from the bill.

Back at the Northern Leaf warehouse in the Jersey parish of St Lawrence, Tan is flipping the lids off large boxes and rifling through clear plastic bags packed full of cannabis.

This is all weed that will not be sold, either because there were issues with production or the THC levels were too low. Instead, it is taken to a secure area of the facility where it is locked away in a reinforced armored vault. Specialist staff with bodycams take the bags to the island’s incinerator, where it is unceremoniously burned, as required by law.

Tan regularly makes a case to the government for changes to the law and support for the cannabis industry, such as an industrial energy tariff to help with spiraling energy costs.

He has claimed that Jersey could be home to “the Monsanto of cannabis,” alluding to the agricultural giant that was acquired by pharmaceutical company Bayer in 2018 for $66 billion. He’d like to get deeper into the genetics of cannabis, form a tissue-culture lab, and “bring out strains that nobody has ever seen.”

But speaking of himself, he references a different genre of business: He’s Willy Wonka, but for weed. Thirty years ago, when Tan quit corporate finance, his father warned him that he could make a lot more money if he stayed in banking. “And you know what, he was right,” he says. “But I would not have had nearly as much fun.”

#617. After 40 years, CERN finds evidence of a one-in-10-billion particle decay

After more than 40 years of searching, physicists at CERN have reached a major milestone in their hunt for one of the rarest known particle decays. Researchers working with the LHCb experiment have detected evidence of a process expected to occur only once in approximately 10 billion beauty particle decays.

The finding, announced during a seminar at CERN, marks the first time a single experiment has produced sufficient evidence for this extraordinarily rare event. Beyond its rarity, the decay is particularly valuable because it could help scientists investigate particles that are too massive to be created directly in the Large Hadron Collider.

At the center of the discovery is a particle called Bd, which contains a beauty quark and a down antiquark. Scientists found evidence that this particle can decay into a pair of muons (μ+μ-). Muons are similar to electrons but have greater mass. The results agree with predictions from the Standard Model, the leading theory describing fundamental particles and their interactions, and help narrow the possibilities for new particles beyond it.

Why Rare Particle Decays Could Reveal New Physics

One reason physicists are so interested in these unusual decays is that quantum effects allow other particles to influence how they occur. Even particles too heavy to be produced in the Large Hadron Collider can potentially leave subtle traces in the rates of these processes.

By measuring extremely rare decays with high precision, researchers can look for differences between experimental results and theoretical predictions. Such discrepancies could provide indirect evidence of previously unknown particles. Generally, the rarer a decay is, the more sensitive it can be to these hidden influences.

The decay known as Bd → μ+μ-belongs to a special category that physicists have long considered 'golden channels' for investigating physics beyond the Standard Model. During this process, a beauty quark and an antiquark with the opposite electric charge annihilate each other. Their energy is transformed into a muon and its antiparticle.

Scientists have previously observed a related process involving a different beauty particle, called Bs. In 2015, a joint analysis by the LHCb and CMS collaborations reported the first confirmed observation of Bs → μ+μ-, along with initial evidence for the much rarer Bd decay.

However, subsequent analyses incorporating data collected through 2018 could not confirm the earlier evidence for Bd → μ+μ-. The difference in rarity is substantial. The Bs decay occurs approximately three times per billion beauty particle decays, while the Bd process is predicted to happen about 30 times less frequently.

Upgraded CERN Detector Makes the Breakthrough Possible

A significant improvement to the LHCb experiment helped researchers overcome the enormous difficulty of detecting such a rare event.

Following a major upgrade, the experiment began collecting data in 2024 at five times the rate achieved between 2010 and 2018. The upgraded detector also became better at identifying different kinds of particles, giving researchers a clearer picture of the activity produced during collisions.

The team combined these improvements with machine learning techniques to distinguish the exceptionally rare signals from the vast number of other particle processes. Without these advances, the events scientists wanted to study would have been overwhelmed by the background.

"Physicists have been chasing this decay for more than forty years," said Marco Santimaria, who co-led the international analysis team of 19 scientists. "Extreme precision is required to pick the muons out of the overwhelming background."

Four Decades of Searching Finally Produce Strong Evidence

In particle physics, scientists use a statistical measure called standard deviations to evaluate how likely an apparent signal is to result from random fluctuations rather than a genuine physical process.

A result must reach three standard deviations to qualify as evidence of a new phenomenon. At that level, the probability of random fluctuations producing a signal at least as statistically extreme is approximately 0.3%.

Using data collected during 2024 and 2025, the LHCb researchers identified evidence of Bd → μ+μ- at a significance of 3.0 standard deviations. When they combined those measurements with earlier data gathered between 2011 and 2018, the statistical significance increased to 3.2 standard deviations, exceeding the widely accepted threshold for evidence.

The same investigation also produced the most precise measurements ever obtained for the related Bs → μ+μ-decay, providing additional information about these exceptionally uncommon processes.

Although the new findings represent a major achievement, physicists have not yet reached the threshold required to declare a confirmed observation. That milestone requires a significance of five standard deviations.

If future measurements achieve that level of certainty, Bd → μ+μ-would become the rarest beauty particle decay ever observed.

Future Large Hadron Collider Upgrades Could Uncover Hidden Particles

Researchers expect upcoming improvements to the Large Hadron Collider to make these measurements even more powerful.

The High Luminosity LHC, also known as the HiLumi LHC, is scheduled to begin operating in 2030. Another major upgrade to the LHCb experiment is planned for the middle of the following decade. Together, these developments could eventually increase the experiment's data collection rate by another factor of ten.

Collecting much larger datasets would allow physicists to detect more of these extremely rare decays and measure their properties with greater precision. That increased sensitivity could make it possible to identify subtle effects associated with particles that remain beyond the direct reach of existing accelerators.

"Seeing evidence of this rare decay is incredibly exciting, opening up new possibilities to probe beyond the direct reach of the LHC," explained Harry Cliff, LHCb's Rare Decays Convener and Analysis Co-lead.

"The future LHCb upgrade will allow us to observe many more of these decays, providing even greater sensitivity to undiscovered particles."

For physicists who have pursued this elusive signal for more than four decades, the new evidence represents a long-awaited advance. It also offers a promising way to investigate whether the known laws of particle physics are part of a much larger picture.

Materials provided by CERN. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#615. Bikers and Bug-Lovers Join Forces to Fight a New Google Data Center

On Wednesday afternoon, Jamie Robins strolled up to the entrance of Google’s UK headquarters in Kings Cross, London. He brandished a letter containing details of a petition signed by almost 55,000 people, calling for Google to abandon plans to build a large new data center in Thurrock, 15 miles to the east of London.

Robins works for wildlife conservation trust Buglife, part of an unlikely alliance of special-interest groups trying to block the Thurrock development. Buglife and local wildlife groups fear for an endangered plant, rare bumblebee, and other invertebrates whose habitat they say will be destroyed. Nonprofit Foxglove says the data center will emit too much pollution. The Thurrock Hammers motorcycle club wants to return to racing on the site.

Members of the various groups joined Robins outside Google’s office last Wednesday, holding placards demanding the company “search elsewhere” for somewhere to put its facility.

Across the UK, companies like Google are preparing to build industrial-scale data centers, part of a scramble to meet the swelling demand for the compute needed to run artificial intelligence models and other digital services. The UK government is eager to encourage them, hoping to capture a share of the hundreds of billions of dollars flowing into the AI industry and minimize the country’s dependence on foreign partners for compute. But like in the US, people living near to the proposed developments are making their objections known and trying to gum up the works.

Google says its plans for the Thurrock site prioritize biodiversity and the reduction of emissions. “We share the concerns that Buglife and others have—and their expectation that this site should be developed sustainably,” Adam Elman, director of sustainability EMEA at Google, tells WIRED. But the situation in Thurrock underscores the level and breadth of resistance that data center developers face as the public mood towards them appears to sour. Though small, the demonstration outside Google was one of a number of anti-data center protests that took place across Europe last week. In Amsterdam, protesters scaled a crane at a Microsoft data center site and unfurled a “fuck AI” banner.

“There’s local campaign groups against data centers popping up every week,” says Robins. “People are angry about data centers turning up in their back gardens. They’re angry about data centers flouting local opinion—and about the impact it has on wildlife.”

The Thurrock site is located next to a fuel stop, where an arterial road meets the highway that encircles London. On the plot, the remnants of Arena Essex, a disused motorcycle racing stadium, border grassland scribbled with old footpaths, and a lake.

Google acquired the site through a subsidiary in 2023. In May last year, it applied for permission to build a data center campus there. When locals caught wind, they coordinated with national wildlife and environmental groups and began to kick up a fuss. Of the 1,000 responses submitted by members of the public to the planning authority, only one supported the project. (Thurrock Council says that many of the responses were submitted by people living outside the local area.)

The protest group argues that Google’s proposal fails to properly account for the negative effects of the data center development: the loss of the raceway sporting facility, the destruction of habitat for rare flora and fauna, and the up to 570,000 tonnes of emissions it could produce each year—which Foxglove claims cannot be accommodated under the UK government’s carbon budget.

“We’re an outlier. We object on different grounds. I think that’s why I think we’re quite useful to the whole campaign,” says Mark Sexton, director at the Thurrock Hammers motorcycle club. “The unlikely alliance has got some attention.”

Google says the objections are grounded in misconceptions about its plans. The company claims it will achieve a net gain in biodiversity by retaining habitat and transplanting endangered plants to another location; and that it will offset the emissions by financing new sources of renewable energy generation, with a view to becoming carbon-neutral by 2030. Google argues that razing the motorcycle stadium will not result in the loss of an invaluable community facility because the track has already been out of use for a number of years. “We want to be good neighbors,” says Elman. “We recognize the ecological value of these sites. Our goal is to minimize the impact as much as possible.”

Unconvinced by Google’s reassurances, the protesters intend to continue to fight the development—one small act of protest at a time. “Do I think we’ve got much hope? Not right now,” says Rob Pownall, founder of nonprofit Protect The Wild, who attended Wednesday’s demonstration. “But this is another chip off that massive ice block.”

There are some signs the grassroots anti-data center movement is starting to win concessions. In the US, some states have instituted permitting freezes and data center moratoria, and pro-data center candidates were roundly crushed in this week’s midterm elections. In the UK, Foxglove recently succeeded in a legal challenge that forced the government to withdraw planning permission for a data center to the west of London.

Thurrock Council tells WIRED it expects to rule on Google’s initial planning application early next year. After that, the company will need to come up with a more granular plan for developing the site, which will be subject to another lengthy vetting process. The protesters say they intend to make life hard for Google at every step.

“These planning campaigns can go on for years,” says Robins. “It’s a war of attrition.”

#613. NASA reveals bold new plan to return astronauts to the Moon in 2028

NASA has revealed new details about its accelerated effort to return American astronauts to the Moon in 2028. The updated strategy includes testing two competing lunar landers, streamlining rocket preparations, and introducing a lighter spacesuit to help make the first moonwalks possible sooner.

During an October 23 briefing, NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman and other agency leaders outlined the next steps for the Artemis program. The revised approach centers on two major missions: Artemis III, a test flight scheduled for 2027, and Artemis IV, which is intended to land astronauts on the lunar surface in 2028.

The announcement builds on priorities introduced earlier this year through NASA's Ignition initiative. Agency officials also highlighted recent progress in spacecraft development, mission preparations, and partnerships with private aerospace companies. These efforts are intended to reduce technical risks, accelerate the schedule, and establish the foundation for a lasting American presence on the Moon.

"This is a very energized NASA, and these updates are only a small part of that story. We are bringing extreme focus to the mission to ensure delivery of world-changing outcomes as quickly, safely, affordably, and successfully as possible," said Isaacman. "NASA is driving outcomes to make Moon missions a reality, and we're not waiting for others to bring their plans to us -- the campaign must move with urgency as strategic competitors pursue a permanent presence beyond Earth."

Artemis III Will Test Two Lunar Landers in Earth Orbit

Before attempting another crewed Moon landing, NASA plans to conduct a major rehearsal in Earth orbit. The Artemis III mission will bring together NASA's Orion spacecraft and experimental lunar landers developed by SpaceX and Blue Origin.

Four astronauts will travel aboard Orion, launching on NASA's powerful SLS (Space Launch System) rocket. During the planned 14-day mission, the crew will practice rendezvous and docking procedures with both companies' lander test vehicles. Rendezvous involves guiding two spacecraft to meet in orbit, while docking allows them to connect so astronauts can move between vehicles.

These demonstrations are designed to verify important spacecraft capabilities before Artemis IV attempts to carry astronauts to the Moon's surface.

The tests will take place in a carefully planned sequence. Blue Origin will first launch a test version of its Blue Moon lander into Earth orbit. NASA will then send Orion and its four astronauts into space aboard the SLS rocket, where Orion will rendezvous and dock with the Blue Moon vehicle.

Next, SpaceX will launch a test version of its Version 3 Starship lunar lander. Orion will separate from Blue Moon and perform another docking maneuver, this time with Starship. By testing both systems during a single crewed mission, NASA aims to gather essential operational experience while reducing uncertainties before the 2028 landing attempt.

NASA Cuts Rocket Preparation Times and Costs

The new mission details follow other recent Artemis progress reports. According to NASA, the hardware needed for Artemis III remains on schedule, and improvements in processing have reduced preparation timelines by approximately 50%.

The agency also reported a significant engineering achievement involving a critical rocket fuel-line component. Rather than relying on an outside supplier, NASA engineers redesigned, manufactured, and tested the component themselves in just five months. The work cost roughly one-tenth of an earlier estimate from an external vendor.

"NASA should be expected to do this kind of work, and we have some of the best technical knowledge in the world on rocket hardware and operations to do it," said Jeremy Parsons, the agency's Artemis program manager. "It's an example of how we are rebuilding our core competencies and developing centers of excellence in technical functions instead of continuing to outsource expertise."

The improvements reflect NASA's broader effort to strengthen its internal engineering capabilities, shorten development schedules, and reduce the cost of preparing missions for launch.

SpaceX and Blue Origin Offer Different Paths to the Moon

For Artemis IV in 2028, NASA intends to launch astronauts aboard Orion using the SLS rocket from Kennedy Space Center in Florida. Once in space, the crew will rely on a lunar landing system developed by either SpaceX or Blue Origin to reach the Moon's surface.

Both companies have revised their original mission designs to make operations simpler, more efficient, and more adaptable. Although they share the same objective, their approaches to transporting astronauts to the lunar surface differ considerably.

SpaceX plans to use its Starship spacecraft, which will take on additional propellant while orbiting Earth. After refueling, Starship will meet Orion and carry the spacecraft toward the Moon. Refueling in orbit is an important part of SpaceX's strategy because it allows the vehicle to receive the propellant needed for its journey after leaving the launchpad.

Blue Origin is developing a different approach using its Blue Moon Mark 2 lander. Its plan calls for additional propulsion stages in Earth orbit to send the lander toward the Moon. The lander will then meet Orion in a relatively low lunar orbit before carrying astronauts down to the surface.

Both companies are working toward having their systems ready to support a crewed Moon landing in 2028. Maintaining two potential landing systems gives NASA greater flexibility as development and testing continue.

NASA Explores New Rocket Options for Artemis IV

NASA is also considering two possible upper stages for the SLS rocket used on Artemis IV. An upper stage is the portion of a rocket that provides additional propulsion after the earlier stages have completed their work, helping send a spacecraft toward its destination.

One option is the interim cryogenic propulsion stage already used by SLS. The other is an extended version of United Launch Alliance's Centaur V upper stage, which would hold additional propellant.

By developing both possibilities, NASA hopes to preserve flexibility in its launch plans and avoid becoming dependent on a single technical solution. Having alternatives could help the agency respond to engineering challenges without jeopardizing the broader Moon landing schedule.

A New Lightweight Spacesuit Could Speed Up Moonwalks

Another important change involves the spacesuits astronauts will wear while exploring the lunar surface.

NASA is working with Axiom Space on a second, lighter design known as the "Sortie suit." The new suit is intended specifically for the first Moon surface excursions and weighs approximately half as much as Axiom's larger primary spacesuit.

The lighter design could simplify preparations for the earliest landing missions and help astronauts begin exploring the lunar surface sooner.

However, the Sortie suit is not intended to replace the primary design. Axiom's more capable suit will remain part of NASA's plans for future missions that require longer periods outside the spacecraft and more demanding surface activities.

Building a Permanent Human Presence on the Moon

NASA's ambitions extend well beyond returning astronauts to the lunar surface for a single mission.

As the Artemis program advances, the agency intends to send crews on increasingly challenging expeditions near the Moon's South Pole. This region is an important focus of future lunar exploration, and the missions are designed to expand how much of the Moon humans can investigate directly.

NASA describes the effort as part of a new Golden Age of innovation and exploration. The agency expects its lunar missions to support scientific discoveries, generate economic benefits, and encourage the development of technologies needed for more ambitious space exploration.

Ultimately, the goal is to establish an enduring human presence on the Moon rather than repeat the brief visits of earlier lunar expeditions. The experience gained from operating spacecraft, supporting astronauts, and carrying out increasingly complex missions on the lunar surface will also help NASA prepare for another historic objective: sending the first astronauts to Mars.

#611. DHS Has Used This Controversial Investigative Tool 185,000 Times Since Trump Took Office

Homeland Security Investigations, the investigative wing of Immigration and Customs Enforcement that normally takes on transnational crime, estimates it has sent out at least 185,000 administrative subpoenas since the start of the Trump administration, according to a recent court filing. The figure shows how HSI has increasingly relied on this warrantless authority to conduct investigations.

To put HSI’s estimate into perspective, a previous WIRED analysis found that in the more than six and a half years from the start of 2016 to mid-August 2022, ICE agents only sent out around 170,000 of a type of administrative subpoena called a 1509 customs summons.

The number of administrative subpoenas was revealed as part of a Freedom of Information Act lawsuit against the Department of Homeland Security filed by the Electronic Frontier Foundation. Aaron Mackey, deputy legal director at the nonprofit, says the EFF hopes to get more details about the 185,000 estimate. The EFF originally asked for information about how many administrative subpoenas were sent to technology companies, internet platforms, and service providers by ICE or DHS since January 20, 2025. It’s not clear if that count is just for administrative subpoenas sent to those types of companies or if it includes a much broader set.

Administrative subpoenas are a powerful but unchecked investigative tool. Unlike with a grand jury subpoena or a court order, the government doesn’t have to explain to the courts why it needs the documents or what data it’s requesting from a company.

Since the start of the Trump administration, the federal government has used DHS’s administrative subpoena power to try to unmask anonymous online critics and pursue broad customer records about protesters. This includes, for example, a list of people in the Minneapolis-St. Paul area who bought a specific green beanie from REI.

Congress originally granted the use of 1509 customs summons to the then stand-alone US Customs Service nearly a century ago, with the idea that it would let customs officers inspect business records related to importing merchandise and figure out whether they owed customs duties. In 2003, Congress transferred that agency’s powers, including the ability to issue customs summons, to the newly formed Department of Homeland Security.

In the past, DHS has maintained that customs summons could be used only for specific purposes. The DHS inspector general admonished Customs and Border Protection’s Office of Professional Responsibility in 2017 for trying to use a customs summons to unmask an anonymous Twitter poster, pointing out that its own procedures manual said that customs summons could only be used to obtain information related to customs or immigrations laws.

That, however, has changed. The Trump administration recently argued in court that because the Customs Service’s powers were transferred to DHS following the passing of the Homeland Security Act, it can use that power to tackle its “broad investigative portfolio,” which it says includes enforcing laws “beyond legacy customs and immigration enforcement.”

“When we give law enforcement agencies powerful investigative tools, even with the best intentions and purposes to try to go after legitimate investigations and crime, you have to be really careful,” says Mackey. The newly reported filing is part of an ongoing Freedom of Information Act lawsuit filed by the nonprofit against ICE and DHS.

"The numbers reported to EFF amount to a dragnet domestic surveillance program by Trump's Homeland Security Department,” says US senator Ron Wyden of Oregon.

ICE did not respond to questions about why there’s been such a huge increase compared to prior years, but Mackey speculates that it may be because there is virtually no downside to filing them.

“The worst thing that will happen to ICE is that the service that receives it says it's not going to respond,” says Mackey. If that happens, ICE can just take the company to court to force it to comply, as it recently did with Google, or withdraw it and try to get the information it’s after by issuing a grand jury subpoena, as the Intercept reported it recently did with Reddit.

“Congress desperately needs to pass my bipartisan legislation to drastically curb federal subpoenas, including consequences for officials who abuse surveillance authorities,” says Wyden, referencing his proposed Subpoena Abuse Prevention Act. “Next year, I plan to use the Senate Finance Committee gavel to get to the bottom of HSI's unprecedented misuse of customs law to target Trump's enemies.”

#610. Moderna raises 2026 outlook as CEO says newer vaccines are gaining traction

Moderna on Friday smashed third-quarter revenue and earnings estimates and hiked its outlook for full-year sales growth, as some of its newer products gain momentum and Covid vaccine revenue outperforms.

Shares of Moderna rose roughly 3% in premarket trading on Friday.

The biotech company now expects 2026 revenue growth of 15% to 25%, up from its previous guidance of up to 10%.

"The new product and the diversification of geography is what has led to such a strong Q3," Moderna CEO Stéphane Bancel said in an interview ahead of the results.

For example, he said the company is seeing the "full-year impact" of its next-generation mRNA Covid vaccine, which won U.S. approval in May 2025. Bancel noted that Covid is a seasonal business and vaccine contracts are typically established in the first quarter, so Moderna's shot only had a "small launch" to start with last year.

Moderna's new stand-alone flu shot, which was approved in the U.S. in August, will similarly have a "limited launch" in the country in 2026 given the timing, he said.

In a separate interview on CNBC's "Squawk Box," Bancel said Moderna's geographic expansion in Canada, the U.K. and Asia helped boost results. The company's work to lower costs also resulted in a profit in the quarter, he added.

"It's the sales that are stronger and the costs that are also lower," Bancel said.

Here's what Moderna reported for the third quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Moderna's third-quarter revenue jumped 29% to $1.31 billion, boosted by higher Covid vaccine sales in international markets and helped by deliveries under long-term government partnerships. Revenue was $780 million in the U.S. and $533 million in international markets.

The company posted a net income of $162 million, or 38 cents per share, for the third quarter. That compares with a net loss of $200 million, or a loss of 51 cents per share, reported for the year-ago period.

The results come weeks after Moderna and Merck unveiled promising late-stage data on their experimental personalized cancer vaccine in combination with immunotherapy Keytruda in certain melanoma patients. Despite the positive results, investors battered Moderna's stock after part of the data was leaked on social media just days before trial investigators were set to present it at a European medical conference.

Bancel called it a "surprising reaction," but said that the companies also saw a positive response from others on Wall Street, including long-term investors and analysts who examined the full data in depth. He added that the doctors in the field "were so impressed with the data," and many expressed that they hope to use it in their day-to-day practice.

Moderna's stock has skyrocketed more than 700% this year, largely on optimism about the potential for the shot.

#607. Scientists finally discover why we learn to love bitter coffee

A first taste of black coffee can be surprisingly unpleasant. Its sharp bitterness is the kind of flavor our brains are naturally inclined to avoid. Yet millions of people eventually come to enjoy that same taste, often looking forward to their morning cup. What changes?

Scientists at UC San Francisco may have uncovered an important part of the answer. Their research reveals a little-understood dopamine pathway linking the digestive system and the brain, helping explain how we develop a liking for certain flavors through repeated experience.

Dopamine is a chemical messenger involved in motivation, reward, and learning. Researchers have long known that it helps the brain recognize experiences worth repeating. However, most studies have examined rapid dopamine signals that appear within seconds of encountering something rewarding, such as the sight of a favorite meal.

The new research, published November 4 in Nature, examines a different system. This slower pathway responds to information coming from inside the body, allowing the brain to learn about the benefits of food after it has been consumed.

"We've shown that this slow dopamine system is what drives us to learn to like certain foods over time, and we can see where and how that happens," said Zachary Knight, PhD, professor of Physiology and senior author of the study.

The findings offer a possible explanation for why an initially unpleasant flavor can gradually become enjoyable. Once the brain connects the taste of coffee with caffeine's stimulating effects, Knight explained, "that coffee starts to taste a lot better."

How the Gut Sends Reward Signals to the Brain

Scientists have spent decades investigating dopamine and its influence on behavior. Its familiar, rapidly acting signals help us respond to opportunities for reward. But dopamine also operates on a slower timescale, through a system that has received far less attention.

According to Knight, this slower process largely unfolds outside our conscious awareness.

When food enters the digestive tract, specialized sensors detect nutrients, including sugar and fat. Information about those nutrients travels toward the brain, where it can produce a dopamine response that develops gradually and continues for several minutes.

To investigate this process, Knight worked with James Grove, PhD, who was a graduate student at the time. They introduced nutrients directly into the stomachs of mice while monitoring dopamine activity throughout their brains.

The researchers used either sugar water or a liquid resembling a milkshake. Delivering these substances directly into the stomach allowed them to separate the effects of nutrients from the experience of tasting food.

The results revealed a powerful, prolonged dopamine response in a specific brain region called the anterior basolateral amygdala, or BLA.

The amygdala is involved in emotional processing and learning, including forming associations between experiences and their consequences. Scientists already knew that dopamine was released in this particular region, but its purpose had remained unclear.

"We could see that this dopamine is part of a different system and begin to understand its role," he said.

Scientists Find a Brain Circuit That Changes Food Preferences

Finding the dopamine signal was only the beginning. The researchers next wanted to determine whether it actually influenced how animals learned to prefer different flavors.

They first interrupted the newly identified pathway in mice. The animals continued eating normally, indicating that the disruption did not prevent them from consuming food. However, they lost the ability to learn that an unfamiliar flavor was associated with receiving calories.

The team then conducted the opposite experiment.

After mice tasted a flavor containing no calories, the researchers artificially activated dopamine fibers in the BLA. Remarkably, the animals developed a preference for that flavor even though it provided no nutritional reward.

In effect, stimulating the pathway reproduced the learning signal that nutrients would ordinarily generate.

Together, the experiments showed that this dopamine system plays a critical role in teaching the brain which flavors predict rewarding nutritional effects.

A Surprising Discovery in the Left Side of the Brain

The researchers also looked for evidence that a comparable process occurs in humans. Brain imaging studies conducted by Knight's collaborators revealed a strikingly similar pattern.

Dana Small, PhD, a psychologist at Yale University, revisited data from an earlier positron emission tomography (PET) study involving 13 men. Participants had consumed either a milkshake or a drink without calories.

Her analysis found that dopamine release following nutrient delivery was especially concentrated in the left side of the BLA.

A separate study led by Marc Tittgemeyer, PhD, and his colleagues provided additional evidence. Using functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI), which measures changes in brain activity associated with blood flow, the researchers examined 38 adults after they consumed glucose, a simple sugar.

They observed increased activity in the same brain region.

One of the most unexpected findings was that the response was not evenly distributed between the brain's two hemispheres.

In both mice and humans, nutrients produced a considerably stronger dopamine response in the left BLA than in the corresponding region on the right.

That imbalance surprised Knight because brain networks responsible for essential functions, such as feeding, are generally expected to work in a relatively symmetrical fashion.

Why the left side plays such a prominent role remains unanswered. Knight's laboratory is now investigating what causes this unusual difference.

Could This Discovery Help Scientists Change Food Cravings?

Identifying the pathway provides researchers with a clearer picture of how the digestive system influences the brain's learned responses to food. It also raises an intriguing possibility: Could scientists eventually use this knowledge to change which foods people find appealing?

One area of interest involves GLP-1 medications, a class of drugs that mimic a hormone naturally released by the gut. These medications can substantially alter appetite and eating behavior.

Knight's team did not investigate GLP-1 drugs in the new study, so it remains unknown whether they influence the dopamine pathway the researchers identified.

Still, Knight suggests that one possible explanation for some of their effects could involve reducing the dopamine signals generated when nutrients reach the digestive system. If that happens, foods might gradually become less rewarding because the brain would receive weaker signals reinforcing their flavors.

For now, this remains a hypothesis rather than a demonstrated effect of the medications.

The broader implications extend beyond hunger and fullness. Many approaches to changing eating behavior focus on reducing appetite or increasing feelings of satisfaction after a meal. The newly mapped pathway suggests that the brain's process of learning food preferences could eventually become another target.

By influencing how the brain connects flavors with their consequences, future treatments might someday help people develop different preferences rather than simply making them feel less hungry.

"By understanding the steps between nutrient detection in the gut and learning in the brain, it might be possible to fine-tune them to subtly change our preferences for different foods," Knight said.

The discovery offers a new perspective on why our tastes can change over time. From learning to enjoy bitter coffee to developing a preference for calorie-rich foods, what we love to eat may depend partly on a quiet conversation between the gut and brain that continues long after the first bite.

The research was supported by the National Institutes of Health (grants R01-DK106399, R01-DK138127, R01-NS116626, and R01-DK145100 and F31-NS120468).

#606. I Let Pangram Loose on My Gmail Inbox. Here’s How Much AI It Found

AI-generated content is everywhere now: From restaurant menus and student essays to full-length movies and social media posts, AI is churning out huge amounts of text, images, audio, and video. In some digital spaces, it's overwhelming human-made content.

But what about your inbox? Just how many of your friends, family members, clients, and colleagues are using AI to compose their emails? Is that store newsletter you just signed up for written by a real person or a bot? It's not always easy to tell.

The Pangram AI text and image detector tool has now extended its reach into your Gmail inbox. The company makes a browser extension that sits in Gmail and runs incoming messages through its algorithms to try to detect AI writing.

You will need at least the $20-per-month individual plan on Pangram to use the Gmail integration—it's not available on the free tier. I tested it to see how accurate it appears to be based on my recent Gmail conversations.

You can let Pangram loose in your Gmail inbox by heading to the Gmail integration page and clicking Connect your Gmail. You can also click the Gmail Integration entry on the left-hand navigation pane in your main Pangram dashboard on the web. You'll need to go through the standard authorization screens to confirm you want to make the connection.

Pangram says the contents of your emails aren't stored after being assessed and won't be used to train models designed to look for AI text. There's still a level of trust here, but you can quickly remove the Pangram plug-in from your Gmail at any point.

After you connect your Gmail account, Pangram gets to work. It'll scan your 100 most recent email threads and then any new messages from then on—you can't get it to check all of the emails you've ever received. As far as I can tell, email scanning doesn't count against your scan credit limit for the month, so you're covered no matter how busy your inbox is.

After scanning, the Gmail Integration part of your Pangram dashboard changes. You can see at a glance how many of your emails are suspected to be AI, how many are most probably human, and how many have been flagged as a mix between the two. Inside Gmail, these three categories can be applied as labels, though this is optional. (If you decide to remove Pangram from your inbox later, any labels it's created will stick around, but you can delete these manually.)

You can also choose to send incoming AI-written emails straight to the archive or to spam if you want, which is handy for keeping AI content away from your eyeballs. You can also choose to exclude specific domains, Gmail labels, subject keywords, and senders from Pangram's scans, if you don't want emails from your boss or mom scrutinized.

So the moment of truth: Out of the 108 threads that Pangram scanned, eight of them were labeled as AI, 37 got the human label, and the remainder were marked as mixed. It's a little dispiriting to discover that way more than half of my incoming messages are probably AI-written, with press releases the most common culprits.

In terms of accuracy, none of the emails I know to have been written by flesh-and-blood people were flagged as fully or partly AI. Everything from friends and family and colleagues I know passed the human test, so for this small sample size at least, Pangram's detection algorithms seem to be working.

I sent a couple of emails to myself from different accounts to see what Pangram would make of them. One was written with Microsoft Copilot, which the detector correctly flagged as AI. The other was a section of this article, written with my own brain and keyboard taps, and again Pangram got it right—it was marked with the Human label.

There does seem to be a minimum email length, and if an email’s word count falls below that threshold, Pangram won't make a decision either way, which is understandable. (AI is harder to detect with a smaller sample size). Only the first email in each thread is scanned (and optionally labeled), so if your correspondent switches to AI halfway through a conversation, it wouldn't get spotted.

I like the way the scanner stays in the background and just gets on with its job, and I can see myself using it long-term. It really does affect your mindset if you know a message has been entirely put together using AI. Of course, Gmail being Gmail, you can quickly fire off an AI response as well, should you want to.

#603. These Robotic Arms Are Powered by Algae. Watch How They Move

What if a robot could be squishy? Soft, flexible robots could be useful for a wide variety of applications, including artificial muscles and wearable devices—or really any application where a robot must be especially delicate.

This is what has fueled development of “soft actuators” that can flex like a bicep and have a fine-tuned sense of touch as in the human hand. Like your stereotypical robot, soft actuators convert energy—such as electricity, heat, or light—into mechanical motion, but they look nothing like the metal and wire constructs that the word “robot” most often evokes.

One variety of these soft robots, known as bioactuators, utilizes the functions of living cells to generate motion. These biological machines have attracted particular attention in recent years, and now a research team at Delft University of Technology in the Netherlands has developed a soft robotic arm colonized by algae on its surface, and when the microbes photosynthesize, the arm moves.

“We didn’t start with a specific application. Our goal has always been to bring the research of living materials to new levels,” Marie-Eve Aubin-Tam, who led the research, explained in a press release. “We wanted to explore what becomes possible when living cells are integrated into functional materials.”

The tiny robot arms—about three-quarters of an inch long and about as thick as a credit card—are constructed of two layers of hydrogel that the algal cells live on. One of these layers responds to changes in pH, which are mediated by the metabolism of the algae. When light strikes the algae, they begin to photosynthesize. By absorbing carbon dioxide and fixing nitrogen in this process, the algae make the local pH more basic, making the responsive hydrogel swell. Conversely, when it becomes dark, the algae release carbon dioxide through respiration, returning the pH to the acidic side and the hydrogel shrinks.

The difference in the volumes of the two hydrogel layers controls the bending. According to the researchers, the pH-responsive hydrogel layer changed significantly with the algae’s activity. In acidic conditions, such as between pH 3 and 7, the layer was approximately 37 percent of its original weight, but it had quadruple its original weight at pH 11.

Other researchers have constructed soft robots that respond to pH, but typically the chemistry of all the surrounding liquid has to be altered. This is not the case for the algal bots. The photosynthetic activity has an effect that’s much more local, meaning it would be better suited to applications where minimal environmental impact is essential.

These aren’t, however, the speediest of robots. The chemical shift begins quickly, about 20 seconds after light is applied and about 10 seconds after it becomes dark, but it takes several hours for the hydrogel to bend significantly.

After their first proof of concept, the research team constructed several more algal devices. One was a four-fingered gripper that closes when exposed to light. It successfully grasped and lifted a hollow metal sphere, releasing it when the light was turned off. Another hook-shaped gripper could pick up and put down a rubber ring. These items were extremely light, weighing far less than a small paperclip, but the arms were able to grasp them with great dexterity.

These soft robots also require no external energy source other than light and no complex mechanical parts. “Microalgae are plantlike organisms that are largely self-sustaining,” Aubin-Tam said. This “gives the actuator potential for long-term operation with minimal external input. In theory, it wouldn’t require a lot of energy to run a system based on this technology.”

In the long term, the research team hopes this will contribute to the development of more autonomous, environment-responsive soft robots that could be used as automated agricultural equipment or for fluid control in lab-on-a-chip devices or really as part of any underwater robotics system.

Since the algae do not need to be genetically modified, there are none of the biosafety concerns often associated with microbe-based technologies. If anything, these are exceptionally environmentally friendly machines, since these organisms can absorb carbon dioxide from the atmosphere while deriving energy from sunlight.

This story originally appeared on WIRED Japan and has been translated from Japanese.

#602. Alzheimer’s may have early warning signs outside the brain

Alzheimer's disease is best known for its effects on memory and thinking, but new research suggests that some symptoms may develop in a surprising place: outside the brain.

Scientists at the University of Central Florida (UCF) have discovered that genetic mutations associated with Alzheimer's can interfere with the nerves that control muscle movement, even without involvement from the brain or spinal cord. The findings could help explain why some people with Alzheimer's experience changes in walking, balance, or coordination long before noticeable memory problems emerge.

The discovery raises the possibility that movement difficulties could provide earlier warning signs of the disease and that treatments focused exclusively on the brain may overlook important problems elsewhere in the body.

The research was led by James Hickman, a professor at UCF's Nanoscience Technology Center, and Research Professor Xiufang "Nadine" Guo, working alongside scientists at the healthcare technology company Hesperos. Funded by the National Institutes of Health's National Institute on Aging, the study used laboratory systems built from human cells to investigate how inherited Alzheimer's mutations affect movement. The findings were published in Alzheimer's & Dementia: The Journal of the Alzheimer's Association.

"Motor deficits may be an earlier indication [of Alzheimer's]," Guo says. "If we can detect those changes and intervene earlier, that could help delay the onset of central nervous system symptoms."

Why Alzheimer's Symptoms May Appear Before Memory Loss

Alzheimer's is generally associated with progressive memory loss, confusion, and declining cognitive abilities. However, doctors have also observed that some patients develop physical symptoms, including changes in balance, coordination, and gait (manner of walking), years before clear cognitive difficulties become apparent.

These observations have raised an important question: Are such movement problems simply a consequence of damage occurring in the brain, or could Alzheimer's also interfere directly with nerves elsewhere in the body?

To investigate, the UCF team focused on familial Alzheimer's, a rare inherited form of the disease that typically develops earlier in life (from 40 to 65 years of age) than the more common form.

Their experiments revealed that motor neurons carrying mutations linked to familial Alzheimer's could disrupt the connections responsible for controlling muscles, even when the brain and spinal cord were completely absent from the experimental system.

Motor neurons are specialized nerve cells that transmit signals to muscles, telling them when to contract and produce movement. The new findings suggest that Alzheimer's mutations can interfere with this process directly, rather than affecting movement only through damage to the brain.

"This is the first time it's been demonstrated that deficits in the peripheral nervous system can arise directly from these mutations," Hickman says. "It means drugs that target the brain may not fix problems in the rest of the body."

The peripheral nervous system includes the nerves located outside the brain and spinal cord. These nerves carry signals between the central nervous system and the rest of the body, helping control movement, sensation, and other essential functions.

Guo also points out that preserving the ability to move could have benefits beyond physical health. Regular physical activity is associated with better cognitive well-being, so protecting motor function may also help support brain health.

How a Miniature Human Cell System Revealed the Discovery

To examine the relationship between Alzheimer's mutations and muscle movement, the scientists used an innovative laboratory platform known as "human-on-a-chip" technology.

Developed and manufactured through Hesperos, a company co-founded by Hickman, these miniature systems bring different types of human cells together under controlled conditions. They allow researchers to recreate specific biological functions and observe how diseases interfere with them.

Unlike conventional laboratory experiments that examine isolated cells, these systems can reproduce important interactions between different tissues. They also provide an alternative to animal models, which do not always accurately reflect how human diseases behave.

For this study, the team created a miniature model of the neuromuscular junction, the connection where a motor neuron communicates with a muscle cell.

The model was deliberately designed without a brain or spinal cord. This was a crucial part of the experiment because it allowed the researchers to investigate whether Alzheimer's-related changes could cause movement problems independently of the central nervous system.

The scientists combined healthy muscle cells with motor neurons derived from stem cells that carried genetic mutations associated with familial Alzheimer's disease. Stem cells are cells that can develop into specialized types of cells, making them valuable tools for studying human diseases in the laboratory.

The researchers then examined how effectively the altered nerve cells communicated with otherwise healthy muscle tissue.

The results showed that the Alzheimer's-associated mutations could interfere with normal nerve-to-muscle function. This suggests that at least some movement difficulties linked to the disease may arise directly in the peripheral nervous system rather than being caused entirely by degeneration in the brain.

Why Damaged Nerve Connections Can Affect Walking and Balance

Every voluntary movement depends on successful communication between the nervous system and the muscles.

At the neuromuscular junction, an electrical signal traveling along a motor neuron triggers chemical communication that causes a muscle to contract. This process makes everyday activities possible, from walking and maintaining posture to lifting objects and performing precise movements.

When the connection becomes impaired, muscles may respond less effectively to nerve signals. Depending on the severity and location of the problem, this can contribute to weakness, reduced coordination, and difficulty sustaining physical activity.

To evaluate these effects, the research team measured how consistently nerve signals produced muscle contractions and how long the muscles could continue contracting before becoming fatigued.

These measurements provided a direct assessment of how well the nerve and muscle cells worked together. They also resemble aspects of the functional testing physicians use when examining patients with movement disorders.

"You can't move unless the motor circuit works," Hickman says. "When a doctor taps your knee to check your reflex, they're testing that exact connection."

By demonstrating impaired nerve-to-muscle communication in a system containing no brain or spinal cord, the study provides evidence that mutations linked to familial Alzheimer's can produce effects beyond the central nervous system.

However, the experiments do not establish that these changes necessarily occur before memory loss in patients. Further research will be needed to determine how closely the laboratory findings reflect the progression of Alzheimer's in people, particularly those with the more common forms of the disease.

Could This Discovery Lead to Earlier Alzheimer's Detection?

The findings suggest a potentially important new direction for Alzheimer's research: looking beyond memory and other cognitive symptoms when investigating the disease.

If future studies confirm that changes in motor function are detectable early in certain patients, tests of walking, coordination, or nerve-to-muscle activity might eventually help identify disease-related changes before more obvious neurological symptoms develop.

The results also raise questions about treatment. Many Alzheimer's therapies are designed to address processes occurring in the brain, but medications that target the central nervous system may not necessarily correct abnormalities affecting peripheral nerves and muscles.

Understanding these separate effects could help researchers investigate treatments that protect physical function alongside cognitive health.

Human-on-a-Chip Technology Could Transform Disease Research

Beyond the implications for Alzheimer's, the researchers see considerable potential in using human-on-a-chip systems to investigate other diseases and evaluate possible treatments.

Because these platforms contain human cells and reproduce measurable biological activities, they can help scientists observe disease-related changes in conditions that more closely resemble human physiology than some conventional experimental models.

This approach may be particularly useful in drug development, where researchers need to understand not only how a potential treatment affects individual cells but also how it influences interactions between different tissues.

Such systems can also uncover biological effects that may be difficult to detect in animal experiments, potentially helping researchers identify promising therapies and recognize limitations earlier in development.

For Hickman, the progress represents the culmination of three decades of work devoted to understanding disease through more realistic laboratory models.

"These systems let us study disease in a way that's closer to what actually happens in the human body, and that's what we need to develop better treatments," he says.

Materials provided by University of Central Florida. Original written by Emily Dougherty. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#599. Audioengine Color of the Year Kit Review: Bright Look, Good Sound

The artist Wassily Kandinsky said, “Color is a power that directly influences the soul.” But in the realm of home stereo gear, the color wheel gets minimal airtime, with equipment typically available in scant options: silver-faced classics, wood-grain boxes, all manner of modern reflective piano black, and not a whole lot else.

Not so with Audioengine, which has painted a neat little world with its annual Color of the Year kit: a suite of home audio components, including laptop speakers, a subwoofer, a turntable, and speaker stands. Each year, the brand drops a new edition of the series in a different color; in the launch year of 2024, a dusky matte blue; in 2025, a subtle metallic green; and now in 2026, a lustrous and smooth egg-yolk yellow.

Having a home audio system in such an unusual color palette is, to me, irresistibly joyful; whenever I look at these stereo components, I can’t help but smile. And the tech holds up, too. After months of testing the suite, I was colored impressed. Straight out of the box, it's a great option for music lovers who value the look and sound of their home stereo setup.

The anchoring component of the Color of the Year suite are the A2+ Gen 3 Wireless Speakers. At 6 inches tall by 4 inches wide, they’re sized like laptop speakers—and get high marks for that purpose as WIRED’s top pick for computer speakers—but that’s really just the start of what they can do. Alone, the speakers pack enough power for a small home theater. They're also easy to integrate into a bigger system, thanks to their included stereo mini-jack inputs, USB inputs, and both stereo RCA inputs and outputs. To make the sound quality more targeted, place the A2+ speakers atop the included DS1M Desktop Stands, and angle them 15 degrees up toward your ears.

This year, Audioengine expanded its Color of the Year suite to include a color-matched Bluetooth turntable in collaboration with Crosley. Called the C6, it's a manual two-speed machine with a nice, heavy steel platter and medium-density fiberboard (MDF) plinth. It's Bluetooth-enabled and features a built-in preamp that lets you directly connect your A2+ speakers—wired or wireless—without a need for additional components.

My favorite part of the Color of the Year suite is the S6 Powered Subwoofer. At 10 inches by 8.7 inches, the compact, front-firing form factor lets you snuggle it into various spaces that wouldn't work for a larger, bottom-firing subwoofer. This is a class D sub with a frequency response down to 33 Hz and an adjustable crossover down to 40 Hz that helps your system sound complete, lifting the bass and rounding out the listening experience.

The ease of setup is one of the major draws for the Audioengine suite. Plug in the A2+ speakers, link them together using the provided stereo cables, and turn them on. You can connect the speakers via Bluetooth, or plug in other components via the stereo inputs. It’s as basic as home hi-fi gets.

Linking the Crosley C6 turntable using Bluetooth is similarly seamless. The A2+s have an intuitive Bluetooth pairing mode that starts up when you turn on the speakers, indicated by a flashing Pair button on the back. From there, turn on the turntable, make sure it’s in the “line” position (by using the turntable’s built-in preamp), and the C6 instantly searches for the A2+s. Once the two units connect, the flashing button on the back of the speakers will turn blue, as will the Bluetooth indicator on the turntable.

Connecting the subwoofer was likewise a cinch: Connect an RCA cable to the back of the A2+s in the RCA output, then run that to the audio input on the back of the S6 subwoofer. Once you connect, you’ll want to tweak the sound and presence of the subwoofer, and once you’re happy with the dial-in, the subwoofers will go up and down in volume, in lockstep with the A2+ speakers.

Each Audioengine product also came with a QR code that launched a step-by-step setup process, which I found especially helpful for dialing in the sound for the woofer. (The Audioengine YouTube channel is also a wealth of information.)

After several colorful, music-filled months of testing the Audioengine kit, I found the system to be versatile, though not without its limitations or opportunities for upgrades and tweaks. Right out of the box, the speakers, subwoofer, and turntable looked fantastic and sounded pretty nice. The A2+ speakers were shockingly nuanced and detailed, considering their small size, with Primus and Frank Zappa records showcasing slashing guitars and Paul Chambers and Bill Evans jazz albums providing a sophisticated soundstage. These speakers also worked great for home gaming and laptop use. The subwoofer did wonders when connected to the A2+ for elevating the home-theater experience. The growling lion and car-chasing monkeys in 1995's Jumanji were vivid and lifelike.

Being able to transition from home stereo to computer to home theater is versatile and fun. It means there’s always a way to incorporate these products into your home.

If there’s a weak link in the Color of the Year kit, it’s the turntable—but even that is pretty strong. The aluminum tonearm leaves something to be desired if you’re accustomed to higher-end units, but the C6 worked well for me and will suit the needs of most casual listeners. There are several similarly priced Bluetooth-enabled turntables on the market, so out of curiosity I swapped in a Sony PS-LX3BT and noticed no significant difference in terms of sound. What those other turntables lack is the aesthetic unity and the satisfaction of having your entire system kitted out in a unique colorway.

Mostly, I just love how this canary-yellow gear looks together. It offers equal parts interior-design pleasure and home audio performance. The price is incredibly competitive across the entry-level home stereo space, and the ease of use and setup feels intuitive and user-friendly for novices. If color influences the soul, then the union of color and music stands to be a source of deep satisfaction—and I’ve found it here.

(If you don't want the entire suite, you can buy the components à la carte without a penalty—there's no price break for the bundled suite.)
