#596. Authors Can Defend Against AI Allegations With ‘Paper Trail’ Tools. Is It Enough?

Andrea Bartz is a prolific novelist with an impressive résumé. Since 2019, she has published five thrillers, become a New York Times bestselling author, had her work optioned by the likes of Netflix and Hulu, and picked up an endorsement from Reese Witherspoon’s influential book club.

She also has a legal claim to fame: Bartz was the lead plaintiff in a class-action suit against Anthropic that alleged the company had downloaded millions of copyrighted books from pirate sites to use as training data for its models. Anthropic last year agreed to pay $1.5 billion in the largest copyright settlement in US history.

But while Bartz and many other writers were on guard against this plunder of their work, another AI threat was brewing. What happens when authors are accused of relying on artificial intelligence themselves?

This past March, the publishing industry found out: Following widespread speculation on social media that a horror novel titled Shy Girl was largely AI-generated, and a Times article about those claims, Hachette Book Group canceled an upcoming US publication run for the novel and discontinued it in the UK, where it had already been released. According to Pangram, a robust but by no means infallible AI detection tool, some 78 percent of the text was AI-generated.

Mia Ballard, the author of Shy Girl, has denied allegations that she used AI to write the novel, claiming that an editor was responsible for AI-assisted changes to a previously self-published version of the book. She has also said that she is pursuing legal action. Since that scandal broke, several more fiction authors have run afoul of AI suspicions, one after winning a short story prize, another after publishers bid into the millions for his crime novel. Both have denied these allegations as well.

But Bartz notes that once the accusations start, the damage is done. As the Shy Girl scandal erupted, she says, “I kind of immediately felt like this was going to lead to a potential witch hunt.” The unavoidable emergence of AI in publishing, she explains, will “have the unintended effect of leading anyone to be able to accuse anyone of using AI,” and at the moment, authors have little recourse in defending themselves against these charges. “There’s no agreed upon standard,” Bartz says, in proving human authorship.

The developers of a host of new software tools hope to change that. Platforms including Workings, Blindproof, Wintertext, Human Ink, and ObraVera promise the means to track and document an author’s writing process, in some cases encrypting and fingerprinting the written work or typing rhythms with unique codes, in order to produce a record of authorship (whether entirely human or AI-assisted). The idea is not just to create a paper trail that writers can point to if someone questions whether they used an LLM but to demonstrate provenance when, for example, text is scraped into AI training sets without attribution or other identifying metadata.

Mary Rasenberger, CEO of the Authors Guild, a professional organization focused on free expression and copyright protection, says that the guild can’t recommend any one of these tools over another but wants writers to know they exist as something of a security net. (Many of the companies have approached the Authors Guild to pitch their services, according to Umair Kazi, the guild’s director of policy and advocacy.)

At the same time, Rasenberger tells WIRED, it’s “insulting” and a “burden” to have to jump through this hoop, and “we don't feel it should be required” for authors to preserve receipts.

“If what you're selling are your words and your voice and your imagination, your creativity, using AI doesn't make sense,” Rasenberger says, noting that authors’ pride in their own work means AI slop is far less common in publishing than one might think. “I've talked to editors, particularly editors of literary nonfiction and fiction,” Rasenberger says, “and some of them will say, ‘I never see AI-generated submissions, I'd know it if I did.’”

Rasenberger worries that authors are up against a climate of distrust—they should “refrain from accusations of other authors because it just does not support a healthy literary community,” she says—and overconfidence in AI detection tools, which can be fooled and have been known to produce false positives. While Pangram claims an “industry-leading” false positive rate of 0.01 percent when used on creative writing, Rasenberger said the Authors Guild has “heard of lots of instances where people have put their own human-authored work in Pangram and gotten a result back saying it was AI-generated.” There are a variety of factors, she adds, that can “make an AI detector think something's AI-generated when it's not.”

By the same token, a process-tracking app isn’t foolproof; the AI arms race means there’s an ongoing cycle of vulnerabilities and fixes on both sides.

“We offer a product that [tracks writing] in Google Docs, and many users find it helpful,” says Max Spero, cofounder and CEO of Pangram. “With that said, ‘paper trail’ tools are not a perfect catch-all. For example, someone could hand-type AI text, or use an auto-typer.” Spero points out that the messiness of writing itself creates further problems. “The average writer doesn't just sit down and type out a book,” he says. “They work with multiple files, send drafts back and forth, use voice notes while on the go. Not all of this gets captured in writing process tools.”

The tools, meanwhile, may be more geared toward copyright concerns than confirmation of human or AI authorship. Brady Ridgway, technical founder of ObraVera—which can cryptographically “fingerprint” patterns in writing as well as audio voice recordings—says his platform is designed to trace reuse of material you have stored in their database as a registered human user. With a time stamp and a crypto hash, he explains, if the content “crops up later somewhere it's not supposed to be, or somebody else claims it as theirs, we can search the database and say, ‘These words were created at this time, by this person.’”

That may demonstrate provenance and ownership, Ridgway says, yet it’s up to the registered individual to declare whether they used AI or not in the creation of the text. He refers to the kerfuffle in May over the writer who was accused of winning a Commonwealth Short Story Prize with an AI-generated submission. “Everybody seems to agree that he's the person who submitted the short story,” Ridgway says. “That's all that the software would do. It would say that somebody on this day made a declaration that it was human only or not AI-assisted.” It can’t confirm whether that’s a true statement or not.

Some argue that trying to institutionalize vetting systems for human authorship is simply impractical—and may soon be a moot point anyway. Jane Friedman, an author and publishing business expert, recently argued in her industry newsletter The Bottom Line, that the era of AI scandals is likely to be short-lived, given the improvements of Pangram and similar detectors. Moreover, she wrote, authors have now seen what may happen if they attempt to pass off AI-generated writing as their own.

Friedman tells WIRED that agents and publishers are unlikely to implement any method of verifying human authorship that “costs extra money or extra time,” not least because it would be impossible to get them to commit to a shared standard. She predicts that “it will be like herding cats to get agreement on this, because publishing is slow to adopt new technologies, especially new technologies developed by industry outsiders.”

Even if diverse publishers with highly variable needs and resources were to coalesce around some theoretical form of authentication, hurdles remain. Friedman alludes to the logistical problems of integrating this benchmark into existing production models, not to mention the lingering problem of “faked or manipulated” certification. “If any of this costs extra money or extra time, forget it,” she concludes. And while authors may be willing to pay for peace of mind, Friedman says, they won’t be able to trust that agents and publishers will accept proof of authorship from any given app.

In the absence of a clear solution, writers are left to fend for themselves. Bartz says that she’s alarmed by how zealous readers are responding to the prospect of being deceived by AI. “I have heard of people getting review-bombed on Goodreads because some stranger felt that the book sounded like AI to them, and everyone else piled on,” she says. “I've seen readers saying, ‘I'm not gonna read anything published after 2022, because then I can be sure that it doesn't have generative AI in it.’ That's a great way to tank an entire industry all at once.”

It so happens that Bartz’s forthcoming 2027 book, Delete Your Account, tackles generative artificial intelligence head-on. Her protagonist is a blocked writer who opts for an unfortunate shortcut, prompting an AI chatbot to imitate her favorite thriller—only to find herself trapped in that original story, facing certain death.

Bartz wonders if the very choice to satirize AI has put her in a precarious spot. “I've written portions that are very much meant to sound like LLMs,” she says. “Transcripts I made up, and annoying, AI-sounding marketing materials from this made-up AI company. And I don't know—if any of that comes back as AI-written, what will I do?”

#595. Tesla to wind down Cybertruck production in Texas next year to ramp Cybercab, Musk says

Tesla CEO Elon Musk on Friday said the electric vehicle maker will "be winding down" production of the Cybertruck at its Texas gigafactory around the middle of next year.

Musk, who hailed the Cybertruck as the future of the pickup truck segment, said in a post on X early Friday morning that the end of production at the massive facility would make room for manufacturing of its Cybercab robotaxi.

The Cybertruck is exclusively produced at the company's plant in Texas alongside the Tesla Model Y and two-seat Cybercab, which Musk has touted as a crucial product for the automaker moving forward.

Musk did not say whether the company would produce the Cybertruck at a different facility, but he later responded to a user on X who called the update an "end of an era" and said the Cybertruck was the "best vehicle Tesla has ever made."

"True, but the Cybertruck fleet will be around for a long time," Musk said.

Tesla began Cybertruck deliveries in late 2023. The company did not immediately respond to a request for comment from CNBC.

The Cybertruck and its polarizing design gained a niche following, but it did not achieve mass-market sales, despite Musk's pre-launch prediction that annual sales would eventually total 250,000.

"Trucks have been the same for a very long time," Musk said when the truck was unveiled in 2019. "Like 100 years. Trucks have been basically the same. We want to try something different."

While Tesla does not break out its sales by model or region, Cox Automotive estimated sales of the Cybertruck last year at 20,237 units in the U.S., down 48% from 38,965 in 2024.

One big buyer of the Cybertruck last year was Musk's aerospace and tech conglomerate, SpaceX, which spent $131 million buying up the angular steel trucks at Tesla's suggested retail price, according to a SpaceX financial filing.

The distinct look of the vehicle made some owners a target of rude gestures and more serious road rage, one owner told CNBC last May, after Musk became the biggest funder of Donald Trump's presidential campaign and led the so-called Department of Government Efficiency, an effort to slash the federal workforce and regulations.

Wired wrote in an October 2025 feature story, "There is likely no object that feels more emblematic of US president Donald Trump's return to the White House than the Tesla Cybertruck." The magazine interviewed owners who described being spit on and sworn at due to their choice of vehicle.

The Cybertruck's specifications also fell short of those Tesla advertised early on, and in some cases fell short of those offered by leading fuel-burning pickups. There have also been many quality and production problems associated with the vehicles.

The 2024 Tesla Cybertruck was the subject of 11 voluntary recalls, according to the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration website. Issues ranged from a "trapped" acceleration pedal risk that could cause unintended acceleration, to software defects and an inverter fault that could result in sudden loss of drive power.

#592. Small retailers brace for 'huge hit' this holiday season as shipping costs spike

When Jenni Shields began her small business eight years ago selling handmade hair accessories, she paid $2.93 to ship an order, or close to $4 in today's dollars when adjusted for inflation.

Last year, Shields said, an average shipping label for her products cost around $4.50. But when she took a typical batch of orders to her local U.S. Postal Service office last month in Kansas City, Kansas, she was shocked to see that it was an average of nearly $6 per package.

Shields is just one of the small- and medium-sized retailers who are struggling to keep up with rising shipping costs as the price of fuel continues to trend upward.

Small business owners such as Shields are facing a difficult holiday season. Customer demand is strong, but some retailers said their profits will be dented — if not eaten up entirely — by higher shipping and other costs.

Shields said she charges her customers $6 in shipping fees, which includes roughly $1.50 to cover her packaging supplies. The dramatic upswing this year means she's no longer breaking even on her orders and instead eating the added costs, she said.

"I am losing money at this point, and I've really taken a huge hit this year," said Shields, the owner of Evie Pearl Handmade, which sells bows, quilted headbands and other accessories. "I feel like it's just been this slow increase up [in shipping costs], and I feel horrible."

The surge in fuel prices since the U.S. and Israel attacked Iran in February has driven up the cost of shipping by sea, air, rail and road.

That means higher costs across the board, including for shipping giant Amazon, which reported an 11% increase in shipping expenses in the third quarter compared with last year, to individual retailers.

Globally, air cargo is going for about $3.37 per kilogram, up 43% from a similar period a year ago, according to transportation marketplace Freightos. Judah Levine, Freightos' head of research, said in addition to higher fuel, demand for fast shipping of hardware used to build data centers is helping to boost air freight rates, even weeks before peak last-minute holiday packages take to the skies.

For businesses, that runup is going to "squeeze their margins in the near-term," said Jason Seidl, managing director and transportation analyst at TD Cowen. "Larger companies tend not to be exposed to the spot market, and they'll have what we call dedicated deals in place."

But that doesn't help smaller companies, which are walking a delicate line of not wanting to alienate customers who have enjoyed free shipping for nearly a generation.

Todd Shelton, founder of the eponymous 23-year-old New Jersey clothing company that makes high-end men's jeans, T-shirts and button-up shirts locally, said he started charging customers for return shipping this week for the first time after absorbing the higher expenses this year. The shipping is deducted from customers' refunds.

Shelton said his costs have been rising for materials, too, such as his imported Japanese denim and shirt fabric from Portugal. The company is still profitable, but the higher costs have eaten into profit margins as they stay elevated, he's considering raising prices or charging for shipping to customers, he said.

Shields said she's considering raising her shipping fees for the first time in six years to keep up with the increases.

The holiday season is one of Shields' busiest. But she said she's seen a slowdown in sales because of shipping fees.

"I can't compete with Amazon on the free shipping. I can't do that. I can't afford that," she said. "I'm seeing so much of my percentage that's supposed to come back to me and my family being put towards the shipping costs."

With no sign of fuel prices declining significantly, Shields, who runs her business out of her spare bedroom at home, said she's not sure that small businesses will be able to emerge on the other side of the holidays.

Shippers including UPS, FedEx and USPS have all instituted holiday surcharges, with prices varying by package type and lasting through the end of the season. Though that's typical during the peak holiday period each year, those charges have continued to rise over the past few years.

But even as costs everywhere climb, consumers are still spending.

According to the National Retail Federation, retail sales through the end of the year are expected to grow 4.2%, translating to total spending of $1.01 trillion. That's compared with last year's holiday sales totaling $972 billion, which was 3.1% growth.

Some of that growth is from inflation, meaning consumers are simply paying more for the same goods.

"Customers all expect to spend more this holiday, but part of why they expect to spend more is they expect things to cost more, not because they'd like to spend more or they were hoping to get more quantity of stuff," said Aaron Cheris, the global retail practice head at Bain & Company. "So what I suspect happens is that non-discretionary categories do fine, because you buy the food you were going to need to buy anyway, but then the amount of cash that's left over for [discretionary] goods is less."

Still, Cheris said it's shaping up to be a record year for shipping costs, which is putting immense pressure on smaller retailers. That means many retailers may find themselves in financial distress in the first quarter of next year as they struggle to make it through the holiday season.

He added that larger retail companies will also have to reckon with rising costs, though they may not feel those effects until later quarters because they have shipping contracts that have already been locked in and have control over their own delivery fleets.

"If anything, it benefits the scale players over the little players, but it's kind of a little bit bad for everybody," Cheris said. "I think that is the challenge."

#589. Scientists May Have Discovered a Massive Starless Galaxy

Some galaxies appear to contain all the ingredients needed to make stars, yet have failed to produce almost any.

Scientists have puzzled over why for years. Recent findings suggest that these “dark galaxies”—that is, galaxies that contain gas and dark matter but few or no stars—may be lurking throughout the cosmos. Finding them could help astronomers understand why some galaxies form stars while others remain dark, and test the best models of galaxy formation.

Researchers’ most successful model of the cosmos, known as Lambda-CDM, assumes the existence of two invisible ingredients: dark energy, the mysterious property thought to be driving the accelerating expansion of the universe, as well as cold dark matter, a hypothetical substance that physicists think makes up about 85 percent of the universe’s matter.

Clumps of dark matter, called haloes, provide the gravitational scaffolding for galaxies to form and grow, according to the model. Gas collects inside these haloes, eventually cooling and collapsing to form stars. But astronomers are still trying to understand why this process appears to happen in some haloes but not others.

One possibility is that some contain vast quantities of hydrogen gas, yet form very few stars. That would make them extremely difficult to spot.

Now, researchers have found a promising candidate for such a dark galaxy and published their findings in the Monthly Notices of the Royal Astronomical Society. The object, known as AGC 322753, contains roughly 2 billion times the sun's mass in hydrogen, yet appears to have no stars, though the researchers note that it is too big to be totally starless. However, if confirmed, it would be an unusually massive example of a starless galaxy.

Researchers at Swinburne University of Technology in Australia made the discovery by comparing observations from two existing radio surveys, one conducted by the Arecibo Observatory in Puerto Rico and the other by China's Five-Hundred-Meter Aperture Spherical Radio Telescope. Both telescopes had picked up a massive cloud of hydrogen.

Although other researchers have identified several dark galaxy candidates over the past decade, this one stands out for another reason: It may be rotating.

Hydrogen gas emits radio waves at a signature wavelength, which shortens when the gas moves toward Earth and stretches when it moves away. The signal from AGC 322753 shows two distinct peaks, suggesting that one side of the gas cloud is moving towards us while the other is receding.

This could mean that the gas has settled into an organized, rotating structure, much like the discs in ordinary galaxies, despite apparently having formed no stars.

"The theoretical models and the simulations show us that dark galaxies shouldn't rotate, so this is extremely interesting," says Marco Monaci, an author of the study at Swinburne University of Technology.

If AGC 322753 really is a rotating disc with no stars, astronomers will have to explain how the gas organized into such a structure without producing them.

That isn’t the only puzzle. Most models of galaxy formation predict that a cloud holding this much hydrogen should have formed at least a few stars, as its densest patches collapse under their own gravity and ignite.

The paper’s coauthors suggest it may be very unlikely that AGC 322753 contains absolutely no stars. So, instead, they think it may contain stars too faint to see–an “almost dark” galaxy. If so, they must be very faint indeed, because even deep optical images from the DESI Legacy Imaging Surveys, which search for starlight rather than the hydrogen gas detected by radio telescopes, reveal none.

From this, the team estimates that AGC322753 contains at least 40 times more mass in hydrogen than in stars, raising questions about what exactly suppressed stars from forming.

But further observations, such as ones made by the Very Large Array, a network of radio antennas in New Mexico, will be needed to confirm whether AGC 322573 is truly a dark—or almost dark—galaxy.

"Finding out more about it really is an essential next step at the moment. It's intriguing, and I want to know more," says Robert Minchin at the National Radio Astronomy Observatory.

Minchin knows how misleading such signals can be. He was involved in the discovery of VIRGOHI21, a dark galaxy candidate found in the early 2000s that was later reinterpreted as a long stream of hydrogen gas pulled from a nearby galaxy by gravity, known as a tidal tail.

Gas torn away like this can keep some of its original motion and mimic the signature of rotation, so Minchin says more observations are needed to rule out the same explanation for AGC 322753. Yet there is no obvious nearby galaxy it could have come from.

Rhys Taylor, an astronomer at the Astronomical Institute of the Czech Academy of Sciences in Prague, says tidal debris is unlikely to explain the observations. Reproducing the candidate dark galaxy’s distinctive double-peaked profile through such a process “is technically possible, but it’s fantastically unlikely,” Taylor says.

Taylor and Minchin both flag another concern, though. The two radio surveys that the researchers used placed the hydrogen at noticeably different positions in the sky. That suggests AGC 322753 may be an extended cloud of gas rather than a compact galaxy, another possibility that follow-up observations could help untangle.

Whether AGC 322753 turns out to be completely dark or merely exceptionally faint, understanding objects like it could still help astronomers work out why some galaxies form stars while others apparently do not, says Elisabeth Adams at the Planetary Science Institute.

One possible reason is a galaxy that spins so fast that its gas stays too spread out to collapse. Another is that ultraviolet light from the universe’s earliest stars heated the gas, keeping it too warm to condense. Studying such almost-dark galaxies could help tell these phenomena apart, revealing how the conditions of the early universe shaped the galaxies we see today.

“Once it’s no longer a dark galaxy, people seem to stop caring, and I actually think that’s not the right approach,” says Adams.

But if AGC 322753 truly is starless, it would be one of the most massive dark galaxies ever identified, potentially helping explain where the missing galaxies predicted by Lambda-CDM have been hiding. It could even help astronomers understand why some gas-rich galaxies form stars while others don't, providing a valuable test for models of galaxy formation—and perhaps even offering new clues about the nature of dark matter itself.

#583. The Midterms Were a Pretty Good Sign for the Right-to-Repair Movement

Right-to-repair policies weren’t the headline issue in the midterms, but lawmakers across party lines ran on fixing the systems that lock buyers out.

Iowa senator-elect Josh Turek and presumptive Minnesota state representative Jake Johnson both centered repair policies in their platforms, focusing on issues that affect farmers and their equipment. (As of publication, Johnson’s race has not yet been called and may be headed to a recount.) In St. Paul, Minnesota, voters also passed a measure ensuring renters' right to repair with overwhelming support.

The right-to-repair movement, propelled by advocates in the US and internationally, pushes lawmakers for rules that prevent manufacturers from locking owners out of their own devices. Repair efforts tend to be bipartisan, with support ranging from consumers to farmers to wheelchair users.

“A political climate where people want policies that stand up for the little guy, help reduce unnecessary costs, and work in a bipartisan way—that’s a very good environment for right-to-repair,” says Nathan Proctor, senior director of PIRG’s right-to-repair campaign, in a text to WIRED.

Advancing right-to-repair legislation would be a step toward the larger ownership battles taking place in the US. People are angry that companies are turning everything into a subscription service or using software locks to restrict people from modifying or fixing their gear. That meshes with a long-simmering fury about the broader political climate, where tariffs, a memory shortage, and an extremely unpopular war with Iran have driven prices up on just about everything.

As Kevin O’Reilly, executive director of Fulu, an advocacy group focused on consumer digital ownership and product rights, points out, the efforts to claw back control over devices mimic the broader message voters sent at the ballot box.

“There’s a lot to be excited about,” O’Reilly tells WIRED. “People want things to be more affordable, politics to be less polarized, and Congress to solve problems that affect them.”

That discontent points straight to the root of the problem: Section 1201 of the Digital Millennium Copyright Act (DMCA). The 1998 federal statute made it illegal to circumvent digital locks, enabling companies to punish people who break them with possible prison time. Repair advocates have widely decried the legislation, as companies have used it to prevent people from fixing their devices without software access.

For advocates, the reshuffled Congress is the cleanest legislative target in decades. With voter frustration cutting cleanly across party lines, O'Reilly suggests the mandate is hard to ignore.

“If I were a new or returning member of Congress,” O’Reilly says, “I would see DMCA reform as a no-brainer.”

#578. New Hulu Doc ‘Get Huge’ Shows the Deadly Toll of Looksmaxxing

Before Clavicular, before Androgenic, before the Tate brothers, before bonesmashing and frame-mogging and the manosphere and looksmaxxing entered the online lexicon, there was Tony Huge.

A 44-year-old former model turned lawyer turned bodybuilder turned supplement impresario, Huge fled the United States for Thailand to escape legal pressure on his company from the Food and Drug Administration and to build out a “harem” of young, nubile Thai women. With his bulky frame, his sex-and-drugs-fueled lifestyle, and his motto “a day natty [natural] is a day wasted,” Huge became an icon for what was then the fledgling biohacking community. He amassed a huge following of disaffected, lonely young men, some of whom moved to Thailand to live with him. Then, some of them started dying.

Huge is one of the main focuses of Get Huge: Looksmaxxing to Death, a bone-chilling documentary series hitting Hulu on November 10. Directed by Stacey Lee and executive-produced by the journalist Jennings Brown, Get Huge kicks off with the untimely death of “Leo and Longevity,” aka Laith Abdallah Algaz, a biohacker and penis enlargement influencer who was found deceased in Huge’s home in January 2023.

What follows, however, is not a typical true-crime documentary. It’s a dizzying investigation into the hedonistic, self-destructive, and often deadly world of self-optimization, in which dudes inject themselves with pig’s brains and liquified gold, alliances shift at the drop of a hat, and you’re only as good as your last high-performing Instagram Reel. (A warning, spoilers for Get Huge follow.)

Since 2023, Huge has opened up his Pattaya, Thailand, home to the Get Huge team, offering “full transparency” into what Lee refers to as “this 24/7 performance of being a man.”

“It was, ‘Here’s my harem, here’s my other men that I’m leading, here’s my muscles, here’s my drugs,’” she says. But as her crew got further and further into the filmmaking process, it became clear that what was going on behind the scenes—not just the turmoil within Huge’s inner circle but also the hangers-on dropping like flies around him—was far more interesting.

“Therein lies the problem for the young kid watching at home who is watching this as a blueprint for their life. They can't discern what's real and what's not,” Lee says. “Even I was having trouble discerning what was real and what’s not.”

In some respects, Get Huge is about the rise of looksmaxxing, the wildly popular and dangerous online self-optimization movement popularized by manosphere influencers such as Clavicular and Androgenic. (The filmmakers say Clavicular participated in initial talks with the producers to participate in the documentary but ultimately declined; Androgenic plays a small but key role in the series.)

Tony Huge in the new docuseries, Get Huge.

But Jennings Brown, a journalist and podcast producer, started delving into Huge’s world in 2023, long before Clav became a household name. Brown and his producing partner, Brett Neitchin, were obsessed with the manosphere and how the internet has ushered in extreme and rigid views of masculinity. “I went to school in Kansas and was short and felt insecure about that and always thought someday I’d save up for leg extensions,” he said. “So I’ve always had a horrified fascination with this world.”

Lee came on board as director a year later, just as the manosphere was starting to become a major talking point with the 2024 election. “It was such an absurd story—I mean, a penis influencer dying in Thailand,” she says. “But what was really interesting beyond the subject matter was the deeper story—why are men going to Thailand, and why is this kind of masculinity becoming more and more prevalent in online spaces?”

In some respects, Get Huge is a relatively straightforward true-crime story. Algaz was found deceased in Huge’s home with his pants around his ankles in January 2023, and Thai police ultimately concluded he died of blunt force head trauma. (The case is still open.) Internet sleuths in the looksmaxxing community immediately started pointing fingers at Huge, who had also been linked to two other prominent looksmaxxers, Nick Weisser and Josh Brooks, who died under equally mysterious circumstances in 2018 and 2021, respectively.

But as the filmmakers delved deeper into Huge’s world, they realized that was not the appropriate tack. “True crime became a vehicle through which to actually explore and understand this world, the stakes of it, the responsibilities of it, what keeps men there after these tragedies happen,” Lee says. “We wanted to know not only what draws them in but what keeps them there.” (That was particularly true for Algaz, who had recently abandoned his wife and child in Los Angeles before coming to live with Huge in Thailand.)

In some ways, the question of what keeps Huge’s acolytes in Thailand is the driving force of the series, particularly after the bodybuilder and influencer Connor Murphy (who one of the men in Huge’s circle refers to as “the ultimate chad”) joins the inner circle. As the men resort to increasingly extreme looksmaxxing methods, with Hughes even undergoing a psychotic break as a result of his drug use, they remain entrenched in Huge’s circle until Huge essentially decides to cast them out.

Huge did not respond to WIRED’s request for comment on the documentary.

Though the men frequently cite the importance of male friendship in their lives, they are constantly bickering and enabling each other, filming themselves engaging in increasingly self-destructive behavior to increase their viewership. “They don’t take care of each other,” one of Hughes’ former acolytes, Elon Muscular, says in Get Huge. “They don’t uplift each other. They find ways to fucking fuck each others’ lives up with bad habits.”

There’s also, it must be said, an obvious strain of queerness throughout the series, with the men regularly taking copious amounts of drugs before having group sex with each other and a bevy of Thai sex workers, who Huge calls his “harem.”

“Bodyhacking and looksmaxxing have a very homoerotic side,” says Lee. “It’s a bunch of dudes oiled up, posing with each other in little Speedos, and many of the drugs that began circulating within the community came from gay culture. [And] I think there is a lot of closeted behavior and tendencies to be explored.”

Huge uses his “harem” to burnish his macho image, tattooing his name on one and paying for another’s breast implants. In one shocking scene, he shoots some members of the harem up with drugs to increase their sexual attraction to him. Huge is clear throughout that he is paying these women for their time, raising uncomfortable ethical questions for the audience and the filmmakers alike.

“As a woman, it was incredibly challenging to witness” the way Huge treats his girlfriends, says Lee. “I think he would rationalize what he's doing is giving these women the best versions of their lives. Same with the men—he finds them at rock bottom, and he thinks he can improve them with his chemical fixes.”

One of the most shocking moments of the series, however, happened after the crew had stopped shooting in Pattaya, when Murphy was found dead in a lake in his backyard in July 2026. Murphy had been engaging in increasingly erratic behavior, with Thai media outlets reporting that two syringes and unidentified pills were found in his vehicle after he died, and according to Get Huge, he had recently started shooting up liquid gold. Authorities attributed his cause of death to accidental drowning.

To the team behind Get Huge, however, Murphy was one more casualty associated with an increasingly dangerous movement, as well as yet another loss in Huge’s inner circle. “I think we’ve always had this feeling that this extreme form of masculinity they all believe in was a huge contributing factor” to the deaths, Lee says. In one eerie scene, Huge appears to foreshadow Murphy’s death, addressing critiques that he may be enabling his drug use by callously saying, “It’s not the end of the world if something bad happens. People also learn from that.”

Perhaps more haunting than Murphy’s death after the cameras stopped rolling, however, is that looksmaxxing has only become more popular since Get Huge started filming.

Since production wrapped four weeks before Murphy’s death, Clavicular’s public profile has only grown, with the 20-year-old influencer garnering headlines for his “Camp Looksmax” workshop and walking in a major New York Fashion Week show. The Hulu team was even hit with a cease-and-desist from Clavicular after Mitchell Jackson, Clavicular’s publicist, says they attempted to get footage from the Elena Velez fashion show without his consent, leading to him withdrawing from the project.

“Hulu needs Clavicular. Clavicular doesn’t need Hulu,” Jackson tells WIRED. “That’s why they’re using his name to promote their stupid documentary.” (Brown, who was present, refutes this, saying he was not there to film and was only attending the fashion show in a personal capacity.)

Even as Huge gets older and his audience share is dwarfed by those of the younger generation of looksmaxxers—“relevancy is everything in this world, and looksmaxxing is a young man’s game,” says Lee—the movement he has spawned continues to gain traction. The deaths of Murphy and Algaz have served as little deterrent.

“No one starts out wanting to become one of these extreme characters, and I think there’s something we can all relate to about just trying to be the best version of yourself that you can be,” says Lee. “[But] when Leo died, did anybody stop? When Connor died, did anyone stop? And I think that for me, the question is, when the goalposts keep shifting and the nihilism and the looksmaxxing and the living for today becomes more and more extreme, where does it end?”

We sexted with a Muse AI agent—and Meta says that’s totally fine

Big Story: Meet the DEA’s premier crypto detective

#576. Where's the growth? Legacy automakers face a tougher 2027 as costs rise, market tightens

DETROIT — There's a growing concern that the global automotive industry may be stuck in neutral, if not reverse, heading into 2027.

The auto industry — from the companies to their customers — has been surprisingly resilient this decade, but there are fears that rising costs, weaker vehicle pricing and a more challenging market could threaten profits and growth going forward.

Automakers also face pressure outside the U.S. from an onslaught of Chinese carmakers, a thinner new-product pipeline after retreating from some all-electric plans, and company- and region-specific challenges.

"With investor focus increasingly shifting to 2027, the setup appears challenging," Barclays analyst Dan Levy said in an investor note last month. "Rising inflation, continued China pressure, affordability concerns in the U.S., and a flat global production outlook suggest another year of limited earnings growth."

Several Wall Street analysts have said automakers will need continued pricing strength and additional cost savings to offset inflation, higher investment spending and a potentially weaker economy just to maintain their current levels.

"It's a little bit more optimistic in the U.S. and then very pessimistic in Europe," RBC Capital Markets analyst Tom Narayan told CNBC. "As a whole, there's some negativity on '27."

Automakers from the U.S. to Europe to Asia have been facing a growing list of headwinds.

For U.S.-based legacy automakers, Ford Motor said it expects next year's results to be in line with 2026, while General Motors grew more cautious about its ability to continue growing earnings as much as it had previously expected.

"We continue to believe that 2027 will be another strong year and better than 2026, assuming customer demand remains strong, and commodity, logistics and other recent cost pressures moderate," GM CEO Mary Barra told investors Oct. 20.

GM CFO Paul Jacobson said that while it's too early to officially forecast, the company is facing additional inflationary costs that could lead to another $2 billion headwind in 2027, like it expects this year. He specifically cited costs of commodity inflation, logistics and DRAM, or dynamic random-access memory chips as challenges.

Those chips are critical components in modern vehicles and are used for everything from temporary data storage and real-time processing from infotainment systems to safety-critical systems.

GM and others have been able to offset outside pressures through cost cuts, operational efficiencies and resilient consumer demand for higher-margin vehicles. However, there's a limit to what companies can do.

"There's only so much pricing you can push to consumers," Narayan said. "They're already at pretty high pricing."

European automakers including Volkswagen, Mercedes-Benz and BMW in recent weeks reduced their 2026 earnings forecasts in response to U.S. tariffs, the growth of Chinese automakers and rising costs.

Most recently, BMW this week said accelerated restructuring measures would reduce its automotive pretax earnings margin this year by 2.5 percentage points. The company said the restructuring should support "positive effects" into 2027 and a 3% to 5% pretax profit margin for 2028. That compares with a 5.3% automotive margin last year and 3.4% during the third quarter.

Chrysler parent Stellantis, which has large operations in Europe and the U.S., is facing its own challenges, as it's in the midst of a restructuring that aims for a low-single-digit adjusted operating margin this year. It has maintained a 2026 guidance that includes a mid-single-digit percentage increase in net revenue and a low-single-digit adjusted operating margin.

In Asia, South Korean automaker Hyundai Motor, which has experienced notable growth in recent years, also cut key metrics when it reported earnings late last month. The company lowered its 2026 wholesales target to 4 million vehicles, down from 4.16 million, and cut its operating-margin target to between 5.5% and 6.5%, down from a previous goal of 6.3% to 7.3%.

Toyota Motor is an outlier for its most recent quarter and outlook, as the world's largest automaker increased its full-year operating income forecast by 500 billion Japanese yen (about $3.2 billion) on Thursday.

Toyota's results were boosted by weakness in the Japanese yen as well as strong hybrid vehicle demand in the U.S. However, the increase in operating income was its first in six quarters.

Broadly, the changes to earnings and outlooks come as production for the global industry slows.

Consulting and insights firm GlobalData expects global light-vehicle production of about 90.96 million units in 2026, down 2.1% compared with last year. It forecast a production increase of less than 1% next year. That comes on the heels of strong annual growth earlier this decade, led by China.

Cox Automotive reported the average transaction price of a new U.S. vehicle in September was more than $50,500. That was just shy of the record of $50,614 in December 2025.

Lofty vehicle prices have led to record-high and record-long financing deals, as well as concerns that customers are getting priced out of the new-vehicle market.

The typical U.S. new-vehicle payment reached $776 a month in September, the highest since December 2023, according to Cox Automotive.

Cox Automotive chief economist Jeremy Robb, discussing the third quarter for the automotive industry, rated the overall economy, buyer ability and borrowing costs as cautionary "yellow lights."

But he did have some "green lights," such as good credit availability and employment conditions, which could give some relief for automakers and consumers, many of whom need vehicles for their everyday lives.

Robb said there's a "tug-of-war" between headwinds such as rising costs, interest rates and fuel prices and tailwinds such as growth in liquid assets and savings that has helped offset some of the additional costs.

"Consumers remain under financial pressure, but the vehicle demand continues to hold, showing more resiliency in the auto industry than we've seen in other parts of the economy," Robb said during a Sept. 24 call, noting consumer sentiment remains low.

#572. King Arthur Baking Sourdough Sidekick Review: Rising to the Occasion

Like many people, I enjoy baking my own bread—especially this time of year. I baked sourdough on and off for nearly a decade, even having a recipe tested for a James Beard-winning bread cookbook, but I’m not proud to admit it’s been over a year since I baked a proper sourdough loaf.

As a full-time working parent, maintaining a sourdough starter sometimes feels like bringing home another pet. (You know, the kind your child swears they will care for before abandoning all tasks after three days.) You can certainly keep your starter in the fridge for around a week between bakes, but reviving it may take a day or two of feedings, and let’s be real, Will you remember to do this every week? Even the pet snake my son begged for only needs feeding every other week.

It is for people like me that King Arthur Baking Company and FirstBuild, GE Appliances’ product development lab, created the Sourdough Sidekick, a gizmo that automatically feeds and maintains a sourdough starter for you. We wrote about the Sourdough Sidekick when it was first announced in 2025, and now, after trying it for three weeks, I can say that my sourdough-baking dry spell has finally come to an end, perhaps even permanently.

Sourdough Sidekick, pictured here with the optional Crock Grip Band ($15)

The Sidekick is a Mr. Coffee-sized appliance with a built-in water reservoir, flour hopper, and glass crock with a stirring-paddle lid. I deal with an awful lot of apps in my job, and the Sidekick uses GE’s generic SmartHQ for GE appliances, which isn’t specific to this device and seemed optional. I admit I couldn’t be bothered with an unnecessary app, so I didn’t download it.

King Arthur Baking the Sourdough Sidekick

Instead, I followed the directions in the included Quick Start Guide booklet, which indicated that I simply needed to fill the water reservoir and flour hopper, set the current day and time (and the day and time I wanted the starter to be ready—you can choose up to seven days ahead; I chose seven), add a little bit of existing starter, and press “Auto Mode.” Almost immediately, a torrent of flour released from the hopper and began mixing into my seed starter with the stirring paddle. (King Arthur recommends keeping a portion of your starter in the fridge in case things go wrong, and I second this suggestion.)

Oddly, no water was dispensed in this process, so post-mixing, my starter was now in the form of an under-hydrated ball. Over the next few days, it became a larger, drier ball until I finally decided to remove the crock to add a couple of tablespoons of water; as I did, I noticed the reservoir was still at its maximum fill line.

I decided I should probably download the app, where I noticed the Sidekick indicated it needed a firmware update. After the update, the Sidekick began dispensing water during mixing, but I realized I really should have followed the instructions in the main instruction booklet, which calls for using a scale to properly calibrate the amounts of water and flour the machine dispenses.

Theoretically, you should only have to do this once, which is good because it’s an irritatingly lengthy and fiddly process. However, my next attempt (remember when I mentioned saving some starter in the fridge?) went much better, properly dispensing 30 grams each of water and flour each day and initiating a stirring cycle every two hours. It starts with a loud beep, then the paddle begins a loud mechanical whir, and for the first few days this can be quite startling, especially if you’re in the same room when it happens.

You can bypass the every-two-hours stirring cycle via the onboard controls, but since the stirring’s purpose is to knock down the starter’s volume and reincorporate any unconsumed flour, I kept it on. (For what it’s worth, I did think the paddle, an update from the Sidekick’s first design, did a pretty good job of incorporating all of the flour and water each time it mixed.)

Note that, in part due to the regular mixing and the lack of discard (Auto Mode will prompt you to discard after a week, while Ratio and Custom modes, detailed below, will have discard reminders), your finished starter in the Sidekick may not look as traditionally puffy or bubbly as you may be used to. You can disable stirring, or you can go on scent—it should smell tangy and indisputably fermented. As the many online Sidekick adherents often say, “Trust the process.” Some online users recommended adding a few teaspoons of more nutritionally dense rye flour to the (presumably all-purpose) flour in the hopper, which is supposed to supercharge the starter’s growth, but I admit I didn’t notice much of a difference in appearance, smell, or performance between an all-purpose starter and a rye-boosted starter.

Boules made with end-of-week starter maintained via the Sourdough Sidekick

Other than enabling firmware updates, the app does little beyond displaying the machine’s current settings and sending a push notification when the starter is finished. You can’t adjust settings or control the machine from it, but the most recent firmware update supports power-loss recovery up to 12 hours, which I tested and found successful after my husband tripped a breaker one day. The display shows the ambient temperature, but unlike other starter-babysitting gadgets like the Goldie or Brod & Taylor’s Sourdough Home, the Sourdough Sidekick has no way to adjust the starter’s temperature, which could be an issue if your house or kitchen runs cold. It does, however, offer “temperature-aware” feedings in Auto Mode, adjusting amounts based on detected room temperature.

In addition to Auto Mode, there’s Ratio Mode (where you can indicate starter-flour-water feedings by ratio—1:1:1, 1:1:2, et al.) and Custom Mode, which is what I ended up using the most, as I like to switch between flour types for different kinds of bread—you can dictate the exact flour and water quantities, as well as timing of the feeding (once a day, or even twice a day). In a typical week, the crock was about two-thirds full by the end, with more than 400 grams of starter—enough for two boules and a sandwich loaf with some seed left over for the next batch—ready to use right out of the crock.

The resulting bread turned out no differently than it had with traditionally maintained starter. If you’re unable to bake at that time, you can just discard most of the starter, leaving some “seed” behind for the next batch, or keep it in the refrigerator for a few days before reviving it the traditional way. (The crock comes with a metal lid for this purpose.)

King Arthur Baking the Sourdough Sidekick

If you’re only baking one loaf, you’ll have excess, but you can use the discard in recipes or give it away. Just note that if you intend to make a starter with a different flour, like whole wheat or full rye, you will need to calibrate the machine again as you did at the beginning. All removable components are top-rack dishwasher safe.

The Sourdough Sidekick is one of the most compelling kitchen-tech gifts I’ve ever seen for someone who bakes a lot or is interested in sourdough baking but intimidated by the process of keeping a starter. (The Sidekick also includes directions for making your own starter right in the machine.) Is it a necessary device? Of course not—especially since it costs about $175 more than a spoon and a Mason jar. But I can tell you I wouldn’t have baked bread this year without it.

#569. The Ocarina of Time remake is setting Metacritic records left and right

Nintendo appears to have made the right call in remaking The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time for Switch 2, at least from a hearts-and-minds perspective.

The visually resplendent remake, which hit store shelves last week, has already set two records on Metacritic in just a few days. For starters, it has the highest aggregate score (96 out of 100) of any game to be released in 2026 so far. That was established when reviews went up a week ago, though, so it's not necessarily news at this point. However, as Forbes noted, Ocarina for Switch 2 has achieved another gaudy Metacritic milestone: It's now the highest-rated game by user scores in the site's history, with an all-time record score of 9.6 out of 10.

For reference, the original Ocarina of Time for Nintendo 64 is the highest-rated game by critics in the history of Metacritic, with a score of 99 out of 100, a record it's held for a long time.

More than 8,750 users have submitted scores for Ocarina on Metacritic, so that score isn't necessarily being affected by a small sample size. It should absolutely be noted and emphasized that Metacritic does not require a verification of purchase for a user to submit a review score, so it's theoretically possible that some people are adding numbers to the mix in either direction without having played the game.

The Ocarina remake has also only been in the general public's hands for a little more than four days at the time of publication, so these takes are coming in piping hot, and people could change their minds over time.

We also would be remiss not to mention that Grand Theft Auto VI launches on Nov. 19, so there's a chance (albeit a small one) that Ocarina doesn't sit atop the user review throne for very long.

#568. Jensen Huang Hired a PR Firm Known for Handling Sex Scandals Last Year

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang began working with a crisis communications firm known for handling public scandals a year before he admitted to having an extramarital affair with a woman suspected by US intelligence of being a Chinese spy. The firm, Heller, has handled Huang’s behind-the-scenes communications since at least October 2025, according to two sources familiar with the matter.

Risa Heller, the firm’s founder, has built a reputation as a fixer for powerful men facing allegations of sexual misconduct and other personal controversies, according to a 2023 New York magazine profile. Her past clients and people who have sought her advice include former congressman Anthony Weiner, chef Mario Batali, and writer Jeffrey Toobin. Heller also handles corporate communications and issues unrelated to personal controversies for its clients.

Heller’s public relations consultants were initially believed to be hired for matters related to Huang’s personal life, including his family’s philanthropic endeavors, according to people familiar with the arrangement. The firm frequently communicated with Huang and his daughter Madison, a product marketing director at Nvidia who has become an increasingly prominent figure within the company, sources tell WIRED. It’s not clear whether Heller’s early work with the Huang family involved Huang’s affair.

In 2024, Huang began appearing in public with a woman who goes by Alice Huang, a former executive at the Singapore-based chip buyer Megaspeed, according to The New York Times. Megaspeed was created when it split off from a Chinese gaming and cloud computing firm in 2023, a few months after the US government began restricting exports of advanced semiconductors to China, including those developed by Nvidia. A subsidiary of Megaspeed later became a major buyer of Nvidia chips.

In early October 2025, Jensen Huang’s business dealings with Megaspeed and his connection to Alice became public in a lengthy New York Times report, which alleged that the US Commerce Department was investigating whether the company was helping Chinese firms evade American export controls through its subsidiaries.

The report caused tensions to rise within Nvidia’s top ranks, and Jensen was rankled by the story, according to sources. By the end of that month, Nvidia’s internal communications teams had been instructed to include Heller’s firm on any press inquiries about Huang, whether personal or professional, according to people familiar with the matter.

When reached by email, a representative for Heller referred WIRED to Nvidia. John Rizzo, Nvidia’s head of public policy communications, declined to comment.

US intelligence officials have determined that Alice (who has no known relation to Jensen) is a spy working for the Chinese government, according to the Times. Nvidia initially said that Jensen’s interactions with Alice were part of routine business with clients. But on Sunday, Huang acknowledged that his relationship with her was romantic in nature. In an email to Nvidia staff, he said he owed them and his family an apology. “I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did. I am deeply sorry,” Huang wrote.

Nvidia’s board of directors announced it is opening an independent investigation into Jensen’s activities. It said it has no reason to believe that national security or confidential Nvidia information has been compromised.

Before founding her firm, Risa Heller worked for Jane Harman, then a Democratic congresswoman from California, and later served as Democratic senator Chuck Schumer’s New York City press secretary. Rizzo, Nvidia’s head of public policy communications, also previously worked for Schumer, serving as his deputy communications director.

Update 11/10/26 8:45am ET: This story has been updated to note that Nvidia declined to comment.

#566. When and how to watch The Odyssey at home: Streaming details and deals

After a record-breaking theatrical run over the summer, The Odyssey will finally be available to watch at home this month. You'll still have to wait a bit to stream it, but dedicated fans can purchase the film at digital on-demand retailers soon.

Raking in $626 million domestically and $1.8 billion globally, Christopher Nolan's adaptation of Homer's ancient Greek epic poem became his highest-grossing film of all time, as well as Universal Pictures'. Mashable Entertainment Reporter Belen Edwards called it "nothing short of masterful" in her review. Other critics and audiences alike have raved about it (it currently holds a 94 percent on Rotten Tomatoes). I can't say I'm surprised, though. With an all-star cast that features Matt Damon, Tom Holland, Anne Hathaway, Robert Pattinson, Lupita Nyong'o, Samantha Morton, Zendaya, John Lenguizamo, and Charlize Theron, it was bound to be a cinematic phenomenon.

The beloved adaptation is set to return to IMAX screens in December for those who want to experience it again in its full glory, but if you don't want to wait, you can catch it from your couch starting Nov. 17. Here are all the streaming details you need to know to watch The Odyssey.

The Odyssey will be available to watch at home this month. You can preorder at Amazon, Fandango, or Apple TV. If you can wait a little longer, it will also be streaming. See all the details below.

The Odyssey is set to make its digital debut on Nov. 17, 2026. This means you can buy the movie from on-demand retailers for $19.99 (or likely rent it for around $14.99).

Here are some quick links to preorder the film on digital. Note that these prices will likely drop a few weeks after its digital debut.

While no specific streaming date has officially been announced, we can make an educated guess that The Odyssey will make its streaming debut on Peacock in February. We'll update this story with the latest information once it's revealed.

Christopher Nolan’s last film, the Oscar-winning Oppenheimer, debuted on digital exactly four months after its theatrical release. The Odyssey has followed its exact same timeline so far. If it follows a similar streaming schedule as well, the director's latest epic will hit Peacock in early February 2027.

Peacock subscriptions start at $8.99, but the cheapest tier won't grant you access to theatrical releases. Instead, you'll need at least Peacock Premium to watch The Odyssey when it's released. A Premium subscription will run you $12.99 per month. Fortunately, there are a few different ways you can save some money on a subscription, which we've rounded up below.

The best way to save money on a streaming subscription on any given day is by opting for the annual plan instead of the monthly plan. Paying for a year of Peacock Premium or Premium Plus up front saves you up to $39.89 per year.

Peacock Premium (with ads) costs $12.99 monthly, which adds up to $155.88 for 12 months. If you pay for the annual plan, however, it'll only cost you $129.99 — a savings of $25.89. Likewise, the Premium Plus plan costs $19.99 per month, which adds up to $239.88. The annual plan, on the other hand, costs just $199.99 — a savings of $39.89. You'll essentially be getting two months of streaming for free by opting for an annual subscription.

Free is our favorite price. If you're an Xfinity customer and have yet to check if you get Peacock for free, I highly recommend heading to Xfinity.com for more details. Here's a brief breakdown:

Xfinity Internet customers who are Diamond or Platinum Xfinity Rewards members can get Peacock Premium for free by redeeming a reward for it. Sign in at xfinity.com/rewards and choose Peacock as a reward. Then, wait for your email (it may take a few hours) with instructions on activating the offer.

NOW TV customers can also receive Peacock Premium as part of their service.

New customers with Xfinity Internet and an X1 TV Box, Flex streaming TV Box, or a Xumo Stream Box from Xfinity can get Peacock Premium for free for six months.

Both students and young adults (ages 18 to 24) can slash $6 per month off a Peacock Premium subscription all year long. Verify your student status or age through SheerID and generate a unique promo code to knock the price down to just $6.99 instead of $12.99. You can renew the offer yearly if you still meet the criteria, but you may have to prove verify your student status or age each time.

First responders, medical professionals, teachers, and military personnel can all unlock Peacock Premium subscriptions (with ads) for a discounted rate of $7.99 per month by verifying professional status through SheerID. If you continue to meet the verification qualifications, then you can renew your subscription at a discounted rate each year. Prices are subject to change.

An Instacart+ membership ($99 per year) unlocks a free Peacock Premium subscription ($129.99 per year value) on top of free grocery delivery, lower fees, and credit back on eligible pickup orders. You'll even get a free two-week trial to kick things off. The cost of Instacart+ is already a better deal than Peacock Premium for the year.

Pro tip: Sign up for your trial of Instacart+ when The Odyssey debuts on Peacock so you can watch it for free. Just be sure to cancel before you're charged the full amount. Setting a calendar alert is highly recommended.

A Walmart+ membership is one of the best ways to score streaming services for free. A $98 Walmart+ subscription unlocks either Peacock Premium or Paramount+ Essential as a free perk — and you can switch every few months. Since Peacock Premium is a $129.99 value, the $98 Walmart+ membership is already a better deal than paying for Peacock on its own. With Walmart+, you'll also get free shipping with no order minimums, fuel discounts, a Scan & Go checkout tool, and early access to deals during Walmart's major shopping events. #563. Raanan Hershberg on Stand-Up, Beating the Algorithm & Finding Your Voice

On Mashable's Say More, hosts Kristy Puchko (Mashable's Entertainment Editor) and Mark Stetson (Senior Creative Producer) bring humor and their trusted insights to the biggest shows, films, digital trends, and cultural moments. From viral-worthy rants and passionate raves to smart recaps and first-look teasers, they cover what everyone is talking about. Celebrity guests join the conversation for real talk about their careers, upcoming projects, and what’s trending online.

Raanan Hershberg is a comic’s comic, known for sharp, long-form premises, ruthless self-awareness, and a distinct refusal to bend entirely to the internet’s whims. Over years grinding through the New York stand-up scene, making specials like Brave and Jokes from the Underground, and co-hosting Your Favorite Movie Sucks, he has built a reputation for comedy that digs into the messy, uncomfortable gray areas of modern life.

In this in-depth conversation, Hershberg sits down to discuss the development of his latest hour, Did You Like Me?, the modern reality of social media virality, and why protecting your artistic integrity matters more than ever.

In this episode, Hershberg takes a sobering look back at early 2000s "punching-down" material, the guilt of edgy comedy, and turning domestic arguments into sharper, honest material.

Hershberg then dives into the illusion of truth in stand-up, why audiences naturally suspend disbelief, how breaking the fourth wall creates bigger laughs, and the art of admitting when a joke is a lie.

Hershberg also addresses the dreaded algorithm trap: why chasing short viral clips compromises a comic’s voice, the line between smart promotion and writing for social media, and why virality often comes from people completely misunderstanding the joke.

Catch Raanan Hershberg performing his hour Did You Like Me? live at the 2026 New York Comedy Festival and watch his specials—including his award-winning short film Memory Room—available now on YouTube.

#562. The 9 Best TV Shows to Stream This Month (November 2026)

With temperatures dropping, the holidays approaching, and relatives preparing to invade the sanctity of our homes, November is the perfect time to find a little peace and quiet in front of the TV.

Fortunately, this month’s lineup of must-watch shows offers plenty of excuses to escape, including a dramatization of one of the biggest scandals in cryptocurrency history, a return to the dystopian world of Blade Runner, and a dark comedy about desperate parents who turn to surveillance technology to find their missing daughter.

There’s also a return to Middle-earth, a deadly game of international espionage, and a mysterious archaeological discovery that sets off a globe-trotting conspiracy. Plus, we're revisiting two groundbreaking television series that helped shape our fascination with government secrets, surveillance, and conspiracy theories.

As always, we’re not just chasing the biggest names or the shows everyone is already talking about (as much as we do enjoy The Gilded Age). Instead, these picks reflect the kinds of stories WIRED covers every day, from technology and science to politics and culture, and the unexpected ways they often intersect.

Whether you’re in the mood for a mind-bending sci-fi thriller, a twisty international conspiracy, or a classic worth rediscovering, these are the TV shows worth making time for this month.

Ben Whishaw (Q in Daniel Craig’s Bond movies) is one of the finest actors working today, even if his name isn’t quite as recognizable as it should be. Fortunately, Black Doves gives him plenty of opportunities to remind us why.

The deliciously twisty British spy thriller stars Keira Knightley as Helen Webb, a seemingly ordinary wife and mother who just happens to be secretly passing government intelligence to a shadowy organization known as the Black Doves. Whishaw plays Sam, her longtime friend and a professional assassin who gets pulled back into her dangerous double life. Then there’s the always-brilliant Sarah Lancashire (Happy Valley) as Helen’s formidable handler, Mrs. Reed.

Season 2, which arrived November 5, raises the stakes considerably as Helen’s husband prepares to become Britain’s prime minister, putting her at the very heart of government. It’s a wickedly entertaining mix of political intrigue, espionage, dark humor, and double-crosses, with a stellar cast that makes even the most murderous characters hard to resist.

If you thought things were looking bleak in Middle-earth at the end of Season 2, just wait. Amazon's spectacularly ambitious—and wildly expensive—prequel to J.R.R. Tolkien’s The Lord of the Rings returns for a third season, picking up five years after the fall of Eregion. Sauron (Charlie Vickers) has been busy building his armies and conquering much of Middle-earth, leaving the Elves, Dwarves, Men, and Wizards scrambling to stop him. Meanwhile, the Dark Lord is working on his most dangerous creation yet: the One Ring.

The new season promises plenty of political intrigue, epic battles, and the kind of jaw-dropping fantasy world-building that makes the series such a visual feast. Even if you know where Tolkien’s story ultimately leads, watching the pieces fall into place is half the fun. The first four episodes arrive November 11, with the remaining four rolling out over the following two weeks.

Remember when cryptocurrency was going to change the world? For a while, Sam Bankman-Fried seemed poised to make that happen. The young entrepreneur became a billionaire seemingly overnight as the founder of FTX, a cryptocurrency exchange that promised to revolutionize finance. Then it all came crashing down in spectacular fashion, exposing an $8 billion fraud that landed Bankman-Fried in prison for 25 years.

The Altruists dramatizes that astonishing rise and fall, with Anthony Boyle (Say Nothing) playing Bankman-Fried and Julia Garner (Ozark) portraying Caroline Ellison (no relation to Larry Ellison), his on-again, off-again girlfriend and the CEO of his trading firm, Alameda Research. Inspired by reporting from New York magazine, the eight-part limited series explores how two young idealists who embraced the philosophy of effective altruism—using wealth and resources to do the most good—became central figures in one of the biggest financial scandals in recent history. All eight episodes premiere November 19.

If Amy Poehler and Mike Schur taught us anything with Parks and Recreation, it’s that even the most mundane workplace can be a breeding ground for brilliant comedy. Their latest collaboration takes that idea to an archaeological dig in Greece.

Based on Kate Myers’ 2023 novel Excavations, the 10-episode comedy stars Poehler as Elise, an ambitious archaeologist who has spent years waiting for her boss (Hugh Laurie) to retire so she can take over the operation. When she and three fellow excavators (Geraldine Viswanathan, Antonia Thomas, and Fina Strazza) uncover a mysterious artifact that could rewrite history, they make the questionable decision to keep it secret. Naturally, things spiral out of control, drawing the women into an international conspiracy that threatens their careers—and possibly much more. With a cast that also includes What We Do in the Shadows’s Kayvan Novak (aka Nandor the Relentless), Dig delivers an entertaining mix of workplace comedy, historical mystery, and archaeological intrigue. All 10 episodes premiere November 23.

More than 40 years after Ridley Scott’s Blade Runner first introduced audiences to a dystopian Los Angeles, where synthetic humans are almost indistinguishable from the real thing, the franchise is still daring to ask science fiction’s most unsettling question. In this case: What makes us human?

Set 50 years after the events of Denis Villeneuve’s excellent Blade Runner 2049, this eight-part limited series stars Hunter Schafer (Euphoria) as Cora, a fugitive who assumes the identity of a Blade Runner, a special operative tasked with hunting down rogue Replicants. She’s forced to team up with Olwen (Michelle Yeoh), a Replicant whose days are numbered, to track down another fugitive whose secret could unravel the very foundations of their society. With artificial intelligence advancing at a dizzying pace in the real world, the franchise’s questions about consciousness, identity, and the increasingly blurry line between humans and machines feel more relevant than ever. All eight episodes arrive November 25.

How far would you go to find your missing child? For Dedo (Axel Stein) and Carlotta Klatt (Heike Makatsch), the answer is: very far. And well beyond the boundaries of what’s legal. When their teenage daughter, Wanda (Lea Drinda), disappears without a trace, the desperate German couple takes matters into their own hands, enlisting their tech-savvy son to help them secretly install surveillance equipment throughout their seemingly sleepy suburban neighborhood. What they discover is that practically everyone has something to hide.

The truth is still out there. More than three decades after The X-Files premiered, Chris Carter's groundbreaking sci-fi series remains one of television’s most addictive explorations of government conspiracies, alien encounters, and things that go bump in the night (or creepily squeeze through air vents). It also made pop culture icons of David Duchovny and Gillian Anderson as FBI agents Fox Mulder, a true believer in the paranormal, and Dana Scully, a scientist and skeptic whose job was ostensibly to debunk his theories. Anderson’s character also helped to inspire a whole generation of women in STEM, a phenomenon known as “The Scully Effect.”

The pair’s chemistry, and seemingly endless will-they-or-won’t-they relationship, helped sustain the show through 11 seasons (including a two-season revival beginning in 2016), two movies, and countless monsters of the week. With Ryan Coogler’s (Sinners, Black Panther) reboot, starring Danielle Deadwyler and Himesh Patel, recently receiving a series order at Hulu, now seems like the perfect time to revisit the original.

Twenty-five years ago this November, Kiefer Sutherland introduced the world to Jack Bauer, a counterterrorism agent who could save the country from almost certain destruction in a single day—but never seemed able to find five minutes to use the bathroom. Premiering just two months after 9/11, 24 became a defining television series of the post-9/11 era, with its real-time format, ticking digital clock, and seemingly endless cliff-hangers making it nearly impossible to watch just one episode.

Each season follows Bauer through a particularly hellish day as he races to thwart terrorist plots, uncover government conspiracies, and navigate an increasingly murky moral landscape. The Emmy-winning thriller also gave us one of television’s most recognizable phone ringtones (courtesy of the fictional Counter Terrorist Unit) and some truly bonkers subplots—including a memorable Season 2 encounter between Bauer’s daughter, Kim (Elisha Cuthbert), and a cougar. Yes, a cougar. Some of the show’s politics and attitudes toward torture haven’t aged particularly well, but its influence on modern action television is undeniable. All nine seasons are available to stream.

This acclaimed British prison drama anthology explores the lives of inmates and correctional officers, with each season tackling a different part of the criminal justice system. The first season starred Game of Thrones’ Sean Bean as a guilt-ridden prisoner and Adolescence’s Stephen Graham as a morally conflicted prison officer, while the second shifted to a women’s prison, with Thirteenth Doctor Jodie Whittaker and The Last of Us’s Bella Ramsey leading the cast.

The third installment stars Doctor Who icon David Tennant as a veteran officer at a juvenile detention facility, where a violent incident raises questions about accountability, rehabilitation, and the psychological toll of incarcerating teenagers. Siobhan Finneran (Downton Abbey) returns as a prison chaplain, the only character to appear in all three seasons.
