#493. Pure has zero built-in background checks — how to vet your matches

TL;DR: Pure has no built-in background-check tools and keeps profiles anonymous. Before meeting up in person, verify their photos in the app, look up their number, and run their name through local court dockets yourself.

Mainstream dating apps love to brag about safety. Tinder briefly tried offering background checks through Garbo a few years back, while apps like Bumble and Hinge push selfie verification and real first names to keep profiles honest.

As Jack Dawes outlined in Mashable's guide to anonymous dating, Pure exists to minimize your digital paper trail. You do not need an email, phone number, or full bio on display to swipe. Instead of permanent profiles, users post temporary "dating ads" that vanish after a set window. Conversations self-delete after 24 hours by default, and built-in protections actively block screenshots to keep private photos from leaking.

AdultFriendFinder — readers’ pick for casual connections

Tinder — top pick for fast hookups

Hinge — popular choice for regular meetups

Pure — best for anonymous hookups

OkCupid — best app for inclusivity

That setup makes it a haven for discreet hookups, but it leaves you with a massive safety blind spot: Pure has zero integrated background-checking tools and does not screen users. In fact, the app's legal terms plainly state that it does not run criminal background or identity checks on anyone who signs up.

If you are meeting a stranger from the internet for a casual hookup, you need to know who is walking through the door. Because Pure will not do the vetting for you, here's how to handle the detective work yourself before taking things offline.

Before digging into someone's legal history, you first need to confirm the person chatting with you actually looks like their photos. Catfishing and bot profiles are common across anonymous ad boards, but Pure gives you a few built-in tools to filter out fakes before you share personal contact info:

Look for the Photo Verification badge — Pure lets people take a quick live selfie to earn a verification checkmark, confirming they actually look like their ad photos and aren't using someone else's pictures.

Request a True Photo or hop on a video call — Pure includes a native "True Photo" feature that requests an instant, unfiltered snapshot taken directly through the app's camera. Even better, jump on Pure's built-in video chat. Pure notes in its own safety guidelines that a quick face-to-face video call acts as immediate "face control" — so if a match refuses a two-minute video chat before meeting, take that as an immediate red flag.

Protect yourself during calls — While Pure blocks in-app screenshots and alerts you if someone tries to take one, an external camera can still record a screen. Keep your clothes on during the initial video vibe check until you establish real trust.

If your match has an unverified profile or their photos look like professional studio shots, run them through an image search before getting invested.

Because Pure blocks in-app screenshots inside private chats and alerts the other user if you try to take one, grab a screenshot of their public ad photo from the main feed before opening a conversation, or snap a quick picture of your screen with a second phone or tablet.

Once you have the photo, run it through Google Lens, TinEye, or a facial search tool like FaceCheck.ID. Stolen images from Instagram models, adult content creators, or Reddit threads usually pop up within seconds.

If the photo belongs to an influencer living in another state, do not bother arguing. Tap the three dots inside Pure's chat or ad menu, hit "Report," select "Fake profile," and move on.

You cannot run a meaningful public records search without a real first and last name, an active phone number, or a confirmed social handle. Pure does not require users to link any personal details, so you will eventually have to exchange contact info if you plan to meet in person.

When you do move the conversation off-app, run a few quick digital checks on whatever info they give you:

Check for disposable numbers — Run their 10-digit phone number through a free lookup like NumLookup or Truecaller. This shows whether the number belongs to an actual wireless carrier (like Verizon, AT&T, or T-Mobile) or a disposable virtual VoIP service (like Google Voice, TextNow, or a burner app).

Use Zelle to confirm their legal name — If they give you a real phone number, you can often confirm their identity without spending a dime. Because Zelle connects directly to verified bank accounts, entering the recipient's phone number on Zelle's send screen will usually display the recipient's legal first name and last initial before you approve a payment. Venmo works too, but remember that users can change their Venmo display name to a nickname or fake handle.

Once you have a legal name and a general metro area, you do not need to pay third-party consumer data broker sites for basic safety checks. The most critical public safety records are maintained directly by government agencies and are completely free to search:

County court clerk records — In the U.S., criminal and civil court records are public information managed at the municipal and county levels. Search "[County Name] court records" or "[County Name] clerk of courts" for the area where your match lives. Most county court websites allow you to search public dockets for free, letting you spot active warrants, felony convictions, or domestic violence restraining orders.

The National Sex Offender Public Website (NSOPW.gov) — Run by the U.S. Department of Justice, NSOPW is a free, nationwide registry search tool. You can search by a prospective date's first and last name, city, or zip code to see whether they appear on any state, territorial, or tribal registry.

Be on high alert if a match asks you to prove yourself on an outside website.

A common hookup app scam involves a match claiming that their personal safety app, roommate, or house rules require you to register on an external link before meeting up. The link usually leads to an official-looking site asking for your name, phone number, and a $1 credit card authorization to run a quick check.

The site is a credit card phishing scheme. Pure never requires third-party registration to chat or meet, and legitimate safety services do not ask strangers for payment details to verify an identity. If a match sends you an external verification link, tap the three dots in Pure's chat menu, report the account for "Scam or fraud," and block them.

Pure's safety guidelines also warn against a common scam in which a match asks you to chip in for gas money, an Uber fare, or a taxi to come to you. If someone asks for cash transfers before you meet face-to-face, block them immediately.

When it is time to meet up in person, turn on Pure's built-in Safety Signal tool.

Safety Signal lets you share your date details with a trusted contact before heading out. When you turn it on, Pure generates a password-protected link you can text to a friend. Your friend does not need a Pure account — opening the link in any mobile browser shows your last-known location on a map, your match's profile photos, and an active timer.

Beyond using Safety Signal, keep the basics in mind: meet in a busy, public spot for the first drink, and never rely on your date for a ride. A little planning lets you have fun without taking unnecessary chances.

Discreet hookups are great, but a little healthy snooping ahead of time is the best way to make sure your date stays fun, safe, and completely on your terms.

With experience writing about everything from AI tools and software to smartphones and home gadgets, she enjoys breaking down product research into clear, useful recommendations. When she's not tracking deals, she's usually comparing products, digging through reviews, and figuring out what actually makes a purchase worth it.

#492. Honda to invest $3 billion in new vehicle assembly plant in Ohio

MARYSVILLE, Ohio — Honda Motor plans to invest $3 billion to build a new vehicle assembly plant in Ohio, the automaker announced Monday.

Honda said the new plant, which will be near its other facilities in Marysville, Ohio, is expected to begin production in 2029, have a capacity for 240,000 vehicles annually and create more than 2,000 jobs.

The facility is expected to be capable of producing traditional vehicles with internal combustion engines, all-electric vehicles and hybrid models, which have been growing in popularity in the U.S.

Honda declined to disclose what vehicles the company plans to produce at the new facility.

The plant is expected to increase Honda's light-duty vehicle capacity to 1.3 million units annually in the U.S. It currently has 12 manufacturing plants in United States, eight of which produce engines or light-duty vehicles for the auto industry.

"This is not simply an investment in another plant. It is an investment in the future of Honda manufacturing in America," Bob Nelson, executive vice president of American Honda Motor, said during the announcement at the company's U.S. heritage center in Ohio.

The auto industry has been trying to localize more production amid President Donald Trump's tariffs and inflated logistics costs that have cut into profits.

Nelson told CNBC after the event that tariffs are affecting the company's business but the new plant is more about Honda's ambitions to grow in the U.S.

"Tariffs are an impact to us, even with the high localization rate that we have," he said. "But the real impact is that we have customers out there wanting Honda product and we can't give it to them. That's the real area."

Nelson said Honda did not work with the Trump administration ahead of Monday's announcement.

Honda's plans for a new plant come roughly two weeks after Volkswagen-owned luxury brand Audi announced it would invest more than $4 billion in a new plant at a rally with President Donald Trump, which Audi CEO Gernot Dollner faced criticism for attending.

Honda's new plant is the company's first new mass production U.S. vehicle assembly plant since it opened a facility in Indiana in 2008. It also opened a $70 million performance vehicle facility in 2016 and a multibillion-dollar joint venture battery plant earlier this year, both in Ohio.

The announcement comes after the automaker in May indefinitely suspended an $11 billion investment plan to produce all-electric vehicles and batteries in Canada. It also canceled three planned EVs for the U.S. market.

American Honda Motor Co. executive Jennifer Thomas recently emphasized the Japanese automaker's commitment to the U.S. and need for additional capacity.

"In order to be competitive in this market, you're going to need to build products here," Thomas, senior vice president of corporate affairs, said Oct. 27 in Detroit. "We are focused on trying to maximize the existing capacity that we have, but we are at capacity, and so we're exploring all options."

Thomas also warned the automotive industry faces a "catastrophic" scenario if the U.S. and Canada cannot reach a new trade deal by the end of this year, when Trump has threatened to double tariffs on Canadian vehicles and parts from 25% to 50% on Jan. 1 if the two countries don't reach a deal.

Honda said it has built more than 33 million vehicles and more than 39 million engines in the U.S. since it started auto production in the U.S. in 1982.

The Japanese automaker currently has more than 30,000 employees in the U.S.

#484. Goldman CEO Solomon says AI financing remains strong despite market jitters

Goldman Sachs CEO David Solomon told CNBC on Monday that financing demand for artificial intelligence infrastructure remains strong despite recent market jitters as investors reassess the risks involving the massive build-out.

Solomon said that his firm has been "very active" in AI capital markets deals over recent weeks, a setup that he expects to continue.

"There's still very strong demand for investments in the AI infrastructure build and the technology around the growth of AI," Solomon told CNBC's Leslie Picker.

Changes in the political environment are among factors that will "create recalibrations and ups and downs, and we're seeing some of that," Solomon said. "But I still deeply believe in the long-term trajectory of the power of AI in our economy to drive productivity."

AI-related stocks, including chipmakers and neocloud providers, have tumbled in recent weeks as investors reassess whether the hundreds of billions of dollars being poured into AI infrastructure will generate good returns.

The selloff, which began before the U.S. midterm elections, intensified after Democrats scored sweeping victories on Tuesday, raising concerns that political opposition could slow the industry's expansion.

The shifting sentiment comes at a key moment for AI model lab Anthropic, which is preparing for a potential IPO that could become one of the biggest tests yet of public-market appetite for AI companies. Goldman is among the banks leading that IPO process.

"We'll see, obviously, if this IPO moves forward, how it all plays out," Solomon said, declining to comment in detail about the IPO. "I would say that the market environment is still pretty constructive."

Solomon also pushed back against political resistance to data center construction, arguing that the infrastructure is essential to the U.S. economy and that it can be built responsibly.

"We certainly don't want this infrastructure going to places outside the U.S., which is what will naturally happen because the demand for compute is there," Solomon said.

#482. The 8 best noise-cancelling headphones we use, love, and recommend

Whether you work from home, spend a lot of time on airplanes, or simply want to turn down the volume of the outside world, noise-cancelling headphones are a worthwhile investment. That said, with flagship headphones from major brands running in the $450 and up price range, they're also a major investment. And the best noise-cancelling earbuds? They can easily run you into the $250 and up range. Thankfully, there are plenty of options other than the major flagship headphones — as much as we do love the pairs from Sony, Bose, and the like.

With that in mind, our headphones and audio experts put the top models at every price point to the test.

These are the tech, tools, and products — from laptops to e-readers, from earbuds to robovacs, and more — that Mashable ranks best in class.

Even the best value headphones will feel lacking if they don't come equipped with halfway decent noise cancellation. Besides, there are simply too many headphones on the market (our testing pool gets bigger by the month) for you to pay hundreds only to get subpar ANC. So, which brand comes out on top? The best tech deal this week The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

For most people, we think the Bose QuietComfort Ultra Headphones (2nd Gen) offer the best overall value, with the ideal blend of comfort, sound quality, and ANC. However, if you're looking for the best active noise cancellation overall, we have to give it up to the Sony XM6 headphones. Fortunately, there are no bad choices here.

To help you wade through the top options from brands like Apple, Beats, Bose, Sony, and Sonos, Mashable editors and reviewers tested dozens of headphones and earbuds. From flagship models to budget buds, we picked out the best options at every price range.

Read our full review of the Bose QuietComfort Ultra headphones (2nd gen).

Anyone wanting the all-around best experience: For the best possible blend of active noise cancellation, sound quality, and comfort, you don't have to look any further than the newly released Bose QuietComfort Ultra Headphones (2nd gen). Though they're pricey — and though we narrowly give Sony the edge on ANC quality — their solid construction and excellent performance are sure to carry you through years of use.

The first-generation Bose QuietComfort Ultra headphones have been one of our favorite headphones since their release in 2023, so the second-generation headphones had big shoes to fill. While the upgrades aren't earth-shattering, Bose does fix the most glaring issues with the first-generation earbuds.

For the most part, the noise-cancelling and sound profile remain unchanged. There are minor improvements in both: the sound profile comes across slightly richer, the adaptive noise cancelling is slightly more effective, and the 10 levels of adjustable noise cancellation are easier to access through the companion app. While we do find the ANC on the Sony XM6 headphones to be slightly more effective, the adjustability of Bose's headphones can't be beat.

The design also remains similar aesthetically, with the main changes coming in the form of a shiny metal accent piece in the headband. They are also, somehow, a bit more comfortable than the first-gen Ultras, especially in the ear cup area. Like the first-gen headphones, the ear cups are still removable, making them easier to clean and replace years down the line.

In combination with the above features, the folding ear cup design of Ultras has long made them one of the best headphones for travel, but Bose made them even better this time around. The battery life is now 30 hours, up from 24, and the headphones support passive playback with the auxiliary cable, meaning they don't need to be powered on to play audio. The USB-C charging cable can also be used to play audio, though the headphones do need to be powered on for this feature to use.

Bose also improved the wear detection of these headphones and added a lay flat mode, which automatically disconnects the headphones from Bluetooth and puts them into a low power mode when laid down on a flat surface. Overall, these headphones feel even more premium than the previous generation, and at the same price point, the first gen was available for — $449.

Read our full review of the Sony WH-1000XM6 headphones.

Folks who need outside sound as close to gone as it gets: Looking for the headphones with the best active noise cancellation in the industry? This is it. We loved the Sony WH-1000XM4s, we loved the XM5s, and now we love the XM6s. They come with a higher price point of $450, so these are for anyone genuinely interested in premium-quality audio and ANC. If you already own a pair of XM5s, however, we wouldn't say these are different enough to justify an upgrade.

Between these headphones and the Bose QC Ultras, our top pick is a bit better at blocking out sound, but we're comparing the highest levels of ANC. And for what it's worth, the XM6s still do an excellent job with significantly blocking or dampening environmental sounds, whether you're near a busy street or working in an office space, thanks to their 12 microphones (which also help with call clarity).

That said, Sony headphones tend to shine the most in the sound category, and the XM6s are no exception. Whether we listened to classical music, classic rock, or electronic music, the XM6s brought a level of clarity, balance, and depth that few other headphones in this price range match. Out of the box, they sound great, but with a little fine-tuning on their equalizer, they sound excellent.

Like the XM5s, they come with the same long-lasting 30-hour battery life. Unlike the XM5s, the earcups fold up, in a return to form of the XM4 model. For anyone who regularly takes their headphones on the go, this genuinely makes a difference — the XM6 case is noticeably smaller than the XM5 case, both in height and depth. Sony also gave the power button a small upgrade with a circular design, making it easier to differentiate by feel alone from the other on-ear noise cancellation button.

Read our review of the Sony WH-1000XM4C headphones.

Value hunters who still want premium performance: The Sony WH-1000XM4C may look like the iconic pair of XM4 headphones released in 2020, but Sony gave them some key upgrades so they sound like a great pair of 2026 headphones. They're the choice for anyone who doesn't have $450 to shell out for headphones, but still wants headphones that feel flagship-quality, from their soundscape to the way they wear.

Mid-range headphones are only becoming more popular, and choosing our top pick was a toss-up between these Sonys and Bose's new second generation QuietComfort headphones. The former won out for a few reasons.

For starters, the value of these headphones is hard to beat. For $399, you're getting Sony-level sound with a 10-band equalizer found on the app (the Bose pair has a three-band equalizer). This level of customization isn't a given on flagship headphones, let alone mid-range ones. While the active noise cancellation won't quite match up to the flagship model, ANC tech has improved incrementally over the past six years. In other words, while even slight improvements would've made these an even stronger pair of headphones, the ANC on XM4Cs is certainly nothing to scoff at and will outperform newer, cheaper models.

Next, the design. It's not hard to see why these were such a fan favorite. The relatively low profile and compact foldable design make these easy to pop into a bag or wear around your neck, making them the ultimate headphones for taking on the go, especially for longer-haul travel. The 27-hour battery life, though a three-hour decrease from the first generation XM4s, still gives you plenty to go on (and beats Bose's headphones by three hours). Finally, it comes down to price point. Though I expect these two headphones to duke it out during sales, at full price, the Sony's are a full $60 cheaper, making them the better get for most folks.

Read our full review of the Soundcore Space 2.

Need to spend under $150 on headphones: The dream is the Bose QuietComforts, without spending the hundreds of dollars. No problem — Soundcore made the Space 2 headphones, which are also comfortable enough to wear for hours on end, and feature solid noise cancellation. Plus, they make for great travel companions. You'll make some concessions because they are a budget model, but the $130 price point helps soften the blow.

Great headphones don't need to be expensive headphones, and the Soundcore Space 2 are one of the best examples of exactly that. The noise cancellation does a solid job of blocking out people chatting around you, other music or audio playing, regular sounds of the neighborhood — in most situations, they'll have you covered. Sometimes higher pitches did come through more clearly, but that's fairly characteristic of most ANC. While we didn't get a chance to test this out ourselves, Soundcore claims these are especially great at blocking out the hum of an engine, making them a worthwhile travel companion.

Also helping on that front is that the ear cushioning and squeeze on these headphones make them comfortable enough to wear for hours on end without issue. While I wouldn't quite call them Bose-level (and why should they be, they're hundreds of dollars cheaper), they definitely punch above their price point. Folding ear cups also make them more compact when not in use.

Rounding out these headphones is their balanced sound and stellar battery life. The sound profile comes with plenty of options for customizations, thanks to the eight-band equalizer and spatial audio listening mode, though it also sounds great out of the box. The battery life far exceeds that of the Bose and Sony picks on this list — 50 hours with ANC compared to the other headphones' 30 hours. Sure, they'll show their price with some creaking occasionally when you move your head and less adept ANC than more expensive headphones, but ultimately, you get more than what you pay for.

Sony quality fans with a $100 headphone budget: Have you heard the bad news? The latest generation of noise-cancelling headphones now cost a minimum of $450. All we can say to that is, "Yikes." And as much as we love the new XM6 noise-cancelling headphones from Sony, we know a lot of people aren't looking to spend more than $100.

For a pair of headphones under $100, these Sony cans have shockingly good ANC and overall sound.

These headphones feature Sony's signature all-plastic design, which we don't love, but it feels much more appropriate in a pair of headphones in this price range. We recently re-reviewed these cans for our guide to the best Sony headphones, and overall, we were very impressed with the amount of value they offer for their price.

As our reviewer wrote at the time, "the ANC is fantastic." On top of that, "The WH-CH720 employs 30mm drivers and exclusive brand technologies to broaden sound performance. Expect a vibrant mix of impactful bass with striking mids and highs. Turning on DSEE raises fidelity on poor-quality recordings, which many luxury headphones can’t do. You can tweak the sound to your liking via the EQ, though the headphones sound great on their default setting."

Read our review of the Bowers & Wilkins Px8 S2 headphones.

Big spenders who want even better sound: Audiophiles who want to spare no expense in their search for the best-sounding, most comfortable headphones in the world. While the $799 price tag is painful, these headphones really do look, sound, and feel a cut above the competition.

Our tech editor Timothy Werth tested the new flagship noise-cancelling headphones from British audio brand Bowers & Wilkins. Upon their release, he wrote that they're the most beautiful pair of headphones he's ever tested, with sophisticated details like premium, full-grain leather and metal components around the band and ear cups. When you compare the design of these headphones to Sony's all-plastic XM6 headphones, they're clearly in an entirely different league.

But headphones have to offer more than good looks to justify a $799 price tag. And while that price will simply be out of reach for most people, these headphones really are phenomenal. Not only are they incredibly comfortable, but they also sound like a pair of $799 headphones, with high-fidelity audio that makes your favorite tracks and albums come alive. Their noise cancellation is also very impressive, though Sony still has the edge in the battle for the best ANC.

"Personally, I can't afford to pay $800 for headphones, no matter how good they are," writes Werth. "But I can tell you this — after testing the Px8 S2 headphones, switching back to my XM6 headphones is painful. Honestly, I don't know if I can ever go back."

Read our full review of the Sony WF-1000XM6 earbuds.

Over-ear noise cancellation without over-ear wear: Not everyone will want to shell out over $300 for earbuds, but for folks who budget for having the best of the best, the Sony WF-1000XM6 are what we recommend.

Their combination of excellent sound quality, noise cancellation, and design is unmatched by any other option currently available, making them the best overall earbuds we've tried.

If the asking price on a pair of earbuds is $330, it better have little to no faults — thankfully, that's exactly the case with the Sony WF-1000XM6 earbuds.

Going into our testing, we expected the XM6 earbuds to sound great — Sony products typically do. We didn't expect them to sound the best out of every Sony earbud and headphone we'd previously tested. Whether we listened to Stravinsky's "The Firebird" or a live performance from Daft Punk, these earbuds highlighted the richness, clarity, and layers of each track we played on them.

What surprised us even more than the excellent sound quality, however, was the active noise cancellation. Sony products generally have pretty good ANC, but not necessarily the best of the best. With the XM6 earbuds, they're making a strong play to change that reputation: the ANC felt at least on par with the Bose QC Ultra 2 earbuds, which had been the best we'd experienced on a pair of earbuds. Sony even shone over the Bose when we tested the ANC without streaming any audio: the slight hiss of the ANC at work is more detectable on the Boses than the Sonys.

Sony also upgraded the design of the case and earbuds between the XM5 buds and the XM6s. Though the case feels like more of an aesthetic change, the earbuds take on a more oval-like shape than the rather circular XM6s, helping them sit more flush and naturally in the shape of the ear. The result? One, the earbuds cause noticeably less fatigue after hours of wear. Two, the tighter seal helps provide more passive ANC.

The short of it? These earbuds aren't cheap — but we totally get why.

Read our full review of the Soundcore Sleep A30 Special earbuds.

Side sleepers, partners of snorers, or anyone who needs to stream audio to fall asleep: Ever find yourself wishing you could (comfortably) wear your noise-cancelling headphones to bed? With the Soundcore Sleep A30 Special earbuds, you can. The specially designed headphones aren't the most versatile, but when it comes to blocking out the sounds of noisy neighbors, snoring partners, pets, or an extra loud AC, the noise cancelling on these buds has you covered.

I used to wear Apple AirPods to sleep, so I could quiet my busy mind with a podcast and block out my neighbor's barking dogs. Of course, AirPods aren't designed for sleeping, and as a side sleeper, I often woke up in the middle of the night to rip the thing out of my ear.

Enter: sleep earbuds. While the growing category has a handful of truly great options, the Sleep A30 Specials are the only pair with active noise cancellation. Other pairs rely on white noise soundscapes and the power of their passive noise cancellation. For some, that might be enough, but if you know your space is more than a little noisy when you go to bed, that extra boost can be a legitimate help.

The battery life and settings on these bad boys are good enough to sustain wear that matches actual sleeping habits. You can set up the ANC or audio to turn off so it's not going all night (and using up extra battery unnecessarily). The charging case itself has about five nights' worth loaded in.

Granted, these aren't going to be your most versatile pair of noise-cancelling earbuds. However, if you've been looking for something specifically for sleep, you'll be hard pressed to find something better. And as an added bonus, they come preloaded with Calm app meditations and basic sleep tracking features.

Even cheap headphones can block some noise from the outside world. And you can always blast your music loud enough to drown out nearby conversations and street noise via passive noise cancellation. However, if you really want to turn the volume of the world down to the limit, then you need headphones with active noise cancellation.

Active noise-cancelling headphones electronically cover up the sounds coming from your surroundings. They use internal microphones to listen to the world around you, then invert the noise and send it to the speakers. The idea is that both the output and the input will cancel out, leaving you with near-silence. The best over-ear headphones also create a tight seal around your ears, providing an extra layer of passive sound blocking.

If you've tried a pair of noise-cancelling headphones before and haven't been impressed, don't be discouraged, as not all ANC performs equally. For instance, cheaper ANC headphones may have a more noticeable hissing sound that you'll hear when the noise cancellation is turned on, which can be distracting for some folks.

Other headphones may be great at blocking low tones, but more prone to letting in high-pitched tones. Typically, premium headphones will yield better results all around, but we have budget picks below that still do a solid job.

In the past, not many wireless earbuds featured active noise cancellation that was comparable to over-ear headphones, but newer iterations like the AirPods Pro and Bose QuietComfort Ultra earbuds hold their own in the ANC space. However, even these earbuds can't quite get the same seal (or fit as many mics) that headphones can, so if you're looking for maximum silence, stick with over-ear headphones.

Absolutely. While some people prefer to stay more in tune with their surroundings, virtually all ANC headphones come with a transparency mode that makes this possible. In recent years, we've seen huge improvements in the quality of ANC from top brands like Bose, Sony, and Apple. So, even though flagship noise cancellers are usually a minimum of $450, we think they justify the investment.

The quick answer is that pretty much any headphones, even the best of the best active noise-cancelling headphones, will let in some sound, but they can significantly dampen down sounds in your environment. With more constant hums of sound like folks talking or loud fans, good ANC headphones are more likely to provide better cancellation.

If you're looking for the most insulation from the outside world possible, we recommend giving your headphones an ANC boost by playing music or a video. That extra bit of audio will make it even harder to hear your surroundings.

Wearing noise-cancelling headphones for long stretches of time is safe, sound-wise. However, we do recommend being careful with what situations you use ANC — if you're walking around in a city, for instance, spatial awareness doesn't hurt. You'll also want to avoid blasting music all day to spare your eardrums from damage.

Likely, the bigger issue you'll run into is comfort. We recommend trying out a pair from the Bose QuietComfort line if you're particularly sensitive to the sensation of wearing headphones or earbuds for long stretches of time, but all the options we included are comfortable enough to wear for at least a few hours without disruption.

In our testing, we preferred headphones from Bose and Sony. While historically Bose often came out on top for providing the most comprehensive noise cancellation, Sony's latest releases have been closing the gap between the two brands, all the while offering a more versatile sound profile. That said, you can still find excellent ANC from other high-end brands like Bowers & Wilkins, or more budget-focused alternatives like Soundcore.

Every single pair of headphones we included in this guide was hands-on tested by a Mashable reporter or editor. You can learn about our in-depth process by taking a look at our standardized testing methodology we developed for headphones and earbuds.

We pulled these recommendations from our existing pool of headphones reviews and from hands-on testing performed specifically for this buying guide. A couple of options that didn't make our updated guide include the AirPods Max, which do have good ANC and sound quality but also a less impressive feature-to-price ratio.

For a condensed version of what exactly makes good noise-cancelling headphones, here's what we considered:

Though we always weigh this feature when testing headphones, we paid extra attention to it for the purposes of this guide. For the headphones tested specifically for this guide, we made sure to use them indoors and outdoors, to wear them while someone was trying to speak to us, while playing loud music externally, and while going about our usual workdays.

Though this category can be extra subjective due to different user preferences and head and ear sizes, we also look at how adjustable headphones are in terms of suiting different wearers, whether with earbud ear tips or headphone band adjustments. A good fit can also factor in improved noise cancelling in headphones, so we made sure to pay extra close attention to how these headphones wore.

While testing headphones, we listen to music across a variety of genres, as well as podcasts to tell how they perform in different audio settings. Where applicable, we test out different equalizer presets and play around with equalizer settings to see how they affect headphones' performance.

We considered all the above features combined in relation to the headphones' price points, and compared them against each other to determine which products are worth it. Where applicable, we noted where the value for one feature made sense and where it didn't or where headphones justified higher price points.

Bethany Allard is a Los Angeles-based shopping reporter at Mashable covering beauty tech, dating, sex and relationships, and headphones. That basically means she puts her hair through a lot, scrolls through a lot of dating apps, and rotates through a lot of different headphones. In addition to testing out and rounding up the best products, she also covers deals for Mashable, paying an especially obsessive amount of attention to Apple deals and prices. That knowledge comes in handy when she's covering shopping holidays like Prime Day and Black Friday, which she's now done for three years at Mashable.

#476. We tested dozens of earbuds: These 8 are the best for noise-cancelling

Over-ear headphones are everyone's usual go-to solution for blocking out the world. And listen, I get it. You couldn't pry my Sony WH-1000XM6s out of my cold, dead hands. But honestly, as much as I love them, carrying around a clunky pair of headphones on the go isn't always ideal. I want a pair of headphones to tuck in my pocket that will still block out noise. That's the promise of the best noise-cancelling earbuds.

These are the tech, tools, and products — from laptops to e-readers, from earbuds to robovacs, and more — that Mashable ranks best in class.

Every top brand has its own take on ANC earbuds, each with a unique style and a plethora of features. Since you can't possibly try all the latest earbuds, the Mashable team has taken on the task of reviewing the best ANC earbuds for you. The best tech deal this week The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

While there are plenty of audio brands that provide good sound quality and noise cancellation, the best of the best ANC earbuds can elevate your listening experience. Not only do they offer phenomenal ANC, but they also make music and podcasts sound better. Trust us — on the right pair of earbuds, Olivia Rodrigo's latest album will have you singing along even louder, somehow.

Earbuds usually aren't as comfortable as over-the-ear headphones. I know this well, as I've found many pairs of earbuds that just plain don't fit in my ear. Others are downright uncomfortable. Once again, Bose offers the most comfortable headphones and earbuds. However, Bose and Sony aren't the only brands out there. That's why we set out to test the best the market offers and see what makes a great pair of wireless earbuds.

Read our full review of the Sony WF-1000XM6 earbuds.

Anyone who needs earbuds to get the world to near quiet: Not everyone will want to shell out over $300 for earbuds, but for folks who budget for having the best of the best, the Sony WF-1000XM6 are what we recommend.

Their combination of excellent sound quality, noise cancellation, and design is unmatched by any other option currently available, making them the best overall earbuds we've tried.

If the asking price on a pair of earbuds is $330, it better have little to no faults — thankfully, that's exactly the case with the Sony WF-1000XM6 earbuds.

Going into our testing, we expected the XM6 earbuds to sound great — Sony products typically do. We didn't expect them to sound the best out of every Sony earbud and headphone we'd previously tested. Whether we listened to Stravinsky's "The Firebird" or a live performance from Daft Punk, these earbuds highlighted the richness, clarity, and layers of each track we played on them.

What surprised us even more than the excellent sound quality, however, was the active noise cancellation. Sony products generally have pretty good ANC, but not necessarily the best of the best. With the XM6 earbuds, they're making a strong play to change that reputation: the ANC felt at least on par with the Bose QC Ultra 2 earbuds, which had been the best we'd experienced on a pair of earbuds. Sony even shone over the Bose when we tested the ANC without streaming any audio: the slight hiss of the ANC at work is more detectable on the Boses than the Sonys.

Sony also upgraded the design of the case and earbuds between the XM5 buds and the XM6s. Though the case feels like more of an aesthetic change, the earbuds take on a more oval-like shape than the rather circular XM6s, helping them sit more flush and naturally in the shape of the ear. The result? One, the earbuds cause noticeably less fatigue after hours of wear. Two, the tighter seal helps provide more passive ANC.

The short of it? These earbuds aren't cheap — but we totally get why.

Read our full review of the Bose QuietComfort Ultra (2nd gen) earbuds.

If your earbuds always fall out, look no further: For earbuds that have noise cancellation that performs better than many over-ear headphones, you'll want to look no further than the second-generation QuietComfort Ultra earbuds. Though they're not drastically different from their predecessor (first-gen owners should skip this release), they provide some of the best ANC and all-day wear available on the market.

We were big fans of the first-generation QC Ultra earbuds, thanks to their rich sound profile, class-leading ANC, and comfortable fit. With the second-generation buds, Bose didn't exactly reinvent the wheel — but it did make some minor improvements that helped make an already standout pair of earbuds even better.

For starters, the QC Ultras come with a wireless charging case, which helps make the case for shelling out $299 for them. They also include Bose SpeechClarity tech, which we noticed helped voices sound noticeably clearer on calls than with the first-generation buds. The ANC also got a boost, somehow blocking even more than the first-generation buds. Though less exciting, they also added an earwax guard to the bud tips, which did help in the cleaning process.

While there were no dramatic improvements, we appreciated that Bose understood you don't want to mess with a good thing. In this case, that's the stability band/ear tip combo that makes wearing them snugly for long periods of time feel possible without any discomfort, a robust sound profile that can make all genres sing out of the box, and overall, a premium earbud that feels like it's worth its price tag.

Read our full review of the Panasonic Technics EAH-AZ100.

The sound-obsessed: Audiophiles who wants the best of the best sound quality should look to the Panasonic Technics AZ100 earbuds. They've flown under the radar since their December launch, but they have unmatched sound clarity. Multiple Mashable team members found that they stood out, even compared to flagship over-ear headphones.

There are earbuds sounds good enough, and there are earbuds that sound so good they make you understand what audiophiles are always going on about. These fall into the latter category. Simply put, if you're looking for the best earbuds for audiophiles, it doesn't get better than the AZ100.

Thanks to the earbuds' Magnetic Fluid Driver, they're capable of producing sound closer to the original recording than most consumer audio devices. In his review of the earbuds, Mashable's Timothy Beck Werth wrote that these earbuds let you appreciate all the layers of your favorite tracks, concluding, "[they] deliver richer, fuller, more detailed sound than any earbuds I've ever tested. The only buds I've tried that achieved similar clarity and sound separation were next-gen in-ear monitors that used ultrasound technology."

In addition to sounding incredible, these earbuds also have excellent (and adjustable!) active noise cancellation that stands up to other premium options. They support lossless audio via LDAC codec, multipoint connection with up to three devices, 10 hours of battery life per charge — and they come with a metal case and accent, lending them an overall more premium experience.

So why aren't they our top pick? Well, these earbuds can cause some soreness after a few hours wear, so they're not a great option if you like to wear earbuds for long stretches of time (like during a work day). They also rarely dip down from their $299 price tag, making them a less accessible option for most people.

Read our full review of the Bose QuietComfort earbuds.

Folks with a medium earbuds budget that want maximum returns: If you want good-quality sound but don’t want to spend over $200, the Bose QuietComfort is an affordable answer to the brand’s line-up. They’re focused on a great fit, as they not only come with multiple ear tips but multiple bands that can be changed out on the earbuds for a better fit on the crest of your ear.

The accompanying app lets you adjust audio levels, but you won’t need to, as these earbuds make all your music sound better. They’re reliable, have a long battery life, and are sure to please the pickiest of audiophiles.

I’ve sung the praises of the QuietComfort Ultra, but Bose’s latest earbuds, the entry-level QuietComfort, are also fantastic, especially with their under-$200 price tag. Even after adjusting the ear tips and bands, I didn’t find the fit on these quite as secure as the Ultras. However, my colleague Bethany Allard, who also tested the QuietComfort earbuds, said they were the best earbuds that have ever fit her.

The QuietComfort’s sound quality is just phenomenal. With these earbuds, listening to Ethel Cain’s A House in Nebraska immersed me in the orchestrations. When Brick House by The Commodores came on, the earbuds elevated the snappy drum beats so that I may have been listening live.

The noise cancellation on the QuietComfort earbuds isn’t as sophisticated as the Ultras; I found they had more sound leakage and that classic ANC buzz when I was in noisy environments. However, the ANC on these is still great, especially in quiet spaces.

One tiny beef I have with the Bose QuietComfort earbuds is that they require a different app than the standard Bose one. This is annoying if you’re an existing Bose user and need to download another app; plus, this earbud app just isn’t as good as Bose’s flagship app.

All in all, these are a solid pair of headphones that, for $179, are an unbeatable value.

Read our full review of the Apple AirPods Pro 3.

Apple fans who appreciate ease: If you're an iOS user who wants to avoid dealing with an auxiliary app, Apple AirPods Pro 3 are your best bet. They have impressive noise cancellation and seamlessly connect to iOS systems. To pair them to your iPhone, just open the charging case. Need to go hands-free? The AirPods will announce your texts to you.

iPhone users have long been fans of the AirPods Pro line, and for good reason. The earbuds offer excellent quality ANC and connect with Apple devices with record speed and consistency.

In the Pro 3 buds, Apple's upped the ante. In his review, Mashable contributor Adam Doud noted that the earbuds noise cancelling ability gave him a "personal cone of silence," even in the midst of a busy conference. Doud found the sound profile was flat, but balanced. Considering many earbuds these days can leave bass heavy, this is a huge plus in Apple's direction. To round out the basics, these earbuds also come with eight hours of battery life per charge, putting them on par with other earbuds leading in this category.

Apple didn't stop there, though. These earbuds can perform live translation across six different languages, making them an excellent travel companion. And for anyone who likes to track their workouts but not so much that they're ready to invest in a fitness tracker, the built in heart rate monitor is an added boon.

Read our full review of the Beats Powerbeats Pro 2.

Runners, cyclers, gym rats: Tired of your earbuds falling during exercise? Even more tired of blaring music at the gym killing your vibe? The Beats Powerbeats Pro 2 kill two birds with one stone, offering an exercise-forward design with an ear hook that can still be used as an in-ear earbud with ANC.

It can be hard enough to motivate yourself to exercise without a pair of earbuds adding friction to the process.

The Beats Powerbeats Pro 2 are designed to fit seamlessly into your workouts and elevate them. After testing them, we found the Mashable Choice Award winner was more than worthy. The ear hook/in-ear combo design is unique and can take some adjusting to get used to, but once you do, these buds aren't going anywhere. While providing top-notch ANC isn't the highest priority on these earbuds, the ANC you do get is workable, especially if you're (most likely) listening to music.

The thoughtfulness of the design extends beyond the ear hook. Tactile on-ear controls are perfect for not accidentally switching songs during reps, IPX4 water and dust resistance is great for keeping the buds safe from sweat, the expansive battery life means you aren't too likely to run out of battery mid-workout, and the built-in heart rate monitor is a nice fitness tracking bonus to an all-over well-rounded pair of earbuds.

Sure, these probably won't be your daily driver in every situation, but for workouts, these are sure to get the job done.

College students, teenagers, or anyone looking for cheap earbuds that don't suck: If you're on a tight budget but still want to enjoy noise cancellation, the Anker Soundcore Life P3i earbuds offer a surprisingly solid performance. Don't expect elite ANC, but these affordable earbuds don't require too much of a sacrifice in sound quality.

We're going to start with the major hitch of these earbuds: switching between devices sucks. They don't have multipoint pairing, and if you want to use them with your phone after using them with your laptop, you have to manually disconnect them (or, in our experience, sometimes outright unpair them) with your laptop first. It's easily their biggest flaw, and where we most obviously felt their price point during testing.

With that out of the way, these earbuds still have a lot going for them. For starters, they cost $50 and can often be found on sale for even less. Though inexpensive, these earbuds are comfortable to wear all day, have a custom equalizer in the app plus 21 presets (we're fans of the Treble Booster), customizable touch controls for each earbud, and a battery life that outdoes the Apple AirPods Pro. You won't have extras like spatial audio, and the ANC leans on background hiss more than premium models, but not terribly so.

For a pair of $40 earbuds, we're impressed. We recommend them for anyone who doesn't want to shell out $100 plus for a pair of noise-cancelling earbuds.

Read our full review of the Soundcore Sleep A30 Special earbuds.

Side sleepers, restless sleepers, or the sleepless: Whoever needs to hear this: there are more comfortable earbuds to sleep in than the ones you'd wear out and about during waking hours. Good sleep earbuds have much flatter profiles while still staying in your ear easily overnight. The feature set on the Soundcore Sleep A30 Special buds makes them the absolute best our team has tried, and our top pick for noise-cancelling sleep earbuds.

When it comes to sleep earbuds with noise cancellation, the pool is limited, as in the only real options are from Soundcore. That said, this wasn't a recommendation by process of elimination situation.

Soundcore makes legitimately great sleep earbuds, with comfortable interchangeable ear and wing tips, excellent battery life that lasts for several nights on end with the charging case, sleep tracking features, integrated meditations from Calm, a built-in alarm, and a host of soundscapes on the companion app. Touch controls work effectively for snoozing alarms or switching listening modes, as do sleep detection features that automatically switch your audio preferences as you drift off.

Considering competitors charge higher prices for similar feature sets (minus the noise cancellation), the Soundcore earbuds are also a great value, relatively speaking. They may be too much of an investment for some, but if you're persistently waking up due to noisy neighbors or a snoring partner at night, these earbuds are likely worth considering.

All noise-cancelling headphones, including earbuds, have some passive noise cancellation. While over-the-ear headphones naturally block out noise by wrapping around the ears, earbuds don't block sound as effectively. Instead of covering your ears, wireless earbuds deliver sound directly into the ear canal to drown out other noises. They rely more on active noise cancellation (ANC) to reduce environmental noise.

ANC is an electronic process within the headphones. Internal microphones listen to your surroundings and then invert the sound, effectively canceling it. Sometimes, with noise-cancelling headphones and earbuds, you'll hear a slight buzzing or humming in the background, and that's the ANC at work. The best ANC earbuds shouldn't have this buzzing, however.

We focus on wireless earbuds in this guide, because historically, wired noise-cancelling earbuds haven't really existed. Until recently, that is. In late September, Bose dropped its Noise Cancelling Wired Earbuds for $99 — we'll be evaluating them as a contender for a spot on this list, but Deputy Shopping Editor Miller Kern had positive initial thoughts on their performance.

Mashable shopping reporters, editors, and contributors regularly test new noise-cancelling earbuds and headphones from brands such as Apple, Beats, Bose, Dyson, JBL, Marshall, Sonos, and Sony.

Samantha Mangino, Bethany Allard, and other Mashable team members tested each pair of earbuds in this guide over several weeks. Similar to how we test the best noise-cancelling headphones, here's what we considered:

Though we always consider this feature when testing headphones, we paid extra attention for the purposes of this guide. We made sure to wear these earbuds indoors and outdoors, while someone was trying to speak to us, and went about our usual workdays.

Though this category can be extra subjective due to different user preferences and head and ear sizes, we evaluated comfort and ear fatigue for each model we tested. We also examined how adjustable headphones will suit different wearers, including whether they come with extra ear tips in varying sizes.

While testing the earbuds, we listened to music across a variety of genres with our standard Mashable headphone playlist, songs we're very familiar with, and podcasts to see how they perform in different audio settings.

With the above features in mind, we compared the earbuds' price points to determine which products are worth it. For premium headphones, we evaluated if they came with interesting extra features through their companion apps, or particularly smart designs. We also made sure every pair checked the basic boxes, like having decent battery life.

#471. Boeing to cut 5% of commercial airplane workers as production increase takes longer than expected

Boeing said Monday that it plans to cut 5% of its commercial airplane workforce as its production ramp-up is moving at a slower pace than the company expected.

The cuts will be through a combination of attrition and layoffs, Stephanie Pope, head of the commercial aircraft unit, said in a memo to staff.

Pope reiterated that the company is still working to stabilize production rates of its bestselling 737 Max planes, which are made outside of Seattle.

CEO Kelly Ortberg told investors last month that the company won't significantly increase production of the Max aircraft until the second half of next year. He said the company's wing production has been slower than expected.

"Adjusting our production schedules and reducing aircraft output puts us in an overstaffed position," Pope said in her note Monday. "Unfortunately, that means we need to align our staffing levels to our revised business plan, resulting in a reduction of approximately 5% through a combination of attrition and involuntary layoffs, with notifications beginning in December."

Boeing is targeting a production rate of 52 planes a month in the second half of 2027. Ortberg said the company has been trying to stabilize output at a rate of about 47 of its 737 Max planes a month.

#467. PepsiCo to cut about 1,000 jobs as North American business struggles

PepsiCo is cutting about 1,000 jobs across North America as the company tries to turn around lagging sales in its home market.

"We have a clear ambition for PepsiCo North America: to build a stronger, more future-fit business that delivers industry-leading growth," Ram Krishnan, CEO of PepsiCo North America, wrote in a Monday letter to employees obtained by CNBC. "To do that, we need to be faster, more connected, and more capable, while continuing to invest in growth — behind our people, capabilities and brands."

Over the next week, Pepsi plans to cut jobs as it says it aims to streamline its operations and better connect teams across the company's North American business. PepsiCo employs about 306,000 people worldwide, including roughly 125,000 in the United States, as of Dec. 27, according to a company filing.

The Wall Street Journal first reported the coming layoffs.

In October, PepsiCo cut its full-year earnings outlook, citing the struggles in its home market. During the third quarter, its North American beverage unit saw volume shrink 2%, while its North American food division reported flat volume.

While the company has tried to boost sales by cutting prices and investing in marketing, the turnaround is taking longer than expected, Chief Financial Officer Steve Schmitt said on the company's earnings conference call.

Shares of Pepsi rose slightly in afternoon trading. The stock is down nearly 1% this year, lagging behind the S&P 500's gains of nearly 11% over the same period.

#464. Children’s Place files for bankruptcy protection, seeks buyer

Children's clothing retailer The Children's Place has filed for chapter 11 bankruptcy protection, it said Sunday.

The company is looking to sell substantially all of its assets and is seeking offers from potential buyers by Dec. 9, with a goal to close a deal by the end of December.

The retailer, whose brands include The Children's Place and Gymboree, says it operates two digital storefronts and 514 North American stores, and has distribution in 13 countries through franchise and wholesale partners. The company's performance has taken a hit in recent years from tariff hikes and slower consumer spending, among other factors.

Its stores and e-commerce sites remain open for now, and the company is planning to continue its ongoing inventory sales with court approval.

"For nearly 60 years, The Children's Place has supported growing families with apparel that customers have come to know and love. We recognize the significance of this development for our employees, customers, vendors, and other partners, and we are committed to conducting an efficient and transparent sale process," said Rudy Morando, co-chief restructuring officer of The Children's Place. "In the meantime, we remain focused on serving the families who have trusted The Children's Place for generations."

This move comes days after interim Children's Place CEO Muhammad Asif Seemab and Chief Strategy Officer Muhammad Umair stepped down from their roles. Seemab and Umair will both remain on the company's board. CFO John Szczepanski took on the role of acting president and principal executive officer.

Seemab is also managing director of Saudi-based Mithaq Capital, which took a majority stake in The Children's Place in 2024 when the company was facing financial trouble.

Even with support from the investors, the company has not been able to significantly improve its financial outlook. In its bankruptcy filing, the company reported about $742 million in assets, compared to about $881 million in debts.

In the second fiscal quarter of 2026, its most recently reported quarter, the company's net sales dropped nearly 19% from the same period the year prior. At the time, Seemab said in a news release that during the quarter the company was "stabilizing our customer file" and "driving traffic to our stores and websites," and saw "lower conversion, combined with a very clearance-heavy mix."

He also pointed to tough comparisons to the prior-year period, due in part to back-to-school shopping demand that "was uniquely elevated ... ahead of anticipated tariff price increases."

The Children's Place stock has plummeted in recent years and was trading around 18 cents per share on Monday.
